# Notes on Motivic Periods

**Francis Brown**

## Abstract

The second part of a set of notes based on lectures given at the IHES in 2015 on Feynman amplitudes and motivic periods.

---
Notes on motivic periods

These notes started out as an appendix to [@Cosmic]. The aim is merely to provide some basic definitions and tools to describe a certain class of numbers and functions defined by integrals of algebraic forms over algebraic domains.

# Introduction

A period, according to an elementary definition of Kontsevich and Zagier, is a complex number whose real and imaginary parts are given by an integral of a rational function over a domain defined by polynomial inequalities [@KoZa].

An example is the number $$\pi = \int_{x^2+y^2\leq 1 } dx dy  \ .$$ The number $\pi$ is ubiquitous and clearly deserves a name of its own. In these notes, we seek to address the problem of how to describe general periods, and families of periods depending on parameters.

Following Grothendieck, periods can be viewed as the coefficients of a comparison isomorphism between two cohomology theories. For simplicity, consider a period $I=\int_{\sigma} \omega$, where $\sigma$ is a closed cycle representing an element in the Betti homology $H_n(X(\mathbb C);\mathbb Q)$ of a smooth affine algebraic variety $X$ over $\mathbb Q$, and $\omega$ is a regular differential form over $\mathbb Q$ representing an element in the algebraic de Rham cohomology $H^n_{dR}(X;\mathbb Q)$. The Grothendieck-de Rham comparison isomorphism: $$\mathrm{comp}: H^n_{dR}(X;\mathbb Q) \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathbb C\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow} H^n(X(\mathbb C);\mathbb Q)\otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathbb C$$ is induced by integration of algebraic differential forms over closed cycles. This data can be represented in a category $\mathcal{T}$ of triples $(V_B, V_{dR}, c)$ where $V_B$, $V_{dR}$ are finite-dimensional vector spaces over $\mathbb Q$, and $c:V_{dR}\otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathbb C\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow} V_{B} \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathbb C$ is an isomorphism. The period integral can be encoded by algebraic data: an object $( H^n(X(\mathbb C);\mathbb Q), H^n_{dR}(X;\mathbb Q),\mathrm{comp})$ in the category $\mathcal{T}$, together with a class $[\sigma]$, an element of the dual of the first vector space, and $[\omega]$, an element of the second. Define a space of periods $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{T} }$ of $\mathcal{T}$ to be the $\mathbb Q$-vector space spanned by symbols $$\label{introtriples}
((V_B, V_{dR}, c) , \sigma, \omega)\quad  \hbox{ where } \sigma\in V_B^{\vee}\ , \  \omega \in V_{dR}$$ modulo a certain equivalence relation (linearity in $\sigma$, $\omega$, and functoriality with respect to morphisms in $\mathcal{T}$), which reflects the fact that periods can have different integral representations. The space $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{T} }$ forms a ring, and is equipped with a homomorphism called the period map $$\mathrm{per}: \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{T} } \longrightarrow\mathbb C\ ,$$ which sends the class of $(\ref{introtriples})$ to $\sigma(c(\omega))$. In this way, we obtain an element $I^{\mathfrak{m} }$ in the ring $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{T} }$ whose period $\mathrm{per}(I^{\mathfrak{m} })=I$ is the integral we started off with.

The crucial point is that $\mathcal{T}$ is a Tannakian category, which automatically endows $\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{T} }^{\mathfrak{m} }$ with the action of a group[^1], whose action on elements such as $I^{\mathfrak{m} }$ is a prototype for a 'Galois group of periods'. In more classical language, $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{T} }$ is simply the affine ring of tensor isomorphisms from the de Rham to Betti fiber functors on $\mathcal{T}$. The idea of a 'Galois theory of periods' has its origins in Grothendieck's Tannakian philosophy of mixed motives, and has been developed by Nori, Kontsevich, André, and most recently Ayoub, Huber and Müller-Stach. The more common, and most sophisticated, approach to this subject involves replacing $\mathcal{T}$ with a suitable category of mixed motives. Several different approaches are possible. In this context, Grothendieck's period conjecture states that the period map is injective.

The naive category $\mathcal{T}$ defined above is the simplest possible framework in which one can set up a working Galois theory of periods. However, much is gained by adding just a little more; namely the requirement that $V_B, V_{dR}$ are equipped with filtrations forming a mixed Hodge structure. This leads to a category $\mathcal{H}$ of triples $(V_B, V_{dR}, c)$ carrying some extra data (Hodge and weight filtrations, and a real Frobenius involution, which encodes the action of complex conjugation). This category was first introduced by Deligne who proved that it is Tannakian. The upshot is that one obtains a 'ring of $\mathcal{H}$-periods' $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }$ defined entirely in terms of linear algebra which encapsulates many fundamental features of periods. A 'motivic' period, for us, is then an element of $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }$ that comes from the cohomology of an algebraic variety in a specific way. We shall use the adjective 'motivic' for such a period, although much of this paper is in fact Hodge-theoretic. The ring $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }$ has the universal property that the periods of any reasonable category of mixed motives admitting Betti and de Rham realisations will factor through it.

These notes explore some simple consequences of this general notion of $\mathcal{H}$-period, and explain how to compute using these objects. For this, one is obliged to use the language of Hopf algebras and matrix coefficients, since the fundamental objects are not the (motivic) Galois groups themselves but their affine rings. This elementary formalism already enables one to attach a panoply of invariants to an $\mathcal{H}$-period, such as its weight, rank, dimension, Hodge polynomial, and more elaborate notions such as its single-valued versions, unipotency filtration and Galois groups. A glossary of non-standard terms is given in §11. Much of this work is motivated by applications to physics, where some of these concepts (such as the notion of 'transcendental weight') have already taken root and have several applications, and we felt there was a need to place these notions in a rigorous context.

One key point, that must be mentioned from the outset, is that all the concepts in these notes translate immediately into a suitable Tannakian subcategory of mixed motives over the rationals $\mathcal{MM}_{\mathbb Q}$, whenever it is defined. Any reasonable candidate for such a category has Betti and de Rham realisations, so we obtain a map of rings of periods (rings $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\bullet}=\mathcal{O}(\mathrm{Isom}^{\otimes}_{ \bullet} (\omega_{dR}, \omega_B))$, with $\bullet = \mathcal{MM}(\mathbb Q), \mathcal{H}$) $$\label{introPemtoH}
\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{MM}_{\mathbb Q}} \longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }\ .$$ All the constructions in this paper can be pulled back to the ring on the left without any difficulty. Possible choices of categories include Nori's category of motives, or the abelian category of mixed Tate motives over number fields [@LevineTate]. If one wants to prove *independence* of periods, then it is enough to work in the elementary category $\mathcal{H}$, and defining invariants of $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }$ provides tools to do precisely that.[^2]

Another important point is that if the Hodge realisation $\mathcal{MM}_{\mathbb Q} \rightarrow \mathcal{H}$ is fully faithful, as one hopes, then $(\ref{introPemtoH})$ is injective and the action of the motivic Galois group of $\mathcal{MM}_{\mathbb Q}$ is already correctly calculated by the action of the 'elementary' Galois group of $\mathcal{H}$. This is the case for mixed Tate motives over number fields, so we can identify motivic periods in $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{MT}(
\mathbb Q)}$, for example, with their image in $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }$ with impunity. For these reasons, we choose to work unconditionally in $\mathcal{H}$, whilst waiting for the dust to settle on the final definition of $\mathcal{MM}_{\mathbb Q}$.

## Contents

In §2 we gather some properties of Tannakian categories, matrix coefficients and unipotent algebraic groups needed for the rest of the notes. This section, which is one of the most technical, can be referred to when needed. If the reader is only interested in periods over $\mathbb Q$, then §2.3 can be simplified by taking the rings $B_1,B_2,k$ to be equal to $\mathbb Q$. In §3 we attach some basic invariants to elements of $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }$. Section 4 defines further concepts including single-valued versions of de Rham $\mathcal{H}$-periods generalising the single-valued multiple zeta values of [@SVMP], and a certain projection map, which can be used, for example, to infer results about $p$-adic periods of mixed Tate motives from their complex periods. Section 5 offers some basic examples and can be read in parallel with the previous sections for illustration. In §6, we study a decomposition map which enables us to break up an arbitrary $\mathcal{H}$-period into elementary pieces. It takes the form of a canonical isomorphism $$\Phi: \mathrm{gr}^{C}_{\bullet }\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} } \overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow} \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} ^{ss}} \otimes_{\mathbb Q} T^c(H)$$ where $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} ^{ss}}$ is the ring of pure or semi-simple periods; $T^c$ is the tensor coalgebra (or shuffle algebra) graded by length of tensors; $H$ is a certain explicitly-defined vector space which is a direct sum of pure Hodge structures; and $C$ (for coradical) is a certain filtration on $\mathcal{H}$-periods by unipotency degree.

**Example 1**. The map $\Phi$ is a generalisation to all periods of the 'highest length' part of the map $\phi$ of [@MZVdecomp], which assigns to any motivic multiple zeta value an element of a shuffle algebra on certain symbols. For example, $$\Phi (\zeta^{ \mathfrak{m}}(2n+1)) = 1\otimes f_{2n+1}  \qquad \hbox{ for all } n\geq 1$$ where $f_{2n+1} \in H$ are certain elements which span a copy of $\mathbb Q(-2n-1)$. Let $\mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{m} }$ denote the motivic period corresponding to $2\pi i$. Since it is the period of a pure object, it satisfies $\Phi( \mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{m} }) = \mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{m} } \otimes 1$. From these ingredients, one can then decompose the $\mathcal{H}$-periods corresponding to multiple zeta values. For example, one finds $$\Phi(\zeta^{ \mathfrak{m}}(2,3)) = 3  (\mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{m} })^2 \otimes f_3    \qquad \hbox{ and } \qquad \Phi(\zeta^{ \mathfrak{m}}(3,2)) = -2 (\mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{m} })^2 \otimes  f_3 \ ,$$ As a further example, $\zeta^{ \mathfrak{m}}(3,5) \in C_2 \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }$ satisfies $\Phi(\zeta^{ \mathfrak{m}}(3,5)) = -5 \otimes (f_5 \otimes f_3)$.

The map $\Phi$ may provide a useful model with which to think about the structure of periods. In section 7, we very briefly describe how one might set up similar notions for period integrals depending on parameters. The notion of 'families of $\mathcal{H}$-periods' is very rich, and we barely do it justice. In section §9 we associate various notions of symbol associated to a family of $\mathcal{H}$-periods. For the symbol to exist, the family must underly a (globally) unipotent vector bundle with integrable connection, which always holds in the mixed Tate case. The symbol is a tensor product of differential $1$-forms modulo some relations. In the words of one of the referees, this should clarify many clumsy approaches in the recent physics literature. One can think of the decomposition map as a kind of analogue of a symbol for constant periods. We also define single-valued versions of families of $\mathcal{H}$-periods, again with applications to physics in mind. Finally, in §10 we provide some geometric examples and prove some technical results required for [@Cosmic]. Lastly, we discuss some examples in the case of the projective line minus three points for illustrative purposes.

Further background about periods can be found in the book project [@PeriodsBook], the surveys [@KoZa] and [@An4] and references therein. An obvious omission from this paper is a discussion of Tannakian ideas relating to limiting mixed Hodge structures and the regularisation of divergent (motivic) periods.

Since these notes are based on lectures, there is not much in the way of technical argumentation. In order to keep the length of the paper down, we provide essential technical arguments only where constructions are new or absent from the literature, and omit proofs which are straightforward or well-known.

# Generalities

## Recap on Tannakian categories

Let $k$ be a field. Following [@Tannaka] §1.2, a *tensor category* $\mathcal{T}$ over $k$ is a $k$-linear rigid abelian tensor category, which is ACU and satisfies $k \overset{\sim}{\rightarrow} \mathrm{End}(1)$ ([@Tannaka], §§2.1, 2.7, 2.8). A *fiber functor* from $\mathcal{T}$ to a scheme $S$ over $k$ is an exact $k$-linear functor from $\mathcal{T}$ to the category of quasi-coherent sheaves on $S$, which is compatible with the tensor product and the ACU constraints. A *Tannakian category* is a tensor category equipped with a fiber functor $\omega$ to a non-empty scheme $S$. If $S = \mathrm{Spec} \,(B)$ is affine, then due to rigidity, $\omega$ necessarily lands in the category of projective $B$-modules of finite type.

**Theorem 2**. *([@Saavedra], corrected in [@Tannaka]). Let $\mathcal{T}$ be a Tannakian category with a fiber functor to $S$, a non-empty scheme over $k$. Then the groupoid of tensor automorphisms $Aut^{\otimes}_{\mathcal{T} }(\omega)$ is faithfully flat on $S\times S$, and $\omega$ defines an equivalence of categories from $\mathcal{T}$ to the category of representations of $Aut^{\otimes}_{\mathcal{T} }(\omega)$.*

In the applications, $k=\mathbb Q$ and all Tannakian categories we consider will be neutralised by the Betti realisation. They will possess a fiber functor $\omega_B$ to the category of vector spaces over $\mathbb Q$, and a second fiber functor $\omega_{dR}$ to a smooth scheme $S$ over $\mathbb Q$. The space $S(\mathbb C)$ will be the domain for a family of periods.

## Matrix coefficients.

The following construction is paraphrased from [@Tannaka] §4.7. []{#sectMatrixcoeffs label="sectMatrixcoeffs"} Let $\mathcal{T}$ be a (small) category over $k$, and let $B_1, B_2$ be two $k$-algebras, not necessarily commutative. The following discussion is excessively general for our purposes, but this actually simplifies the presentation. Let $\omega_i$, for $i=1,2$, be a functor from $\mathcal{T}$ to the category of right projective $B_i$ modules of finite type.

**Definition 3**. A *matrix coefficient* in $\mathcal{T}$ is a triple $(M, f, v)$, where $M$ is an object of $\mathcal{T}$, $f\in \omega_1(M)^{\vee}$ and $v\in \omega_2(M)$. Consider the following $k$-vector space $$\mathcal{P}^{\omega_1, \omega_2}_{\mathcal{T} } = \langle (M,f,v) \rangle_{k} / \sim$$ spanned by symbols $(M,f,v)$ modulo the following relations:

$(i)$. (Bimodule structure). For all $\lambda_1,\lambda_2\in B_2$, and $\mu_1,\mu_2 \in B_1$, $$\begin{aligned}
(M,f,  v_1 \lambda_1+  v_2 \lambda_2) & \sim &   (M, f, v_1 )\lambda_1 + (M, f, v_2)   \lambda_2\nonumber \\
(M,\mu_1 f_1 + \mu_2 f_2, v) & \sim &  \mu_1 (M,  f_1 ,v ) + \mu_2 (M,  f_2,v)  \nonumber
\end{aligned}$$ Furthermore, if $\lambda \in k$, then $(M,f,v) \lambda \sim \lambda (M,f,v)$. Thus $\mathcal{P}^{\omega_1, \omega_2}_{\mathcal{T} }$ is a left $B_1$-module and right $B_2$-module, whose induced $k$-vector space structures coincide. We shall call such an object a $(B_1,B_2)$-bimodule over $k$.\
$(ii)$. (Morphisms). If $\phi: M_1 \rightarrow M_2$ is a morphism in $\mathcal{T}$ then $$(M_1, f_1, v_1) \sim (M_2, f_2, v_2) \nonumber$$ whenever $v_2 = \omega_2(\phi)(v_1)$ and $f_1 = (\omega_1(\phi))^t f_2$, where $t$ is the transpose.

Denote the equivalence class of $(M,f,v)$ by $[M,f, v]_{\mathcal{T} }^{\omega_1, \omega_2}$, or simply $[M,f, v]^{\omega_1, \omega_2}$ when there is no ambiguity about the ambient category.

The space $\mathcal{P}^{\omega_1, \omega_2}_{\mathcal{T} }$ is denoted by $L_k(\omega_1,\omega_2)$ in [@Tannaka]. We use the letter $\mathcal{P}$ because we shall eventually think of elements of $\mathcal{P}^{\omega_1, \omega_2}_{\mathcal{T} }$ as periods. Condition $(i)$ implies that for all objects $M$ in $\mathcal{T}$, there is a morphism $f\otimes v \mapsto [M,f,v]^{\omega_1,\omega_2}$ $$\label{omegaMuniversalmap}
\omega_1(M)^{\vee} \otimes_k \omega_2(M) \longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\omega_1, \omega_2}_{\mathcal{T} }$$ of $(B_1,B_2)$-bimodules over $k$, which is functorial in $M$. This is a universal property satisfied by $\mathcal{P}^{\omega_1,\omega_2}_{\mathcal{T} }$: any such collection of functorial maps from $\omega_1(M)^{\vee} \otimes_k \omega_2(M)$ for all $M$ into a $(B_1,B_2)$-bimodule over $k$ factors through $\mathcal{P}^{\omega_1,\omega_2}_{\mathcal{T} }$.

There is a natural $k$-linear map $k \rightarrow \omega_2(M) \otimes_{B_2} \omega_2(M)^{\vee}$, which sends $1$ to the element corresponding to the identity via the isomorphism $$\omega_2(M) \otimes_{B_2}  \omega_2(M)^{\vee} \overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow} \mathrm{Hom}_{B_2} ( \omega_2(M), \omega_2(M))\ .$$ Writing $\omega_1(M)^{\vee} \otimes_k \omega_2(M)  =  \omega_1(M)^{\vee}\otimes_k  k \otimes_k\omega_2(M)$ we deduce a map $$\omega_1(M)^{\vee} \otimes_k \omega_2(M)    \longrightarrow\omega_1(M)^{\vee} \otimes_k \omega_2(M) \otimes_{B_2} \omega_2(M)^{\vee} \otimes_k \omega_2(M)$$ which in turn induces a morphism of $(B_1,B_2)$-bimodules over $k$: $$\label{eqn: coaction}
\Delta : \mathcal{P}^{\omega_1, \omega_2}_{\mathcal{T} }    \longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\omega_1, \omega_2}_{\mathcal{T} }  \otimes_{B_2} \mathcal{P}^{\omega_2, \omega_2}_{\mathcal{T} }\ .$$ One verifies that it defines a right coaction of $\mathcal{P}^{\omega_2, \omega_2}_{\mathcal{T} }$ on $\mathcal{P}^{\omega_1, \omega_2}_{\mathcal{T} }$. Since $\omega_2(M)$ is projective of finite type, we can write $\omega_2(M)$ as a direct summand of $B_2^n$ for some $n$ and let $e_i$ (respectively $e_i^{\vee}$) for $1\leq i \leq n$ be coordinates of $B_2^n \rightarrow \omega_2(M)$ (respectively $\omega_2(M) \rightarrow B_2^n$). The element $\sum_{i=1}^n  e_i \otimes e_i^{\vee}$ represents the identity on $\omega_2(M)$. This gives the following formula for $(\ref{eqn: coaction})$ on the level of matrix coefficients: $$\Delta {[}M, f, v]^{\omega_1, \omega_2}  =  \sum_{i} [M, f, e_i]^{\omega_1, \omega_2} \otimes  [M, e^{\vee}_i, v]^{\omega_2, \omega_2}$$ In a similar way, the space $\mathcal{P}^{\omega_1,\omega_1}_{\mathcal{T} }$ naturally coacts on $\mathcal{P}^{\omega_1,\omega_2}_{\mathcal{T} }$ on the left.

## Tannakian case

Now suppose that $B_1,B_2$ are commutative, $\mathcal{T}$ is a tensor category and $\omega_i$ is a fiber functor to $\mathrm{Spec} \,(B_i)$, for $i=1,2$. The tensor structure on $\mathcal{T}$ implies that $\mathcal{P}^{\omega_1, \omega_2}_{\mathcal{T} }$ is a commutative $k$-algebra. In formulae: $$[M_1, f_1, v_1]^{\omega_1, \omega_2} \times [M_2, f_2, v_2]^{\omega_1, \omega_2}  = [M_1\otimes M_2, f_1\otimes f_2, v_1\otimes v_2]^{\omega_1, \omega_2} \ ,$$ which is well-defined as one easily checks.

Consider the affine scheme over $B=B_1\otimes_k B_2$ defined by $$\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{T} }^{\otimes}(\omega_2,\omega_1) = \mathrm{Spec} \,\, \mathcal{P}^{\omega_1,\omega_2}_{\mathcal{T} }\ .$$ If $R$ is any commutative $B$-algebra then its $R$-points are given ([@Tannaka], proposition 6.6) by collections of homomorphisms of $R$-modules $$\label{phiM}  {\phi}_M: R \otimes_{B} \big( B_1 \otimes_k \,\omega_2(M) \big) \longrightarrow\big( \omega_1(M) \otimes_{k} B_2 \big) \otimes_{B} R\ $$ which are functorial in $M$ and respect the tensor product. The corresponding homomorphism $\phi: \mathcal{P}^{\omega_1,\omega_2}_{\mathcal{T} } \rightarrow R$ is given on matrix coefficients by the formula $$\phi [M, f, v]^{\omega_1,\omega_2}  = f(\phi_M(  v)) \ .$$ It follows from the existence and properties of duals in $\mathcal{T}$ that the $\phi_M$'s are automatically isomorphisms and therefore $\mathrm{Isom}_{\mathcal{T} }^{\otimes}(\omega_2, \omega_1)  \overset{\sim}{\rightarrow} \mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{T} }^{\otimes}(\omega_2, \omega_1)$ is an isomorphism and we indeed have $\mathcal{P}^{\omega_1,\omega_2}_{\mathcal{T} } = \mathcal{O}( \mathrm{Isom}_{\mathcal{T} }^{\otimes}(\omega_2, \omega_1))$.

Applying the above in the case when both fiber functors are equal implies that for $i=1,2$, $\mathcal{P}^{\omega_i,\omega_i}_{\mathcal{T} }$ is a commutative bialgebra over $B_i$. It has an antipode, which on matrix coefficients is the involution $S: [M,f,v]^{\omega_i,\omega_i} \rightarrow [M^{\vee}, v,f]^{\omega_i, \omega_i}$, unit $[1,1,1]^{\omega_i,\omega_i}$ and counit $\varepsilon: [M,f,v]^{\omega_i,\omega_i} \mapsto f(v)$, and is a Hopf algebra with respect to these structures. It therefore defines an affine group scheme $\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{T} }^{\otimes}(\omega_i)$ over $B_i$ which we shall denote by $$G_{\mathcal{T} }^{\omega_i} = \mathrm{Spec}\, \mathcal{P}^{\omega_i, \omega_i}_{\mathcal{T} } \ .$$ If $R$ is a commutative $B_i$-algebra, then an $R$-valued point $g\in G_{\mathcal{T} }^{\omega_i}(R)$ can be viewed as a functorial collection of isomorphisms $$g_M:  R \otimes_{B_i} \omega_i(M) \overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow} \omega_i(M) \otimes_{B_i} R$$ for every object $M$ of $\mathcal{T}$, which are compatible with the tensor product. The homomorphism from $P^{\omega_i,\omega_i}_{\mathcal{T} }$ to $R$ is defined by the formula $g [M, f, v]^{\omega_i, \omega_i} = f ( g_M v)$, and every such homomorphism arises in this way.

We shall use the main theorem 2 in the following case:

**Theorem 4**. *The functor $\omega_i: \mathcal{T} \rightarrow \mathrm{Rep}\, (G_{\mathcal{T} }^{\omega_i})$ is an equivalence of categories.*

The scheme $\mathrm{Isom}^{\otimes}_{\mathcal{T} }(\omega_2, \omega_1 ) =  \mathrm{Spec}\, \mathcal{P}^{\omega_1, \omega_2}_{\mathcal{T} }$ is a $G_{\mathcal{T} }^{\omega_1}\times G_{\mathcal{T} }^{\omega_2}$ - bitorsor, with $G_{\mathcal{T} }^{\omega_1}$ acting on the left, $G_{\mathcal{T} }^{\omega_2}$ on the right. The action of $G_{\mathcal{T} }^{\omega_1}\times G_{\mathcal{T} }^{\omega_2}$ on $\mathcal{P}^{\omega_1, \omega_2}_{\mathcal{T} }$ is given as follows. The right coaction $(\ref{eqn: coaction})$ is equivalent to a left action of $G_{\mathcal{T} }^{\omega_2}$ $$\label{eqn: leftgroupaction}
G_{\mathcal{T} }^{\omega_2} \times \mathcal{P}^{\omega_1, \omega_2}_{\mathcal{T} } \longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\omega_1, \omega_2}_{\mathcal{T} }$$ via the formula $g(\xi) = (\mathrm{id} \otimes g)\Delta(\xi)$. We shall call $(\ref{eqn: leftgroupaction})$ the 'Galois action' on $\mathcal{P}^{\omega_1,\omega_2}_{\mathcal{T} }$. Concretely, if $R$ is a $B_2$-algebra, then a point $g\in G_{\mathcal{T} }^{\omega_2}(R)$ acts by $$g [ M, f, v]^{\omega_1, \omega_2} = [ M, f, g_M ( v)]^{\omega_1, \omega_2}\ .$$ There is correspondingly a right action on $\mathcal{P}^{\omega_1,\omega_2}_{\mathcal{T} }$ by $G^{\omega_1}_{\mathcal{T} }$ which acts on the element $f \in \omega_1(M)^{\vee}$ on the right. Depending on the situation, it can happen that one or other action of $G^{\omega_i}_{\mathcal{T} }$ on $\mathcal{P}^{\omega_1,\omega_2}_{\mathcal{T} }$ plays a more important role than the other.

## Minimal objects

Return to the situation of §(sectMatrixcoeffs). The following useful lemma will be used to attach quantities to motivic periods.

Suppose that $\mathcal{T}$ is an abelian category such that every object of $\mathcal{T}$ has finite length, and suppose that the functors $\omega_1,\omega_2$ are exact.

**Lemma 5**. *Let $\xi \in \mathcal{P}^{\omega_1,\omega_2}_{\mathcal{T} }$. There exists a smallest object $M(\xi)$ of $\mathcal{T}$, unique up to isomorphism, such that $\xi$ is a matrix coefficient of $M(\xi)$, i.e., every object of $\mathcal{T}$ of which $\xi$ is a matrix coefficient admits a subquotient isomorphic to $M(\xi)$.*

*Proof.* First observe that if $[M_1, f_1, v_1]$ and $[M_2, f_2, v_2]$ are two matrix coefficients in $\mathcal{T}$, then the relations imply that $$[M_1, f_1, v_1] + [M_2, f_2, v_2] = [ M_1 \oplus M_2, (f_1,f_2), (v_1,v_2)]\ .$$ Similarly, every linear combination of elements $\xi \in \mathcal{P}^{\omega_1,\omega_2}_{\mathcal{T} }$ can be represented by a single matrix coefficient $[M, f, v]$. In fact, $\mathcal{P}^{\omega_1,\omega_2}_{\mathcal{T} }$ can alternatively be defined as the set of equivalence classes $(M,f,v)$ with respect to relation $(ii)$ of §(sectMatrixcoeffs), equipped with a well-defined bimodule structure $(i)$.

Now let $M$ be an object of $\mathcal{T}$, and $f\in \omega_1(M)^{\vee}, v\in \omega_2(M)$. Define $M_v$ to be the smallest subobject of $M$ such that $v$ is in the image of $\omega_2(M_v)$. It exists, because if every subobject $N\subsetneq M$ satisfies $v\notin \mathrm{Im} \, \omega_2(N)$, then $M_v= M$, otherwise we can replace $M$ by any subobject $N$ such that $v\in \mathrm{Im}\, \omega_2(N)$, and proceed by induction on the length. It is unique up to isomorphism; if $v \in  \omega_2(N_1)$ and $v\in \omega_2(N_2)$, where $N_1,N_2$ are both minimal subobjects of $M$, then by writing $N_1 \cap N_2 = \ker (N \rightarrow N/N_1 \oplus N/N_2)$ and using the exactness of $\omega_2$, it follows that $v \in  \omega_2(N_1 \cap N_2) \cong \omega_2(N_1) \cap \omega_2(N_2)$ and hence by minimality $N_1 \cong N_1 \cap N_2 \cong N_2$. Similarly, let ${}_f M$ be the smallest quotient object of $M$ such that $f\in \omega_1({}_f M)^{\vee}$.

Now consider a morphism $\phi: M \rightarrow M'$. We first show that $$\phi(M_v)\cong \phi(M_v)_{\phi(v)} \cong M'_{\phi(v)}\ .$$ The second isomorphism holds since $\phi(M_v)$ is a subobject of $M'$. Note that $\omega_2(\phi(M_v))$ contains $\phi(v)$. We show that $\phi(M_v)$ is minimal for this property. For if $N\subset \phi(M_v)$ is a subobject such that $\phi(v) \in \omega_2(N)$, then $\phi^{-1}(N):= \ker (M_v \rightarrow \phi(M_v)/N)$ satisfies $v\in \omega_2(\phi^{-1}(N))$, and so by definition of $M_v$ we have $\phi^{-1}(N) \cong M_v$, and $N\cong  \phi(M_v)$. This proves the first isomorphism.

It follows that if $\phi: M \rightarrow M'$ is surjective, so too is $\phi: M_v \rightarrow M'_{\phi(v)}$. It follows from the definition that if $\phi$ is injective, $M_v \cong M'_{\phi(v)}$ is an isomorphism. The same statement holds for ${}_fM$ with the words injective and surjective interchanged.

Now apply the first remark to the surjective map $M_v \rightarrow \!\!\!\!\!\rightarrow {}_f(M_v)$. Denote the image of $v$ in $\omega_2({}_f(M_v))$ by $v$ also. We obtain a commutative diagram $$\begin{array}{ccc}
(M_v)_v  &  \rightarrow \!\!\!\!\!\rightarrow    & ({}_f(M_v))_v  \\
 \downarrow &   & \downarrow  \\
   M_v  &  \rightarrow \!\!\!\!\!\rightarrow   &  {}_f(M_v)
\end{array}$$ where the two vertical maps are injective. Since $(M_v)_v = M_v$, it follows that the vertical map on the right is an isomorphism. On the other hand, applying the second remark to $M_v \hookrightarrow M$, we obtain an injection ${}_f(M_v) \hookrightarrow {}_fM$, and hence an isomorphism $({}_f (M_v))_{v} \cong ({}_fM)_v$. We conclude that $${}_f (M_v) \cong ({}_fM)_v$$ and we shall subsequently denote this object simply by ${}_f M_v$.

We now show that the map which assigns to a matrix coefficient $(M,f,v)$ the isomorphism class of ${}_f M_v$ respects the equivalence relation $(ii)$. Consider an equivalence $[ M, f, v] = [N, f', v']$ arising from a morphism $\phi: M \rightarrow N$. In other words, $f'= \phi^t(f)$ and $v' = \phi (v)$. If $\phi$ is injective, $M_v \cong N_{v'}$ by definition and hence ${}_f M_v  \cong {}_f(M_v) \cong {}_f (N_{v'})   \cong {}_{f'} N_{v'}$. When $\phi$ is surjective, we again have ${}_f M_v \cong {}_{f'} N_{v'}$ by a similar argument. Since any morphism can be expressed as a composition of an injection and surjection, we deduce that the isomorphism class of $M(\xi):= {}_fM_v$ only depends on the equivalence class $\xi = [M,f,v].$ It follows from the definitions that the morphisms $M\rightarrow {}_fM \leftarrow {}_fM_v$ define an equivalence $\xi = [ {}_fM_v, f, v]$. ◻

Now suppose that $\mathcal{T}$ satisfies the more stringent conditions of §2.3, and is in particular Tannakian. This implies that every object has finite length.

Let $\xi \in \mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{T} }^{\omega_1, \omega_2}$. The $G_{\mathcal{T} }^{\omega_2}$ representation it generates is the $\omega_2$-image of an object of $\mathcal{T}$, by the Tannaka theorem. We show that it is isomorphic to $M(\xi)$.

**Corollary 6**. *Consider $\xi \in \mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{T} }^{\omega_1, \omega_2}$. Let $\langle G_{\mathcal{T} }^{\omega_2} \xi \rangle_{B_2}$ (respectively $\langle \xi\, G_{\mathcal{T} }^{\omega_1}\rangle_{B_1}$) denote the representation of $G_{\mathcal{T} }^{\omega_2}$ (resp. $G_{\mathcal{T} }^{\omega_1}$) it generates. Then $$\omega_2 (M(\xi)) \cong \langle G_{\mathcal{T} }^{\omega_2} \xi \rangle_{B_2}  \qquad \hbox{ and }  \qquad\omega_1(M(\xi)) \cong \langle \xi G_{\mathcal{T} }^{\omega_1}\rangle_{B_1} \ .$$*

*Proof.* The morphisms $(\ref{omegaMuniversalmap})$ induce functorial morphisms $$\omega_1(M)^{\vee} \otimes \omega_2(M) \longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\omega_1,\omega_2}_{\mathcal{T} }$$ in the (ind)-category of left $G^{\omega_2}_{\mathcal{T} }$-representations. By the Tannaka theorem, there exists an ind-object $\mathcal{P}^{\omega_1,\bullet}_{\mathcal{T} }$ of $\mathcal{T}$ whose $\omega_2$-image is $\mathcal{P}^{\omega_1,\omega_2}_{\mathcal{T} }$, and the above morphisms are the $\omega_2$-image of a family of functorial morphisms $$\omega_1(M)^{\vee} \otimes M \longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\omega_1,\bullet}_{\mathcal{T} }\ $$ of ind-objects of $\mathcal{T}$. Apply $\omega_1$ to obtain a family of functorial morphisms $$\omega_1(M)^{\vee} \otimes \omega_1(M) \longrightarrow\omega_1(\mathcal{P}^{\omega_1,\bullet}_{\mathcal{T} })\ ,$$ and by the universal property we deduce that $\omega_1(\mathcal{P}^{\omega_1,\bullet}_{\mathcal{T} }) \cong \mathcal{P}^{\omega_1,\omega_1}_{\mathcal{T} }$.

Now suppose that $\xi = [M, f, v]^{\omega_1,\omega_2}$. By the previous lemma, we can assume that $M =  {}_fM_v$. Consider the morphism $$\label{inproofetapage8}
 \eta \mapsto [ M, f,  \eta ]^{\omega_1,\omega_2}  \quad : \quad  \omega_2(M) \longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\omega_1, \omega_2 }_{\mathcal{T} }\ .$$ It respects the action of $G^{\omega_2}_{\mathcal{T} }$ on both sides. Therefore by the Tannaka theorem it is the $\omega_2$-image of a morphism $$e_f: M \longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\omega_1, \bullet }_{\mathcal{T} }$$ of ind-objects of $\mathcal{T}$. Its $\omega_1$-image is $$p  \mapsto  [ M, f,  p ]^{\omega_1,\omega_1}
\quad : \quad  \omega_1(M)  \overset{\omega_1(e_f)}{\longrightarrow}   \mathcal{P}^{\omega_1, \omega_1 }_{\mathcal{T} } \ .$$ Under the dual map, the counit $\varepsilon: \mathcal{P}^{\omega_1, \omega_1}_{\mathcal{T} } \rightarrow k$ maps to $f \in \omega_1(M)^{\vee}$. Incidentally, this shows that $e_f$ defines an equivalence $\xi = [\mathcal{P}^{\omega_1, \bullet}_{\mathcal{T} }, \varepsilon, \xi]^{\omega_1, \omega_2}$.

Let $N \subset \mathcal{P}^{\omega_1, \bullet }_{\mathcal{T} }$ be the sub object such that $\omega_2(N)\subset \mathcal{P}^{\omega_1, \omega_2}_{\mathcal{T} }$ is the $G_{\mathcal{T} }^{\omega_2}$-representation generated by $\xi$. We obtain a commutative diagram $$\begin{array}{ccc}
 \omega_2(M)   &  \overset{(\ref{inproofetapage8})}{\longrightarrow}   &   \mathcal{P}^{\omega_1,\omega_2}_{\mathcal{T} } \\
  \cup  &   &  \cup  \\
 \langle G^{\omega_2}_{\mathcal{T} } v \rangle   &   \longrightarrow&    \omega_2(N)
\end{array}$$ By assumption, $M= M_v$ and hence the vertical inclusion on the left is an equality. The map along the bottom is surjective since $\omega_2(N)$ contains $\xi$, the image of $v$ under $(\ref{inproofetapage8})$. Therefore $\omega_2(N)$ is isomorphic to the image of $\omega_2(M)$ in $\mathcal{P}^{\omega_1,\omega_2}_{\mathcal{T} }$, and the map $e_f$ factors as $$e_f: M \longrightarrow\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\! \longrightarrow N \subset \mathcal{P}^{\omega_1, \bullet}_{\mathcal{T} } .$$ On the other hand, the image of the counit $\varepsilon \in \mathcal{P}^{\omega_1, \omega_1}_{\mathcal{T} }$ in $\omega_1(N)$ maps to $f\in \omega_1(M)^{\vee}$. Since $M= {}_fM$, it has no non-trivial such quotients, and $M\cong N$. This proves that $\omega_2(M(\xi)) = \langle G_{\mathcal{T} }^{\omega_2} \xi \rangle_{B_2}$. The other statement is similar. ◻

## Coradical filtration and decomposition

Let $U$ be a pro-unipotent algebraic group over a field $k$ of characteristic $0$, and let $M$ be a left $U$-module over $k$, i.e., $M$ is a right $\mathcal{O}(U)$-comodule. Denote the coaction by $\Delta: M \rightarrow M \otimes_k \mathcal{O}(U)$, and let $\mathcal{O}(U)_+$ be the kernel of the augmentation $\varepsilon: \mathcal{O}(U) \rightarrow k$.

Define a filtration $C_i M$ on $M$ by $C_{-1}M=0$, and $$C_i M = \{x \in M  \ :\  \Delta(x) = x\otimes 1 \pmod{C_{i-1}M}\}$$ Equivalently, $C_iM$ is the fastest increasing filtration on $M$ such that $C_{-1}M =0$ and such that $U$ acts trivially on $\mathrm{gr}^C M$. In particular, $C_0 M = M^U$. This filtration is functorial with respect to morphisms of $U$-modules. It exhausts $M$, i.e., $M = \bigcup_{i\geq 0} C_i M$. This follows from Engel's theorem in the case when $M$ is of finite type over $k$, for then the action of $U$ on $M$ factors through a unipotent algebraic matrix group, and $M$ has a non-trivial fixed vector $v\in C_0M$. Replacing $M$ with $M/C_0M$ and by induction on the dimension of $M$, we deduce that $M=C_nM$ for some $n$. The general case follows from the fact that $M$ is the inductive limit of its sub $\mathcal{O}(U)$-comodules of finite type.

Now consider $\mathcal{O}(U)$, viewed as a right $\mathcal{O}(U)$-comodule in the natural way, and denote by $\Delta : \mathcal{O}(U) \rightarrow \mathcal{O}(U) \otimes_k \mathcal{O}(U)$ the coproduct dual to the multiplication in $U$. The above construction defines an increasing filtration $C_i \mathcal{O}(U)$. It satisfies $C_{0} \mathcal{O}(U) = k$, because $\mathrm{id} = (\varepsilon\otimes \mathrm{id} ) \Delta$ in any Hopf algebra and hence $x \in C_0 \mathcal{O}(U)$ satisfies $x = \varepsilon(x)$ and is constant, via $\mathcal{O}(U) = \mathcal{O}(U)_+ \oplus k$.

Now let us denote by $\Delta^r=\Delta - \mathrm{id} \otimes 1: M \rightarrow M\otimes_k \mathcal{O}(U)$, and $$\Delta' = \Delta - \mathrm{id} \otimes 1 - 1 \otimes \mathrm{id} : \mathcal{O}(U) \rightarrow \mathcal{O}(U) \otimes_k \mathcal{O}(U)\ .$$ Note that $x \in C_n M$ if and only if $(\Delta^r)^{n+1}x = 0$. If $M = \mathcal{O}(U)$ and $n \geq 1$, $x \in C_n\mathcal{O}(U)_+$ if and only if $(\Delta')^n x=0$. This follows since $$\Delta^r x = \Delta' x + 1 \otimes x \equiv  \Delta' x \pmod{C_{i-1}\mathcal{O}(U)}$$ for all $x \in C_i\mathcal{O}(U)$, and $i\geq 1$. In particular, $\mathrm{gr}^C_1 \mathcal{O}(U) = (C_1 \mathcal{O}(U))_+$ is the space of primitive elements in $\mathcal{O}(U)$, which we shall occasionally denote by $\hbox{Prim}(\mathcal{O}(U))$.

**Lemma 7**. *The coaction satisfies $$\Delta C_n(M) \subseteq \sum_{i+j=n} C_i(M) \otimes_k C_j (\mathcal{O}(U))\ .$$*

*Proof.* The coassociativity $(\Delta \otimes \mathrm{id} ) \Delta = (\mathrm{id} \otimes \Delta) \Delta$ implies, by substituting in the definitions of $\Delta'$ and $\Delta^r$, the following identity $$\label{Deltarcoassoc}
 (\Delta^r\otimes \mathrm{id} ) \Delta^r = (\mathrm{id} \otimes \Delta') \Delta^r\ .$$ Now let $x\in C_n(M)$, and write using a variant of Sweedler's notation $$\Delta^r x = \sum_{0 \leq i \leq n}  x_i \otimes \alpha_i$$ where the $x_i$ are in $C_i(M)$ and are linearly independent modulo $C_{i-1}(M)$. Since $x\in C_n(M)$, we have $(\Delta^r)^{k+1}x\in C_{n-k-1}M$. By coassociativity $(\ref{Deltarcoassoc})$ $$(\Delta^r)^{k+1}x  = (\mathrm{id} \otimes \Delta')^k \Delta^r x =  \sum_{0 \leq i \leq n}  x_i \otimes (\Delta')^k \alpha_i \ .$$ By the independence assumption on the $x_i$, we must have $(\Delta')^k \alpha_i =0$ whenever $i\geq n-k$, and hence $\alpha_{n-k} \in C_{k}$. ◻

It follows from the lemma that $$\Delta^r \, C_n M \,  \subseteq \,  C_{n-1} M \otimes_k C_1\mathcal{O}(U) + C_{n-2}M \otimes_k \mathcal{O}(U)$$ and taking the quotient modulo $C_{n-2}M\otimes_k \mathcal{O}(U)$ defines a map $$\delta:  \mathrm{gr}^C_n M \longrightarrow\mathrm{gr}^C_{n-1} M \otimes_k C_1\mathcal{O}(U)$$ which is injective by definition of the filtration $C$. The exact sequence $$0 \longrightarrow C_0 \mathcal{O}(U) \longrightarrow C_1 \mathcal{O}(U) \longrightarrow\mathrm{gr}^C_1 \mathcal{O}(U)\longrightarrow 0$$ splits via the augmentation map $\varepsilon$ which sends $C_1 \mathcal{O}(U) \rightarrow C_0\mathcal{O}(U) =k$. Thus $C_1 \mathcal{O}(U) = \mathrm{gr}^C_1 \mathcal{O}(U) \oplus k$. Since in any Hopf algebra $(\mathrm{id} \otimes \varepsilon )\Delta^r =0$, the map $\delta$ lands in the kernel of $\mathrm{id} \otimes \varepsilon$, namely $\mathrm{gr}^C_{n-1} M \otimes_k \mathrm{gr}^C_1\mathcal{O}(U)$.

**Definition 8**. Iterating $\delta$ we deduce an injective map $$\label{firstdefnofPhi} \Phi:  \mathrm{gr}^C M \longrightarrow M^U  \otimes_k T^c( \mathrm{gr}^C_1 \mathcal{O}(U))$$ where $T^c(V) = \bigoplus_{n\geq0 } V^{\otimes n}$ denotes the tensor coalgebra on $V$, graded by the length of tensors. Recall that $M^U = \mathrm{gr}^C_0 M$. The map $\Phi$ respects the grading on both sides. We shall call this map the *decomposition into primitives*.

In the case $M= \mathcal{O}(U)$, $M^U=k$ and $(\ref{firstdefnofPhi})$ yields an injective graded map $$\Phi: \mathrm{gr}^C_n \mathcal{O}(U) \longrightarrow T^c( \mathrm{gr}^C_1 \mathcal{O}(U))\ .$$ Equip the tensor coalgebra $T^c(V)$ with the deconcatenation coproduct $$\begin{aligned}
\Delta^{\mathrm{dec}} : T^c(V)  &\longrightarrow& T^c(V) \otimes_k T^c(V)   \nonumber \\
   v_1\otimes \ldots \otimes v_n  & \mapsto  &  \sum_{k=0}^n (v_1 \otimes \ldots \otimes v_k) \otimes (v_{k+1} \otimes \ldots \otimes v_n) \ .\nonumber
\end{aligned}$$

**Lemma 9**. *The following diagram commutes $$\begin{array}{ccc}
 \mathrm{gr}^C M  & \overset{\mathrm{gr}^C \Delta}{\longrightarrow}   & \mathrm{gr}^C M \otimes_k  \mathrm{gr}^C \mathcal{O}(U)   \\
  \downarrow &   & \downarrow   \\
   M^U \otimes_k T^c(\mathrm{gr}^C_1 \mathcal{O}(U))    & \overset{\mathrm{id} \otimes \Delta^{\mathrm{dec}}}{\longrightarrow}    &   M^U \otimes_k T^c(\mathrm{gr}^C_1 \mathcal{O}(U))  \otimes_k T^c(\mathrm{gr}^C_1 \mathcal{O}(U))
\end{array}\ .$$ where the vertical map on the left is $\Phi$, and that on the right is $\Phi \otimes \Phi$.*

*Proof.* The lemma can be proved directly from the recursive definition of $\Phi$ in terms of $\delta$. It is more instructive to give a conceptual proof by interpreting $\Phi$ in the following way. Consider the sequence $$0 \longrightarrow\mathrm{gr}^C_{n-1} M \longrightarrow C_nM / C_{n-2} M \longrightarrow\mathrm{gr}^C_{n} M \longrightarrow 0\ .$$ Since $U$ acts trivially on $\mathrm{gr}^C M$, it follows that the action of $U$ on $C_n M / C_{n-2}M$ factors through $U^{ab}$. If we write $\mathfrak{u}=\mathrm{Lie}\,\, U$, then we deduce a map $$\mathfrak{u}^{ab} \times  \mathrm{gr}^C_n M \longrightarrow\mathrm{gr}^C_{n-1} M$$ and hence, denoting $\mathbb{L}(\mathfrak{u}^{ab})$ the free Lie algebra on $\mathfrak{u}^{ab}$, we have $$\label{LLuaction}
\mathbb{L}(\mathfrak{u}^{ab}) \times \mathrm{gr}^C M \longrightarrow\mathrm{gr}^C M\ .$$ The affine ring of $\mathbb{L}(\mathfrak{u}^{ab})$ is the tensor coalgebra $T^c(\mathrm{gr}^C_1 \mathcal{O}(U))$, so the dual of the previous map is $\mathrm{gr}^C M \rightarrow \mathrm{gr}^C M \otimes_k T^c  (\mathrm{gr}^C_1 \mathcal{O}(U))$. The map $\Phi$ is obtained by projecting onto the component of $\mathrm{gr}^C M$ of degree zero. The fact that $\mathbb{L}(\mathfrak{u}^{ab}) \times \mathrm{gr}^C M \rightarrow \mathrm{gr}^C M$ is a left action can be expressed as a commutative diagram. Dualising it and projecting onto $C_0M$ gives precisely the statement of the lemma. ◻

### Multiplicative structure

Now suppose in addition that $M$ is a commutative $k$-algebra, and that the action of $U$ respects the multiplication $\mu$ on $M$. An induction on the indices $i, j$ shows that $\mu ( C_i M \times C_j M) \subseteq C_{i+j}M$. In particular $C_0M = M^U$ is a subalgebra of $M$. Recall that the tensor coalgebra $T^c(V)$ is equipped with a commutative product $\, \hbox{\rus x} \,$ called the shuffle product.

**Lemma 10**. *If $M$ is a commutative $k$-algebra, then $\Phi$ is a homomorphism of graded commutative $k$-algebras, where $T^c(V)$ is equipped with the shuffle product.*

*Proof.* Since $\Delta$ is a homomorphism, $\Delta^r(xy) = \Delta^r(x) (y \otimes 1) + ( x\otimes 1) \Delta^r(y) + \Delta^r(x) \Delta^r(y)$ for all $x,y\in M$. It follows that $\delta$ is a derivation $$\delta(xy) = (x\otimes 1 ) \delta(y) + \delta(x)  (y \otimes 1)$$ for $x,y \in \mathrm{gr}^C M$, and multiplication by $1$ denotes the identity on $\mathcal{O}(U)_+$. Now denote by $\partial$ the right deconcatenation map $\partial : T^c(V) \rightarrow T^c(V) \otimes V$ which is given by the formula $\partial (v_1\otimes \ldots \otimes v_n ) = (v_1\otimes \ldots \otimes v_{n-1}) \otimes v_n$. It follows from the definition of the map $\Phi$ as the iteration of $\delta$ that $$\partial \Phi  = (\Phi\otimes \mathrm{id} ) \delta \ .$$ Suppose that $\Phi(ab) = \Phi(a) \, \hbox{\rus x} \,\Phi(b)$ for all $a ,b \in \mathrm{gr}^C M$ of total degree $< n$. If $a$ or $b$ is in $\mathrm{gr}^C_0M$ then the statement is trivial. Then for $x,y \in \mathrm{gr}^C M$ of total degree $n$ and degree $\geq 1$, we have $$\partial \Phi(xy) = (\Phi\otimes \mathrm{id} ) \delta(xy) = (\Phi\otimes \mathrm{id} ) \big( (x\otimes 1 ) \delta(y) + \delta(x)  (y \otimes 1)\big)\ .$$ By induction hypothesis applied to $\Phi\otimes \mathrm{id}$, the right hand side is $$(\Phi\otimes \mathrm{id} )(x\otimes 1) \, \hbox{\rus x} \,(\Phi\otimes \mathrm{id} ) \delta(y) + (\Phi\otimes \mathrm{id} )(y \otimes 1) \, \hbox{\rus x} \,(\Phi\otimes \mathrm{id} ) \delta(x)$$ which by the previous equation gives $\partial \Phi(xy) = \Phi(x) \, \hbox{\rus x} \,\partial \Phi(y) + \Phi(y) \, \hbox{\rus x} \,\partial \Phi(x)$. This is in fact one of the many equivalent definitions of the shuffle product, and proves, by the injectivity of $\partial$, that $\Phi(xy) = \Phi(x) \, \hbox{\rus x} \,\Phi(y)$. ◻

Another way to see this lemma is simply to note that the action $(\ref{LLuaction})$ respects the multiplication on $\mathrm{gr}^C_M$ and to encode this by a commutative diagram. The dual diagram, after projecting to $C_0M$ in the appropriate place implies the lemma.

*Remark 11*. One can think of $C_i \mathcal{O}(U)$ as functions of 'unipotent monodromy' of degree $i$ in the following way. For any $f \in \mathcal{O}(U)$, viewed as a function on groups $U(R)$, for all commutative $k$-algebras $R$, define a new function $M_u f$ by $(M_u f)  (x)  = f(ux)-f(x)$. An $f\in C_i(\mathcal{O}(U))$ satisfies $M_{u_1} \ldots M_{u_{n}} f =0$ for all $u_1,\ldots, u_n \in U(R)$ whenever $n\geq i+1$. In particular, elements of $C_0 \mathcal{O}(U)$ are constant, and elements of $C_1 \mathcal{O}(U)$ are functions $f$ satisfying $f(ab) = f(a) + f(b)$.

### Cohomological interpretation

Let $M, U$ be as above. Consider the normalised cobar complex, dual to ([@Weibel], page 283), $$0 \rightarrow M   \rightarrow M\otimes_k \mathcal{O}(U) \rightarrow  M \otimes_k \mathcal{O}(U)_+ \otimes_k \mathcal{O}(U) \rightarrow   M \otimes_k \mathcal{O}(U)^{\otimes 2}_+ \otimes_k \mathcal{O}(U) \rightarrow \ldots$$ where the $(n+1)^{\mathrm{th}}$ arrow is given by $$d_n =  \sum_{i=0}^n (-1)^i\,  \mathrm{id} ^{\otimes i}\otimes \Delta \otimes \mathrm{id} ^{\otimes n-i}\ ,$$ (e.g., $d_1 = \Delta$, and $d_2 = \Delta \otimes \mathrm{id} - \mathrm{id} \otimes \Delta$), followed by projection onto $\mathcal{O}(U)_+$ in the appropriate places. It is a resolution of $M$ in the category of $\mathcal{O}(U)$-comodules. Derive the functor $\otimes_{\mathcal{O}(U)} k$, where $k$ is viewed as an $\mathcal{O}(U)$-module via the augmentation map, by applying it to the previous complex with the first two terms removed. It defines a complex $$\mathcal{R}_M: \qquad 0 \longrightarrow M  \overset{\Delta}{\longrightarrow} M\otimes_k \mathcal{O}(U)_+  {\longrightarrow} M \otimes_k \mathcal{O}(U)_+^{\otimes 2} {\longrightarrow} \ldots \ ,$$ with essentially the same differentials $d_n$ as before. Since the category of $\mathcal{O}(U)$-comodules is equivalent to the category of representations of $U$ of finite type, we deduce that $$H^n(\mathcal{R}_M) =\mathrm{Ext}^n_{\mathcal{O}(U)-\hbox{comod}}(k, M)=  \mathrm{Ext}^n_{\mathrm{Rep}(U)}(k, M)=  H^n(U; M) \ .$$ Therefore $H^0(U;M)  = H^0(\mathcal{R}_M)) =  C_0 M$, since the image of $\Delta m$ vanishes in $M \otimes \mathcal{O}(U)_+$ if and only if $\Delta m = m\otimes 1$. In the special case $M= k$, we have $$\mathcal{R}_{k} : \qquad 0 \longrightarrow k  \overset{0}{\longrightarrow}  \mathcal{O}(U)_+  \overset{\Delta'}{\longrightarrow} \mathcal{O}(U)_+^{\otimes 2}{\longrightarrow} \ldots$$ and it follows that $H^0(U;k)=k$, and $$H^1(U; k ) = \mathrm{gr}^C_1(\mathcal{O}(U)) \ .$$ This is a cohomological interpretation for the terms in the right-hand side of the decomposition map $\Phi$. The map $\delta$ can in fact be viewed as a differential in a certain spectral sequence, as we shall show below.

**Lemma 12**. *Viewing $\mathcal{O}(U)$ as a left $U$-module (right $\mathcal{O}(U)$-comodule) $$H^0(U;\mathcal{O}(U))=k \qquad \hbox{ and } \qquad H^n(U; \mathcal{O}(U))= 0  \hbox{  for all  } n \geq 1 \ .$$*

*Proof.* Take the cobar resolution with $M=k$ and reverse all tensors to give $$0 \rightarrow k   \rightarrow  \mathcal{O}(U) \rightarrow  \mathcal{O}(U) \otimes_k \mathcal{O}(U)_+ \rightarrow  \mathcal{O}(U) \otimes_k  \mathcal{O}(U)^{\otimes 2}_+  \rightarrow \ldots$$ It is a resolution for the same reasons as the cobar resolution. It agrees with $\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{O}(U)}$ from the second term onwards, and has the same differentials up to a possible overall sign, so we can read off the cohomology of $\mathcal{O}(U)$. ◻

**Proposition 13**. *Suppose that $U$ has cohomological dimension $1$. Then $$0 \longrightarrow\mathrm{gr}^C_n M \overset{\delta}{\longrightarrow} \mathrm{gr}^C_{n-1} M \otimes_k H^1(U;k) \longrightarrow\mathrm{gr}^C_{n-1} H^1(U;M) \longrightarrow 0$$ is exact for all $n \geq 1$.*

*Proof.* Filter the complex $\mathcal{R}_M$ by $F^p \mathcal{R}_M = \mathcal{R}_{C_{-p}M}$. It defines a spectral sequence with $E^0_{p,q} = \mathrm{gr}_F^p \mathcal{R}_M^{p+q}$ and $E^1_{p,q} = H^{p+q}(U; \mathrm{gr}^{C}_{-p} M)$, and converges to $\mathrm{gr}_F^p H^{p+q}(U; M)$. Since $\mathrm{gr}^C M$ is a trivial $U$-module we have $$E^1_{p,q} = \mathrm{gr}^C_{-p} M \otimes_k H^{p+q}(U;k)\ .$$ The differential $d^1: E^1_{-p,p} \rightarrow E^1_{1-p,p}$ is the operator $\delta$ defined earlier. Since $H^j(U;k)$ vanishes for all $j\geq 2$, $E^1_{p,q}$ vanishes unless $p+q\in \{0,1\}$ and the spectral sequence degenerates. Therefore the following sequence is exact: $$0 \rightarrow   \mathrm{gr}_F^{-n} H^0(U;M) \rightarrow \mathrm{gr}^C_n M \overset{\delta}{\rightarrow} \mathrm{gr}^C_{n-1} M \otimes_k H^1(U;k) \rightarrow \mathrm{gr}_F^{1-n} H^1(U;M) \rightarrow 0$$ The result follows since $H^0(U;M) = C_0 M$ and $\mathrm{gr}^{-n}_F H^0(U;M)=0$ if $n \geq 1$. ◻

**Corollary 14**. *Suppose that $M=  T\otimes_k \mathcal{O}(U)$, where $T$ is a trivial $U$-module, and $U$ has cohomological dimension $1$. Then the map $\Phi$ is an isomorphism $$\Phi: \mathrm{gr}^{C}  M \overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow} T \otimes_k  T^c(H^1(U;k))\ .$$*

*Proof.* By the previous two lemmas, $H^1(U;M) = H^1(U; \mathcal{O}(U))\otimes_k T=0$, and therefore $\delta : \mathrm{gr}^C_n M \rightarrow \mathrm{gr}^C_{n-1} M \otimes_k H^1(U;k)$ is surjective as the last term in the exact sequence of the previous lemma vanishes. The iterations of $\delta$ are therefore also surjective, hence so is $\Phi$. ◻

# Motivic periods over $\mathbb Q$

For the rest of this section, we only require the results of §(sectMatrixcoeffs) and §2.3 in the case $k=B_1= B_2 =\mathbb Q$.

## Category $\mathcal{H}$ of Betti and de Rham realisations {#sect: PeriodsoverQ}

Based on [@DeP1] §1.10, consider the category $\mathcal{H}$ whose objects are triples $(V_{B}, V_{dR},  c)$ consisting of the following data:

1.  A finite-dimensional $\mathbb Q$-vector space $V_B$ with a finite increasing (weight) filtration $W_{\bullet} V_B$.

2.  A finite-dimensional $\mathbb Q$-vector space $V_{dR}$ with a finite increasing (weight) filtration $W_{\bullet} V_{dR}$ and finite decreasing (Hodge) filtration $F^{\bullet} V_{dR}$.

3.  An isomorphism $$c: V_{dR} \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathbb C\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow} V_B\otimes_{\mathbb Q}\mathbb C$$ which respects the filtrations $W_{\bullet}$ on both sides.

4.  A linear involution $F_{\infty}: V_B \overset{\sim}{\rightarrow} V_B$ called the real Frobenius.

This data is subject to the conditions:

-   if $c_{dR}$ (resp. $c_B$) is the $\mathbb C$-antilinear involution on $V_{dR} \otimes \mathbb C$ (resp. $V_B \otimes \mathbb C$) given by $x\otimes \lambda \mapsto x \otimes \overline{\lambda}$, then the following diagram commutes: $$\begin{array}{ccc}
    V_{dR} \otimes \mathbb C&  \overset{c}{\longrightarrow}   &   V_B \otimes \mathbb C\\
     \downarrow_{c_{dR}}  &   &   \downarrow_{F_{\infty} \otimes c_B}  \\
     V_{dR} \otimes \mathbb C& \overset{c}{\longrightarrow}    &   V_B \otimes \mathbb C
    \end{array}$$ In particular, $c\, c_{dR} \, c^{-1}$ preserves the lattice $V_B \subset V_B \otimes \mathbb C$.

-   that $V_B$, equipped with the weight filtration $W_{\bullet}$ and Hodge filtration $c F^{\bullet}$ on $V_{\mathbb C}=V_B \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathbb C$, is a $\mathbb Q$-mixed Hodge structure. Writing $F$ instead of $cF$, this is equivalent to $\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{n} V_{\mathbb C} =\bigoplus_{p+q=n} F^p \cap \overline{F}^q$. We assume furthermore that this mixed Hodge structure is graded-polarizable.

The morphisms in the category $\mathcal{H}$ are given by morphisms of triples respecting the above data. It is shown in [@DeP1], 1.10, that $\mathcal{H}$ is a Tannakian category, the essential point being that mixed Hodge structures form an abelian category [@De2]. This category could be further enriched by adding more realisations.

By $(3$), the weight filtration defines a filtration on $(V_B, V_{dR}, c)$ by subobjects: $$\label{eqn: Wnsubobj}
W_n (V_B, V_{dR}, c) = (W_n V_B, W_n V_{dR}, c\big|_{W_n}) \ .$$ Denote the *Hodge numbers* []{#gloss: Hodgenumbers label="gloss: Hodgenumbers"} of an object $V=  (V_B, V_{dR}, c)$ in $\mathcal{H}$ by $$\label{eqn: Hodgenos}
h_{p,q}(V) = \dim_{\mathbb Q} (W_{p+q} \cap F^p) V_{dR} = \dim_{\mathbb C} (F^p \cap \overline{F}^q) V_{\mathbb C}\ .$$ The Tate objects $\mathbb Q(n)$, where $n\in \mathbb Z$, are the unique triples $(\mathbb Q,\mathbb Q, c)$ such that $c(1) = (2\pi i)^{-n}$, with weight $-2n$ and Hodge filtration on the second vector space $\mathbb Q$ defined by $F^{-n} \mathbb Q=\mathbb Q$, $F^{1-n} \mathbb Q=0$.

## The ring of $\mathcal{H}$-periods

The category $\mathcal{H}$ has two fiber functors $$\begin{aligned}
\omega_{\bullet} : \mathcal{H} &\longrightarrow&  \mathrm{Vec}_{\mathbb Q} \qquad \qquad \bullet= B \hbox{ or } dR \nonumber \\
 (V_{B}, V_{dR}, c) & \mapsto & V_{\bullet} \ ,\nonumber
\end{aligned}$$ so we can apply $\S\ref{sectMatrixcoeffs}$ with $\mathcal{T} =\mathcal{H}$, $k=\mathbb Q$. Let us write[^3] $$\mathfrak{m} = ( \omega_B, \omega_{dR}) \qquad \hbox{ and } \qquad \mathfrak{dr}= (\omega_{dR}, \omega_{dR})\ .$$ This defines rings $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }$ and $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{dr}}_{\mathcal{H} }$ as in §(sectMatrixcoeffs) and §2.3, and a canonical element $$c \in \mathrm{Isom}_{\mathcal{H} }^{\otimes}(\omega_{dR}, \omega_B)(\mathbb C)$$ which is given by the data $(3).$

**Definition 15**. The ring of $\mathcal{H}$-periods is $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }$. It is equipped with

-   a period homomorphism $$\mathrm{per}: \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} } \longrightarrow\mathbb C$$ which sends $[(V_B, V_{dR}, c), \sigma, \omega]^{\mathfrak{m} }$ to $\sigma c (\omega)$.

-   an increasing weight filtration $$W_n \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} } = \langle  [(V_{B}, V_{dR}, c),  \sigma ,\omega ]^{\mathfrak{m} }: \hbox{ where } \omega \in W_{n} V_{dR}  \rangle_{\mathbb Q}$$ Equivalently, this is the subspace spanned by $[M, \sigma, \omega]^{\mathfrak{m} }$ for objects $M$ in $\mathcal{H}$ satisfying $W_n M =M$ where $W_n$ was defined in ((eqn: Wnsubobj)).

-   a right coaction $\Delta^{\mathfrak{m} }: \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} } \longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }  \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{dr}}_{\mathcal{H} }$ or equivalently, a left action $$G_{\mathcal{H} }^{dR} \times \mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{H} }^{\mathfrak{m} } \longrightarrow\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{H} }^{\mathfrak{m} }\ .$$ It respects the weight filtration on $\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{H} }^{\mathfrak{m} }$ by $(\ref{eqn: Wnsubobj})$. Likewise, $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }$ admits a right action of the Betti Galois group $G_{\mathcal{H} }^B$ which also preserves $W$.

-   the real Frobenius involution $$F_{\infty}:  \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} } \overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}    \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }$$ defined by $F_{\infty} [M,  \sigma ,\omega ]^{\mathfrak{m} } = [M,  \sigma \circ F_{\infty},\omega]^{\mathfrak{m} }$. It has the property that $$\mathrm{per}(F_{\infty} \xi) = \overline{ \mathrm{per}(\xi)}\ ,$$ where the bar denotes complex conjugation. In particular, $F_{\infty}$-invariant motivic periods have periods in $\mathbb R$. The $G_{\mathcal{H} }^{dR}$-action commutes with $F_{\infty}$.

Note that, since $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }$ admits a $G^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} }$-action, it is the $\omega_{dR}$ image of an (ind-)object of the category $\mathcal{H}$ via theorem $\ref{thm:  Tannaka}$. Therefore $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }$ also carries, in addition to the weight filtration, a decreasing Hodge filtration $F$. The subspace $F^n \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }$ is spanned by the $[M,\sigma,v]^{\mathfrak{m} }$ with $v\in F^n M_{dR}$. The Hodge filtration is not preserved by the group $G^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} }$, but will nonetheless play a role later on, and is of course preserved by the (right) action of the Betti Galois group $G^B_{\mathcal{H} } = Aut^{\otimes}_{\mathcal{H} } (\omega_B)$.

## Some variants

Denote the subspace $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} , +}_{\mathcal{H} } \subset \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }$ of *effective $\mathcal{H}$-periods* []{#gloss: effectiveHp label="gloss: effectiveHp"} to be the subspace of $\mathcal{H}$-periods of objects with non-negative Hodge numbers $$\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} , +}_{\mathcal{H} } = \langle [M, \sigma , \omega]^{\mathfrak{m} } : M\in Ob(\mathcal{H} ) \hbox{ such that } h_{p,q}(M)=0   \hbox{ unless } p,q\geq 0\rangle_{\mathbb Q}$$ It forms a ring and is stable under the action of $G^{\mathfrak{dr}}_{\mathcal{H} }\times G^B_{\mathcal{H} }$.

Similarly, the ring of *mixed Artin-Tate $\mathcal{H}$-periods*[^4] []{#gloss: mixedTate label="gloss: mixedTate"} is the subspace $$\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{HT} = \langle [M, \sigma ,\omega]^{\mathfrak{m} } : M\in Ob(\mathcal{H} ) \hbox{ such that } F^p W_{p+q} M_{dR}=0 \hbox{ if } p>q \rangle_{\mathbb Q}\ .$$ It is again a ring and is stable under the action of $G^{\mathfrak{dr}}_{\mathcal{H} } \times G^B_{\mathcal{H} }$. The notation is justified as follows: let $HT\subset \mathcal{H}$ be the full Tannakian subcategory of $\mathcal{H}$ consisting of triples $(V_B, V_{dR}, c)$, whose underlying mixed Hodge structure has Hodge numbers $h_{p,q}=0$ if $p\neq q$. Its ring of motivic periods is $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{HT}$. Suppose that $M$ is any object of $\mathcal{H}$ of this type. Then the weight filtration on its de Rham vector space splits via $$\mathrm{gr}^W_{2n} M_{dR} = W_{2n} \cap F^n M_{dR}\ .$$ It follows that $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{HT}$ is also graded by the weight and we can write $$\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{HT} = \bigoplus_{n\in \mathbb Z}  \mathrm{gr}^W_{2n}  \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{HT}\ .$$ Note that the weight grading is not preserved by $G^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} }$, only the weight filtration. The weight grading is, however, preserved by $G^B_{\mathcal{H} }$ since the latter preserves the Hodge filtration.

Combining the two, we have a ring of *effective mixed Artin Tate $\mathcal{H}$-periods* $$\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,+}_{HT} = \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} , +}_{\mathcal{H} } \cap \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{HT}\ .$$ One can show that it is the largest subalgebra of $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{HT}$ which is stable under the action of $G^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} }$ and has non-negative weights.

The ring of $\mathcal{H}$-*de Rham* []{#gloss: HdeRham label="gloss: HdeRham"} periods $\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{H} }^{\mathfrak{dr}}$ has similar properties to $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }$ (weight filtration, left $G^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} }$ action), except that it does not have a real Frobenius involution, and the period map is replaced by the evaluation map (counit) $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathrm{ev}: \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{dr}}_{\mathcal{H} } & \longrightarrow& k \nonumber \\
 {[}M, e,v]^{\mathfrak{dr}} &\mapsto&  e(v)\ ,  \nonumber

\end{aligned}$$ which is nothing other than evaluation on the element $1 \in G^{\mathfrak{dr}}_H(k)$. There are analogous effective and Artin-Tate versions.

*Remark 16*. The fact that the weight filtration is strict on the category of mixed Hodge structures [@De2] implies that the functor $$(V_B, V_{dR}, c) \,   \mapsto \, \mathrm{gr}^W_{\bullet} (V_B, V_{dR}, c) = (\mathrm{gr}^W V_B, \mathrm{gr}^W V_{dR}, \mathrm{gr}^W c)$$ is exact. Thus, by composing with $\omega_{dR}$ or $\omega_B$ one obtains new fiber functors we denote by $\omega_{\underline{dR}} = \omega_{dR}\,  \mathrm{gr}^W$ and $\omega_{\underline{B}} = \omega_{B} \,\mathrm{gr}^W$.

## \*   Weight filtrations on $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{dr}}_{\mathcal{H} }$

The ring $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{dr}}_{\mathcal{H} }$ is none other than $\mathcal{O}(G^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} })$ viewed as a left $G^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} }$-module (or right $\mathcal{O}(G^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} })$-comodule). It could be written $\mathcal{P}^{l, \mathfrak{dr}}_{\mathcal{H} }$ to distinguish from $\mathcal{P}^{r, \mathfrak{dr}}_{\mathcal{H} }$ and $\mathcal{P}^{c,\mathfrak{dr}}_{\mathcal{H} }$ which are the same vector space, but considered with the right (respectively conjugation) action of $G^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} }$. We shall never consider $\mathcal{P}^{r,\mathfrak{dr}}$, except to remark that the antipode (inversion in $G^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} }$) interchanges $\mathcal{P}^{l,\mathfrak{dr}}_{\mathcal{H} }$ and $\mathcal{P}^{r,\mathfrak{dr}}_{\mathcal{H} }$. By the Tannaka theorem, these rings all define ind-objects in $\mathcal{H}$ and in particular are equipped with weight filtrations. It is important to note that, since the $G^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} }$-action is different in each case, these structures are distinct.

To avoid ambiguity, one can distinguish the following two coactions: $$\begin{aligned}
\Delta^{\mathfrak{m} , l} : \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} } \longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} } \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathcal{P}^{l, \mathfrak{dr}}_{\mathcal{H} } \nonumber \\
\Delta^{\mathfrak{m} , c} : \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} } \longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} } \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathcal{P}^{c, \mathfrak{dr}}_{\mathcal{H} } \nonumber
\end{aligned}$$ They are given by an identical formula, namely $(\ref{eqn: coaction})$, but differ in that the interpretation of the right-hand side is slightly different. If $G$ is a group acting on a set $X$, the former corresponds to the action of $G$ on $G\times X$ via $g (h, x) = (gh, x)$, where $g,h\in G$ and $x\in X$. This is the usual formula for a left action. It satisfies $\Delta^{\mathfrak{m} ,l} ( g\xi) = (\mathrm{id} \otimes g)\Delta^{\mathfrak{m} ,l} \xi$ for $g\in G^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} }$ and hence $\Delta^{\mathfrak{m} ,l} W_n\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }  \subset \mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{H} }^{\mathfrak{m} } \otimes_{\mathbb Q} W_n   \mathcal{P}^{l, \mathfrak{dr}}_{\mathcal{H} }$.

The second coaction $\Delta^{\mathfrak{m} ,c}$ corresponds to the action of $G$ on $G\times X$ given by the formula $g (h,x) = (ghg^{-1}, gx)$. Therefore $$\Delta^{\mathfrak{m} ,c} ( g\xi) = (g \otimes c_g) \Delta^{\mathfrak{m} ,c} \xi \qquad \hbox{ for } g\in G^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} }$$ where $c_g$ is conjugation by $g$. In this case we have $$\Delta^{\mathfrak{m} ,c} W_n\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }  \subset \sum_{i+j=n} W_i \mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{H} }^{\mathfrak{m} } \otimes_{\mathbb Q} W_j   \mathcal{P}^{c, \mathfrak{dr}}_{\mathcal{H} }\ .$$ Note that the ring $\mathcal{P}^{l, \mathfrak{dr}}_{\mathcal{H} }$ has elements in negative weights, but that $$\label{NoNegWeights}
 W_{-1} \mathcal{P}^{c, \mathfrak{dr}}_{\mathcal{H} } = 0 \ .$$ To see this, observe that the canonical map $(\ref{omegaMuniversalmap})$ $$\label{canmaptoPecdr}
 \omega_{dR}(M)^{\vee} \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \omega_{dR}(M) \rightarrow \mathcal{P}^{c,\mathfrak{dr}}_{\mathcal{H} }$$ is compatible with the action of $G^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} }$, and hence respects the weight filtration on both sides. The left-hand side can be identified with $\mathrm{End}(\omega_{dR}(M))^{\vee}$. Since elements of $G^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} }$ preserve the weight filtration on $\omega_{dR}(M)$ the natural transformation $G^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} } \rightarrow \mathrm{End}(\omega_{dR}(M))$ of functors from commutative $\mathbb Q$-algebras to sets, lands in $W_0 \mathrm{End}(\omega_{dR}(M))$, so dually, the image of $W_{-1} (\omega_{dR}(M)^{\vee} \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \omega_{dR}(M))$ under $(\ref{canmaptoPecdr})$ is zero. Since the direct sum of the maps $(\ref{canmaptoPecdr})$ generates $\mathcal{P}^{c,\mathfrak{dr}}_{\mathcal{H} }$, it follows that $W_{-1}  \mathcal{P}^{c,\mathfrak{dr}}_{\mathcal{H} }=0$.

## Some periods which could be called motivic

The period homomorphism $\mathrm{per}: \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} } \rightarrow \mathbb C$ is surjective since any complex number can be obtained as a period of a mixed Hodge structure. A *motivic period* will be an element of $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }$ which comes from the cohomology of an algebraic variety in a precise way. The following family of examples is sufficient for our purposes and covers many cases considered in [@KoZa].

**Example 17**. []{#ex: motivicperiods label="ex: motivicperiods"} Let $X$ be a smooth scheme over $\mathbb Q$ and $D\subset X$ a normal crossing divisor over $\mathbb Q$. Consider the triple consisting of

-   relative Betti cohomology $H_B^n (X,D) = H^n(X(\mathbb C), D(\mathbb C);\mathbb Q).$ Since complex conjugation on the topological spaces $X(\mathbb C), D(\mathbb C)$ is continuous it defines an involution $F_{\infty}: H_B^n (X,D) \overset{\sim}{\rightarrow} H_B^n (X,D)$.

-   relative algebraic de Rham cohomology $H_{dR}^n (X,D)$. It is the hypercohomology of the sheaf of Kähler differential forms on a cosimplicial variety constructed out of the irreducible components of $D$.

-   the comparison isomorphism ([@Groth]) $$\mathrm{comp}_{B,dR}: H_{dR}^n (X,D)\otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathbb C\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow} H^n_{B}(X, D) \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathbb C\ .$$

It follows from the existence of a natural mixed Hodge structure [@De2; @De3] that $$\label{ExampleHmotive}
H^n(X,D) :=   ( H_B^n(X,D), H^n_{dR}(X,D), \mathrm{comp}_{B,dR})$$ is an object in the category $\mathcal{H}$. Given a cohomology class $\omega \in H_{dR}^n (X,D)$ and a relative homology cycle $\sigma \in H_B^n (X,D)$ we can define the *motivic period* associated to this data to be the matrix coefficient $$\label{eqn: motperiod}
[ H^n(X,D) , \sigma , \omega]^{\mathfrak{m} } \in P^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }\ .$$ Its period $\sigma(\mathrm{comp}_{B,dR}\, \omega)\in \mathbb C$ could be written $\int_{\sigma} \omega$ and is given by a linear combination of integrals. The Hodge numbers $h_{p,q}$ of $H^n(X,D)$ are all zero unless $0\leq p, q \leq 2n$, so $(\ref{eqn: motperiod})$ is effective and lies in $W_{2n} \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,+}_{\mathcal{H} }$.

**Definition 18**. The space of *effective motivic periods* $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,+}$ is the subspace of $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,+}_{\mathcal{H} }$ spanned by the elements $(\ref{eqn: motperiod})$.

We shall not need to define a ring of motivic periods which are not effective (we shall never write $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }$ except in this sentence).

This working definition of effective motivic periods amply suffices for many purposes (e.g., the constant cosmic Galois group [@Cosmic]). By the Künneth formula, $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,+}$ is closed under multiplication and it is immediate from $(\ref{eqn: coaction})$ that it is closed under the action of $G_{\mathcal{H} }^{dR}$ and $G^B_{\mathcal{H} }$. Likewise, the ring of effective de Rham periods $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{dr},+}\subset \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{dr}, +}_{\mathcal{H} }$ is the subspace spanned by $[ H^n(X,D) , v, \omega]^{\mathfrak{dr}}$, where $v\in H^n_{dR}(X,D)^{\vee}$.

Now define $G^{dR}$ to be the quotient of $G_{\mathcal{H} }^{dR}$ by the subgroup which acts trivially on the ring $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,+}$ of effective motivic periods. The affine group scheme $G^{dR}$ acts faithfully on $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,+}$, and is an approximation to a motivic Galois group. Its category of representations $\mathrm{Rep}\,  G^{dR}$ is a crude version of a Tannakian category of mixed motives. A key point is that the groups $G^{dR}$ and $G_{\mathcal{H} }^{dR}$ act in an identical manner on $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,+}$.

A folklore version of Grothendieck's period conjecture states that

**Conjecture 1**. The period homomorphism $\mathrm{per}: \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,+} \rightarrow \mathbb C$ is injective.

Note that this conjecture is weaker than the analoguous conjecture for the motivic periods of the category of Nori motives, for example.

## Some terminology

We list a sample of possible quantities to describe $\mathcal{H}$ periods. For any object $M$ in $\mathcal{H}$ one can make the following definitions.

-   Let $M_B^{+}, M_B^-$ denote the $\pm$ eigenspaces for $F_{\infty}$, and set $\mathrm{rank}^{\pm} M = \dim_{\mathbb Q} M^{\pm}_B$. The comparison $M_{dR} \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathbb C\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow} M_{B} \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathbb C$ implies that $$\mathrm{rank}(M)= \mathrm{rank}^+(M) + \mathrm{rank}^-(M) = \dim_{\mathbb Q} M_{dR}\ .$$

-   Define the *de Rham Galois group* []{#gloss: GaloisGroup label="gloss: GaloisGroup"} $G^{dR}(M)$ of $M$ to be the largest quotient of $G^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} }$ which acts faithfully on $M_{dR}$. Equivalently, it is the de Rham Tannaka group of the full Tannakian subcategory of $\mathcal{H}$ generated by $M$. Define the *Betti Galois group* in the same way on replacing $dR$ by $B$. The comparison map gives a canonical isomorphism $$G^{B}(M) \times \mathbb C\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow} G^{dR}(M) \times \mathbb C\ .$$ Define the *transcendence dimension* []{#gloss: transdim label="gloss: transdim"} of $M$ to be $$\dim_{\mathrm{tr}} (M) = \dim G^{dR}(M) = \dim G^{B}(M) \ .$$ Define the *component group* []{#gloss: component group label="gloss: component group"} $\pi^{\bullet}_0(M)$ to be $\pi_0 (G^{\bullet}(M))$, where $\bullet = B,dR$ and $\pi_0$ is the étale group scheme whose affine ring $\mathcal{O}(\pi^{\bullet}_0(M))$ is the largest separable subalgebra of $\mathcal{O}(G^{\bullet}(M))$ (see [@Waterhouse], §6.5-7). Since $\pi_0$ commutes with change of base field, the comparison isomorphism gives $$\pi^{B}_0(M) \times \mathbb C\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow} \pi^{dR}_0(M)\times \mathbb C\ .$$

-   Define the *Hodge polynomial* []{#gloss: Hodgepoly label="gloss: Hodgepoly"} of $M$ to be (see $(\ref{eqn: Hodgenos})$) $$h(M) (r,s) = \sum_{r,s}    h_{p,q}(M) \, r^p s^q   \quad \in \quad  \mathbb Z[r^{\pm},s^{\pm}]$$

-   Define the class of a *period matrix* []{#gloss: periodmatrix label="gloss: periodmatrix"} of $M$ as follows. Let $M = (M_{B}, M_{dR}, c_{M})$, and $r= \mathrm{rank}(M)$. Choose isomorphisms $M_B \cong \mathbb Q^r$ and $M_{dR} \cong \mathbb Q^r$, which are adapted to the weight (resp. Hodge and weight) filtrations, and write $c_M$ in this basis. This gives a well-defined element $$[c_{M}] \quad \in \quad  W_0 \mathrm{GL}(M_B)  \backslash W_0 \mathrm{GL}( \mathbb C^r) / F^0 W_0 \mathrm{GL}(M_{dR})$$ where $W_0 \mathrm{GL}(M_B)$ denotes the subgroup of $\mathrm{GL}(M_B)(\mathbb Q)$ which preserves $W$, and so on. It is an equivalence class of square $d\times d$ matrices of complex numbers, where $d$ is the rank of $M$. The matrix $c_M$ in fact lies in the subspace satisfying $F_{\infty} c_M = \overline{c}_M$. Thus if we furthermore choose our Betti basis $M_B \cong \mathbb Q^r$ to be compatible with the decomposition $M_B = M_B^+ \oplus M^-_B$, then the rows corresponding to $M_B^+$ have entries in $\mathbb R$, and those corresponding to $M_B^-$ have entries in $i\mathbb R$.

    The *determinant* []{#gloss: determinant label="gloss: determinant"} $\det(M)$ is defined to be $\det(c_{M})$ in $\mathbb C/ \mathbb Q^{\times}$.

**Definition 19**. Now let $\xi \in \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }$ and denote its minimal object (§2.4) by $M(\xi)$. This enables us to attach the following invariants to $\xi$:

1.  Define the *space of de Rham Galois conjugates* []{#gloss: conjugates label="gloss: conjugates"} of $\xi$ to be the right $\mathcal{O}(G_{\mathcal{H} }^{dR})$-subcomodule of $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }$ generated by $\xi$. It is isomorphic to $M(\xi)_{dR}$ by corollary 6.

    Likewise, define the *space of Betti Galois conjugates* to be the left $\mathcal{O}(G_{\mathcal{H} }^{B})$-subcomodule of $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }$ generated by $\xi$. It is isomorphic to $M(\xi)_{B}$ by corollary 6.

    Define the *space of biconjugates of* $\xi$ to be the left $\mathcal{O}(G_{\mathcal{H} }^{B})$ and right $\mathcal{O}(G_{\mathcal{H} }^{dR})$-subcomodule of $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }$ generated by $\xi$. It is the vector space spanned by the set of all matrix coefficients $[M(\xi), M(\xi)_{B}^{\vee}, M(\xi)_{dR}]^{\mathfrak{m} }$.

    Define the *algebra of de Rham/Betti/bi-conjugates of $\xi$* to be the subalgebras of $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }$ generated by the above vector spaces.[^5]

2.  Define the $\mathrm{rank}( \xi): = \mathrm{rank} \,M(\xi)$ (similarly $\mathrm{rank}^{\pm} (\xi) = \mathrm{rank}^{\pm} \,M(\xi)$). []{#gloss: rank label="gloss: rank"}

3.  Define the de Rham (resp. Betti) *Galois group* $G^{\bullet}_{\xi}= G^{\bullet}(M(\xi))$, where $\bullet \in \{B, dR\}$. Define the *transcendence dimension* []{#gloss: transdim2 label="gloss: transdim2"} of $\xi$ to be $$\dim_{\mathrm{tr}} (\xi) = \dim G^{dR}_{\xi} = \dim G^{B}_{\xi}  \ .$$ Define the *component group* []{#gloss: component group2 label="gloss: component group2"} to be $\pi^{\bullet}_0(\xi):=\pi_0 (G^{\bullet}_{\xi})$, and the *degree* (for want of a better word) of $\xi$ to be $\deg(\xi) = \big| \pi^{\bullet}_0(\xi)(\mathbb C) \big| \in \mathbb N$.

4.  Define the *Hodge polynomial* []{#gloss: Hodgepoly2 label="gloss: Hodgepoly2"} of $\xi$ to be $h(\xi) (r,s) =  h(M(\xi)) (r,s)$.

5.  Define the class of a *period matrix* []{#gloss: periodmatrix2 label="gloss: periodmatrix2"} of $\xi$ to be $[c_{\xi}] = [c_{M(\xi)}]$, and define the *determinant* to be $\det(\xi)=\det(c_{\xi})$.

Many of the above definitions go through for an $\mathcal{H}$-de Rham period $\xi \in \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{dr}}_{\mathcal{H} }$ with the obvious changes, which we leave to the reader.

The Hodge polynomial $h(\xi)$ is symmetric in $r, s$ and satisfies $\mathrm{rank}\, \xi = h(\xi)(1,1)$. The element $\xi$ is *effective* if and only if $h(\xi)\in \mathbb Z[r,s]$, and is *mixed Artin-Tate* (lies in $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{HT}$) if and only if $\xi \in \mathbb Z[(rs)^{\pm 1}]$.

It follows from the formulae for sums and products of matrix coefficients that if $\xi_1, \xi_2 \in \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }$ then $M_{\xi_1+ \xi_2}$ is a subquotient of $M_{\xi_1} \oplus M_{\xi_2}$ and $M_{\xi_1 \xi_2}$ is a subquotient of $M_{\xi_1} \otimes M_{\xi_2}$. Therefore the rank satisfies $$\mathrm{rank} (\xi_1 + \xi_2 ) \leq \mathrm{rank}( \xi_1) +  \mathrm{rank}( \xi_2)  \quad \hbox{ and } \quad  \mathrm{rank} (\xi_1 \xi_2 ) \leq \mathrm{rank}( \xi_1) \,\mathrm{rank}( \xi_2)\ .$$ More generally, the Hodge polynomial satisfies $$h(\xi_1+ \xi_2)  \preccurlyeq h(\xi_1) + h(\xi_2) \quad \hbox{ and  } \quad h(\xi_1\xi_2)  \preccurlyeq h(\xi_1)  h(\xi_2)$$ where $\preccurlyeq$ means that the inequality $\leq$ holds coefficient by coefficient.

*Remark 20*. The transcendence dimension of $\xi$ is $\dim G^{dR}_{\xi} = \deg_{\mathrm{tr}} \mathcal{O}(G^{dR}_{\xi})$. The latter is the ring generated by the 'de Rham biconjugates' $[M(\xi), v, w]^{\mathfrak{dr}}$ for all $v \in M(\xi)^{\vee}_{dR}$ and $w \in M(\xi)_{dR}$. Applying the comparison map, this is isomorphic to the ring generated by the biconjugates $[M(\xi), \sigma, w]^{\mathfrak{m} }$ where $\sigma \in M(\xi)^{\vee}_{B}$ and $w \in M(\xi)_{dR}$, tensored with $\mathbb C$. It follows from this argument that $$\dim_{\mathrm{tr}}(\xi) = \deg_{\mathrm{tr}} \langle \hbox{Ring of biconjugates of } \xi \rangle$$ Therefore the period conjecture 1 implies the following (compare [@An1]):

**Conjecture 2**. Let $\xi \in \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} , +}$ be a motivic period. Let $P_{\xi} \subset \mathbb C$ be the $\mathbb Q$-algebra generated by the images of the Galois biconjugates of $\xi$ under the period homomorphism. Then the transcendence degree of $P_{\xi}$ satisfies $\deg_{\mathrm{tr}} P_{\xi} = \dim_{\mathrm{tr}} (\xi)$.

## Semi-simple and unipotent periods

Let $\mathcal{H} ^{ss}$ denote the full Tannakian subcategory of $\mathcal{H}$ generated by semi-simple objects. Define the *ring of semi-simple (or pure) periods* []{#gloss: semisimple label="gloss: semisimple"} to be $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} ^{ss}}$, and respectively $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{dr}}_{\mathcal{H} ^{ss}}$ its de Rham version.

Every object of $\mathcal{H} ^{ss}$ is graded by the weight filtration. It follows that $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} ^{ss}}$ is also graded by the weight filtration. The action of the group $G^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} }$ on $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} ^{ss}}$ factors through a quotient we denote by $S^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} }$. It is a projective limit of reductive affine algebraic groups over $\mathbb Q$, and there is an exact sequence $$\label{UHexactsequence}
1 \longrightarrow U^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} } \longrightarrow G^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} } \longrightarrow S^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} } \longrightarrow 1$$ where $U_{\mathcal{H} }^{dR}$ is pro-unipotent. Define the ring of *unipotent de Rham periods* []{#gloss: unipotentperiods label="gloss: unipotentperiods"} to be $$\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathcal{H} }= \mathcal{O}(U^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} })\ ,$$ equipped with the conjugation action of $G^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} }$. The left action of $U^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} }$ on $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }$ is equivalent to a right coaction, which we call the *unipotent de Rham* coaction $$\label{Deltau} \Delta\!^u: \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} } \longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} } \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathcal{H} }\ .$$ It is given by the same formula as $(\ref{eqn: coaction})$, where elements on the right hand side of the tensor product are viewed as functions on $U^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} }$. This coaction is equivariant with respect to the action of $G^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} }$, i.e., $\Delta\!^u (g \xi) = (g\otimes c_g) \Delta\!^u \xi$, where $c_g$ denotes conjugation by $g\in G^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} }$. In particular, $\Delta\!^u W_n \subset \sum_{i+j = n} W_i \otimes W_j$.

The ring of unipotent periods $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathcal{H} }$ is equipped with an *antipode* []{#gloss: U-antipode label="gloss: U-antipode"} $$S: \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathcal{H} } \rightarrow \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathcal{H} }\ ,$$ which is dual to inversion in the group $U^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} }$. Using notation introduced earlier, the restriction gives a natural surjective map $\mathcal{P}^{c, \mathfrak{dr}}_{\mathcal{H} } \rightarrow \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathcal{H} }$ which is $G^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} }$ equivariant, and it follows from a previous calculation $(\ref{NoNegWeights})$ that $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathcal{H} }$ has non-negative weights: $$W_{-1} \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathcal{H} } = 0\ .$$

*Remark 21*. If one replaces the de Rham functor with the graded de Rham functor $\omega_{\underline{dR}}$, then the analogous sequence to $(\ref{UHexactsequence})$ is canonically split, since $\mathrm{gr}^W$ is a fiber functor from $\mathcal{H}$ to $\mathcal{H} ^{ss}$ which is the identity on $\mathcal{H} ^{ss}$. Thus $G^{\underline{dR}}_{\mathcal{H} } = U^{\underline{dR}}_{\mathcal{H} } \rtimes S^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} }$.

**Proposition 22**. *There is a non-canonical isomorphism of algebras $$\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }\otimes_{\mathbb Q} \overline{\mathbb Q} \cong   \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} ^{ss}}   \otimes_{\mathbb Q}  \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathcal{H} } \otimes_{\mathbb Q}  \overline{\mathbb Q} \ .$$ It does not respect the coalgebra structure.*

*Proof.* Choose points in $\mathrm{Isom}_{\mathcal{H} }(\omega_{B}, \omega_{dR})(\overline{\mathbb Q})$ and $\mathrm{Isom}_{\mathcal{H} } (\omega_{dR}, \omega_{\underline{dR}})(\overline{\mathbb Q}).$ They induce isomorphisms over $\overline{\mathbb Q}$ $$\mathrm{Isom}_{\mathcal{H} } (\omega_{dR}, \omega_B) \times \overline{\mathbb Q}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow} \mathrm{Isom}_{\mathcal{H} } (\omega_{dR}, \omega_{dR})\times \overline{\mathbb Q} \cong  \mathrm{Isom}_{ \mathcal{H} } (\omega_{\underline{dR}}, \omega_{\underline{dR}})\times \overline{\mathbb Q}\ .$$ The group in the middle is $G_{\mathcal{H} }^{dR}\times \overline{\mathbb Q}$, and the one on the right is $G_{\mathcal{H} }^{\underline{dR}}\times \overline{\mathbb Q}$ which splits canonically since $G_{\mathcal{H} }^{\underline{dR}}\cong U_{\mathcal{H} }^{\underline{dR}} \rtimes S^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} }$ by remark 21.
