On the level of affine rings we deduce isomorphisms of algebras $\mathcal{O}(G_{\mathcal{H} }^{dR}) \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \overline{\mathbb Q}\cong \mathcal{O}( G_{\mathcal{H} }^{\underline{dR}})\otimes_{\mathbb Q} \overline{\mathbb Q} =   \mathcal{O}(S_{\mathcal{H} }^{dR})\otimes_{\mathbb Q}   \mathcal{O}( U_{\mathcal{H} }^{\underline{dR}}) \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \overline{\mathbb Q}$. This gives a non-canonical isomorphism of algebras $$\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} } \otimes_{\mathbb Q}  \overline{\mathbb Q} \cong    \mathcal{O}(S_{\mathcal{H} }^{dR})   \otimes_{\mathbb Q}  \mathcal{O}(U_{\mathcal{H} }^{dR}) \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \overline{\mathbb Q}  \ ,$$ which, on taking $U_{\mathcal{H} }^{dR}$-invariants, induces $\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{H} ^{ss}}^{\mathfrak{m} } \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \overline{\mathbb Q} \cong \mathcal{O}(S_{\mathcal{H} }^{dR}) \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \overline{\mathbb Q}$. The statement follows from the identification $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathcal{H} } =  \mathcal{O}(U_{\mathcal{H} }^{dR})$. ◻

## Filtration by unipotency and decomposition

The existence of the weight filtration implies that we can apply the constructions of §2.5 to $$U = U^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} } \qquad \hbox{ and } \qquad M = \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }\ ,$$ where $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }$ is equipped with the comodule structure $\Delta^u:M \rightarrow M \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathcal{O}(U)$.

**Definition 23**. We shall say that an element $\xi$ in $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }$ is of *unipotency degree* or *coradical degree* $\leq i$ if it lies in $C_i\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }$.

An element $\xi \in \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }$ is of unipotency degree zero if and only if $\Delta(\xi)= \xi \otimes 1$, so it is $U^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} }$-invariant and hence semi-simple: $$C_0 \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} } =\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} ^{ss}}\ .$$ An element $\xi$ of unipotency degree at most one corresponds to a period of a simple extension. This is discussed in further detail in §6.

Recall that $\Delta^{u,r} = \Delta^u - \mathrm{id} \otimes 1.$ Then $\xi \in C_i \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_H$ if and only if $$(\Delta^{u,r})^{i+1} \xi  = 0\ .$$

As in §2.5, we deduce the existence of a derivation $$\delta : C_n \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} } \longrightarrow C_{n-1} \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} } \otimes_{\mathbb Q} H^1(U) \ .$$

**Definition 24**. The *decomposition into primitives map* $$\Phi: \mathrm{gr}^C_{\bullet} \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} } \longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} ^{ss}} \otimes_{\mathbb Q} T^c(H^1(U)   )$$ is defined by iterating $\delta$.

The map $\Phi$ is a homomorphism of $S^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} }$-modules. To see this, recall that the coaction $\Delta^u$ is equivariant with respect to the action of $G^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} }$ on the left on $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }$, and by conjugation on $\mathcal{O}(U)$. Therefore so is $\Delta^{u,r}$, and likewise $\delta$. On the other hand, $U$ acts trivially on both $\mathrm{gr}^C \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }$ and $H^1(U)$, so the action of $G^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} }$ factors through its quotient $S^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} }$. Hence $\delta$ is $S^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} }$-equivariant, and by iteration, so is $\Phi$.

The map $\Phi$, together with the invariants defined above, give the first steps towards a classification of motivic periods by group theory and provides a tool for proving linear or algebraic independence of motivic periods (e.g., [@BrMTZ]). This is discussed in §6, where we shall also show that $\Phi$ is in fact an isomorphism. Note that $\Phi$ is not to be confused with the notion of symbol (§9).

# \*   Further remarks on motivic periods

The paragraphs below are independent from each other and can be skipped.

## Universal period matrix and 'single-valued' periods

Let $M$ be an object of $\mathcal{H}$. Then there is a canonical morphism $$\label{univcoact}  M_{dR} \longrightarrow M_{B} \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{H} }^{\mathfrak{m} }$$ obtained by composing the natural map $\delta^{\vee} \otimes \mathrm{id} :  \mathbb Q\otimes_{\mathbb Q} M_{dR} \rightarrow M_B\otimes_{\mathbb Q} M_B^{\vee} \otimes_{\mathbb Q} M_{dR}$ with the map $(\ref{omegaMuniversalmap})$ $M_B^{\vee} \otimes_{\mathbb Q} M_{dR} \rightarrow \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }$. It is given by the formula $$v \mapsto \sum_i e_i \otimes [M, e_i^{\vee}, v]^{\mathfrak{m} }$$ where $e_i$ (resp. $e_i^{\vee}$) is a basis (resp. dual basis) of $M_B$. Extending scalars from $\mathbb Q$ to $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }$, it defines an isomorphism $$c^{\mathfrak{m} }_M: \,  M_{dR} \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} } \overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow} M_B \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }$$ which is functorial in $M$ and which we think of as a *universal comparison map*. []{#gloss: universalcomparison label="gloss: universalcomparison"} It is equal to the isomorphism of fiber functors $\iota_M$ (the notation is defined in $(\ref{phiM})$; set $B_1=B_2=k=\mathbb Q$ and $R= \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }$), where $$\iota \in \mathrm{Isom}_{\mathcal{H} }^{\otimes}(\omega_{dR}, \omega_B) ( \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} })$$ is the element corresponding to the identity on $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }$. Since $c \in \mathrm{Isom}_{\mathcal{H} }^{\otimes}(\omega_{dR}, \omega_B)(\mathbb C)$ is, by definition of the period homomorphism, equal to $\mathrm{per}(\iota)$, it follows that the comparison map $c_M: M_{dR}  \rightarrow M_B \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathbb C$ is obtained from $(\ref{univcoact})$ by applying the period homomorphism; i.e., $c_M = (\mathrm{id} \otimes \mathrm{per}) c^{\mathfrak{m} }_M$.

As a first application, the universal coaction $(\ref{univcoact})$ defines a lift of the period matrix of $M$ to the ring of $\mathcal{H}$-periods: $$[c^{\mathfrak{m} }_M] \quad \in \quad  W_0 \mathrm{GL}(M_B)  \backslash W_0 \mathrm{GL}(\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }, r ) / F^0 W_0 \mathrm{GL}(M_{dR})\ $$ where $r = \mathrm{rank}\, M$. We have $[c_M] = \mathrm{per}[c^{\mathfrak{m} }_M]$ since the period homomorphism is $\mathbb Q$-linear. Applying this to the minimal object $M= M(\xi)$ defines an invariant $[c^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\xi}]$ of any element $\xi \in \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }$. Its determinant is an element $\det(c^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\xi}) \in \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }/\mathbb Q^{\times}$.

Another application is to construct single-valued versions of $\mathcal{H}$-periods, inspired by [@Be-De]. We only need the fact that the real Frobenius $F_{\infty}$ defines a $\mathbb Q$-linear involution on $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }$ which commutes with the action of $G^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} }$. Let $$f \in     \mathrm{Isom}_{\mathcal{H} }^{\otimes}(\omega_{dR}, \omega_B) ( \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} })$$ correspond to $F_{\infty} : \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} } \overset{\sim}{\rightarrow} \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }$. It satisfies $f_M = (F_{\infty} \otimes \mathrm{id} ) \iota_M = (\mathrm{id} \otimes F_{\infty}) \iota_M$. Since $\mathrm{Isom}_{\mathcal{H} }^{\otimes}({\omega_{dR}, \omega_B})$ is a right $G^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} }$-torsor, there is a unique element $$\mathbf{s}\in G^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} }(\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }) \quad \hbox{such that} \quad  f \, \mathbf{s}= \iota  \ $$ which is computed explicitly below. This gives rise to a homomorphism (*single-valued map*) []{#gloss: singlevalued1 label="gloss: singlevalued1"} $$\label{single-valuedmapdefn}
\mathbf{s}^{\mathfrak{m} }: \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{dr}}_{\mathcal{H} } \longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }$$ which is $G^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} }$-equivariant if one equips the left-hand side $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{dr}}_{\mathcal{H} }=\mathcal{O}(G^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} })$ with the action of $G^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} }$ by conjugation.

*Remark 25*. In [@SVMP] a slightly different single-valued map $\mathrm{sv}^{\mathfrak{m} }$ was defined on the ring of mixed Tate periods, which is graded by weight. It is defined by a similar formula on replacing $F_\infty$ by $F_{\infty}$ twisted by $(-1)^n$ in weight $2n$.

The situation is summarised by the following commutative diagram $$\begin{array}{ccc}
M_{dR} \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }   & \overset{\iota_M}{\longrightarrow}    & M_B \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }    \\
 \downarrow_{\mathbf{s}^{\mathfrak{m} }_M}   &   & \uparrow_{ \mathrm{id} \otimes F_{\infty}}    \\
M_{dR} \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }   & \overset{
\iota_M}{\longrightarrow}   &   M_B \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }
\end{array}$$ where all maps are isomorphisms, which is functorial in $M$.

The *single-valued $\mathcal{H}$-period matrix* []{#gloss: singlevaluedperiodmatrix1 label="gloss: singlevaluedperiodmatrix1"} $s^{\mathfrak{m} }_M:M_{dR} \rightarrow M_{dR} \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }$ can be computed as follows. Since $F_{\infty}^{-1} = F_{\infty}$, it follows from the above that $s^{\mathfrak{m} }_M$ is given by the composite $\iota_M^{-1} \circ  (\mathrm{id} \otimes F_{\infty})^{-1} \circ \iota_M= \iota_M^{-1} \circ  (\mathrm{id} \otimes F_{\infty}) \circ \iota_M$ which is explicitly $$M_{dR} \overset{c^{\mathfrak{m} }_M}{\longrightarrow} M_B \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} } \,\,{\overset{\mathrm{id} \otimes F_{\infty}}{\longrightarrow}} \,\, M_B \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} } \overset{(c^{\mathfrak{m} }_M)^{-1}}{\longrightarrow} M_{dR} \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }\ .$$ Thus if $C_M$ is a matrix representing the map $c^{\mathfrak{m} }_M$ with respect to some choice of bases for $M_{dR}, M_B$, then $s^{\mathfrak{m} }_M$ is represented by $(F_{\infty} C_M)^{-1} C_M$. This is indeed invariant under change of basis for $M_B$, which amounts to replacing $C_M$ with $P C_M$ for some $P \in \mathrm{GL}(M_B;\mathbb Q)$; the quantity $(F_{\infty} C_M)^{-1} C_M$ is unchanged since $F_{\infty}$ acts trivially on the coefficients of $P$ because they are rational.

Finally, the *single-valued period matrix* $s_M$ is obtained by applying the period map to $s^{\mathfrak{m} }_M$, and is given directly from the usual comparison map by $s_M = \overline{C}_M^{-1} C_M$.

## Motivic philosophy

It is hoped that there exists a neutral Tannakian category $\mathcal{MM}_{\mathbb Q}$ of mixed motives over $\mathbb Q$ equipped, in particular, with Betti and de Rham realisations, and hence a functor $\mathcal{MM}_{\mathbb Q} \rightarrow \mathcal{H}$ and thus a homomorphism $$\label{eqn:  MMtoH}
\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{MM}_{\mathbb Q}} \longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} } \ .$$ The elements $(\ref{ExampleHmotive})$ should certainly be in its image, and the following diagram $$\begin{aligned}
\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{MM}_{\mathbb Q}}  &\longrightarrow&   \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{MM}_{\mathbb Q}}     \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{dr}}_{\mathcal{MM}_{\mathbb Q}} \nonumber\\
\downarrow &  &  \qquad \downarrow  \qquad  \nonumber \\
\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} } &\longrightarrow&   \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }  \otimes_{\mathbb Q}  \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{dr}}_{\mathcal{H} }     \nonumber \ ,
\end{aligned}$$ where the horizontal maps are given by the coactions $(\ref{eqn: coaction})$, would commute. Therefore the action of $G^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} }$ on the de Rham realisation $M_{dR}$ of an object $M\in \mathcal{MM}_{\mathbb Q}$ would be motivic, i.e., would factor through $G^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} } \rightarrow G^{dR}_{\mathcal{MM}_{\mathbb Q}}$. Grothendieck's period conjecture states that the period $\mathrm{per}:  \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{MM}_{\mathbb Q}} \rightarrow \mathbb C$ is injective, which motivates many classical conjectures in transcendence theory [@An1; @An2; @Be]. Since $\mathrm{per}$ factors through $(\ref{eqn:  MMtoH})$ this would imply conjecture 1 and a much weaker conjecture: namely that the homomorphism $(\ref{eqn:  MMtoH})$ is injective. In this case, relations between elements of $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{MM}_{\mathbb Q}}$ could be detected in $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }$, and $G^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} } \rightarrow G^{dR}_{\mathcal{MM}_{\mathbb Q}}$ would have the same image in $Aut(M_{dR})$ for every object $M \in \mathcal{MM}_{\mathbb Q}$ (and likewise for Betti). It is for this reason that we allow ourselves to call the periods $(\ref{eqn: motperiod})$ 'motivic'.

One important situation in which much of the above certainly works is the case $\mathcal{MT}(\mathbb Q)$ of mixed Tate (or Artin-Tate) motives over $\mathbb Q$[^6] [@LevineTate; @DeGo]. One then has a morphism $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{MT}(\mathbb Q)} \rightarrow \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }$ which is known to be injective (by the full faithfulness of the Hodge realisation). Borel's deep results on the rational algebraic $K$-theory of $\mathbb Q$ (see example $\ref{exampleMTcase}$ below) give a precise upper bound for the size of the ring $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{MT}(\mathbb Q)}$. Several applications of motives to number theory rely in an essential way on this upper bound. Note that even if one has the 'right' definition of $\mathcal{MM}_{\mathbb Q}$, this upper bound is not available in general.

*Remark 26*. Defining mixed motives as a full subcategory of realisations (à la Jannsen, Deligne) as opposed to by explicit generators and relations (à la Nori) would not give the same answer if the realisation functors are not fully faithful. Furthermore, theorems about the *independence* of motivic periods proved in the ring $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }$ will carry over unconditionally to any reasonable definition of a category of mixed motives (irrespective of whether $(\ref{eqn:  MMtoH})$ is injective or not). On the other hand, when proving *relations* between motivic periods, it is preferable to prove them using morphisms of mixed Hodge structures which come from geometry, in which case they would also carry over to any suitably defined $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{MM}_{\mathbb Q}}$.

## Projection map

An inconvenience of working with de Rham periods is the lack of a (complex) period homomorphism. One way around this is to construct single-valued periods as we did in §4.1. Another approach is to write de Rham periods as images of motivic periods. The latter works particularly well in the mixed Artin-Tate case as we now explain.

**Proposition 27**. *Every effective motivic period of weight zero is a motivic algebraic number. The period map gives an isomorphism $$\mathrm{per}: W_0 \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,+} \overset{\sim}{\rightarrow} \overline{\mathbb Q} \ .$$*

*Proof.* See §10.2 below. ◻

Suppose that $M$ is an object of $\mathcal{H}$ which is effective (all Hodge numbers $h_{p,q}(M)$ vanish unless $p, q\geq0$). Say that $M$ is *separated* []{#gloss: separated label="gloss: separated"} if $$W_0 M_{dR} \longrightarrow M_{dR} \longrightarrow M_{dR} / F^1 M_{dR}$$ is an isomorphism. This implies that there is a splitting $$\label{sepsplit}
M_{dR} = W_0 M_{dR} \oplus F^1 M_{dR}\ .$$ Equivalently, $h_{p,q}(M) =0$ unless $(p,q)=(0,0)$ or $p,q>0$.

Define a comparison map $c_0^t:M_B^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }(W_0M) \rightarrow M_{dR}^{\vee} \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }(W_0M)$ to be the dual of the composition $c_0$ of the maps $$M_{dR} \longrightarrow M_{dR}/F^1 M_{dR} \cong W_0 M_{dR} \overset{c^{\mathfrak{m} }_{M}}{\longrightarrow} W_0 M_B\otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }(W_0M) \subset M_B \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }(W_0M)$$ where $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }(W_0M)$ is the vector space of motivic periods of $W_0M$. By the previous proposition, these are algebraic motivic periods (see §5.1) in the case where $M$ is an object of the form $(\ref{ExampleHmotive})$. In this case, we obtain a linear map from the motivic periods of $M$ to its de Rham periods: $$[ M, \sigma, \omega]^{\mathfrak{m} } \mapsto [M, c_0^t (\sigma), \omega]^{\mathfrak{dr}} : \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }(M) \longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{dr}}(M) \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \overline{\mathbb Q}\ .$$ If $M$ is of Artin-Tate type (Hodge numbers equal to $(p,p)$ only) and effective, then it is necessarily separated. So, writing $\mathcal{P}^{\bullet,+}_{HT} = \mathcal{P}^{\bullet,+ } \cap \mathcal{P}^{\bullet}_{HT}$ for $\bullet \in \{\mathfrak{m} , \mathfrak{dr}\}$, we obtain a linear map $$\label{pidRmm}  \pi_{\mathfrak{dr}, \mathfrak{m} +} : \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,+}_{HT} \longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{dr},+}_{HT} \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \overline{\mathbb Q} \ .$$

Another way to define $(\ref{pidRmm})$ is by the coaction $$\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,+}_{HT} \overset{\Delta}{\longrightarrow} \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,+}_{HT}\otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{dr},+}_{HT} \longrightarrow\overline{\mathbb Q} \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{dr},+}_{HT}$$ where the second map is the projection of $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,+}_{HT}$ onto its weight $0$ component (recall that it is graded by the weight and has non-negative degrees). Note that the projection map, restricted to the subring of motivic periods of mixed Tate motives, lands in $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{dr},+}$, i.e., without tensoring with $\overline{\mathbb Q}$.

One possible application of the projection map is to prove identities between de Rham periods of mixed Tate motives using the complex period map. One can even deduce identities between $p$-adic periods using complex analysis. The idea is the following. Take a relation $P(\xi_1, \ldots, \xi_n)=0$ between motivic periods in, say $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,+}_{\mathcal{MT}(\mathbb Z)}$ for simplicity. Such a relation can be proved by combining the coaction and complex analysis [@MZVdecomp]. By applying the projection map we deduce a polynomial identity between de Rham periods. Finally, take the $p$-adic period to deduce $P(\xi^{(p)}_1, \ldots, \xi^{(p)}_n)=0$ where $\xi_i^{(p)} =  \mathrm{per}_{p} \pi^{\mathfrak{dr}, \mathfrak{m} +} \xi_i$. This answers a question of Yamashita ([@Yamashita], remark 3.9): the motivic Drinfeld associator $\mathcal{Z}^{\mathfrak{m} }$ defined in [@SVMP] provides a common source for relations between both the complex and $p$-adic multiple zeta values via the period map $\mathrm{per}$ for the former, and via $\mathrm{per}_p \pi^{\mathfrak{dr}, \mathfrak{m} +}$ for the latter. The fact that $\mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{dr}}\neq 0$ but $\pi^{\mathfrak{dr}, \mathfrak{m} +} \mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{m} }=0$ explains the confusing fact that it is sometimes stated that '$2\pi i=0$' and sometimes that '$2 \pi i =1$' in this context.

Stated differently, let $\overline{G}_{MT}^{\mathfrak{m} }$ and $\overline{G}_{MT}^{dR}$ be the affine (monoid) schemes defined by the spectra of $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,+}_{MT}$ and $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{dr}, +}_{MT}\subset \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{dr}}_{\mathcal{MT}}$. Then the projection is a morphism $$G_{MT}^{dR} \longrightarrow\overline{G}_{MT}^{dR} \longrightarrow\overline{G}_{MT}^{\mathfrak{m} }$$ and a Frobenius element $F_p \in  G_{MT}^{dR} (\mathbb Q_p)$ maps to a $\mathbb Q_p$-valued point on $\overline{G}_{MT}^{\mathfrak{m} }$.

# Some basic examples of motivic periods

Before proceeding further with the discussion, we list some very simple examples of motivic periods to illustrate the concepts introduced earlier.

## Algebraic numbers

This is the study of Artin motives ([@DeP1], 1.16) which in principle reduces to Grothendieck's version of Galois theory. However, the point of view of motivic periods leads to some interesting twists on this well-known tale. Let $P\in \mathbb Q[x]$ be an irreducible polynomial, set $F=\mathbb Q[x]/(P)$, and apply example (ex: motivicperiods) with $X= \mathrm{Spec}\, F$, $D=\emptyset$, and $n=0$. The object $H^0(X)$ is (the realization of) an Artin motive. Its de Rham and Betti realizations are $H^0_{dR}(X) = F$, and $H^0_B(X)= H_0(X(\mathbb C);\mathbb Q) ^{\vee}= \mathrm{Hom}(F,\mathbb C)^{\vee}.$ Let $\overline{\mathbb Q}$ denote the algebraic closure of $\mathbb Q$ in $\mathbb C$. Given $\alpha \in \overline{\mathbb Q}$ such that $P(\alpha)=0$, denote by $\sigma_{\alpha}:F \hookrightarrow \mathbb C$ the unique embedding of $F$ such that $\sigma_{\alpha}(x)=\alpha$. Define a *motivic algebraic number* []{#gloss: motivicalgebraicnumber label="gloss: motivicalgebraicnumber"} $$\alpha^{\mathfrak{m} } = [H^0(X), \sigma_{\alpha},x]^{\mathfrak{m} } \in W_0 \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,+}\ .$$ Its period is by definition $\mathrm{per}(\alpha^{\mathfrak{m} }) = \alpha$. The diagonal $X\rightarrow X\times X$ gives rise to a morphism $H^0(X)\otimes H^0(X)\rightarrow  H^0(X)$ in the category $\mathcal{H}$. Using this and the defining relations between matrix coefficients, we deduce that $$f(\alpha^{\mathfrak{m} }) = [ H^0(X), \sigma_{\alpha}, f(x)]^{\mathfrak{m} }$$ for $f = x^n$ and by additivity, for any polynomial $f\in \mathbb Q[x]$. By embedding $\mathbb Q(\alpha), \mathbb Q(\beta)$ into $\mathbb Q(\alpha, \beta)$, we deduce that $\alpha \mapsto \alpha^{\mathfrak{m} } : \overline{\mathbb Q} \rightarrow  \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,+}$ is a homomorphism. It follows that the period map is an isomorphism $$\label{perisomonalg}  \langle \alpha^{\mathfrak{m} }: \alpha \in \overline{\mathbb Q}\rangle_{\mathbb Q} \overset{\mathrm{per}}{\longrightarrow} \overline{\mathbb Q} \subset \mathbb C$$ so we can identify algebraic numbers with their motivic versions. Note that the minimal object $M_{\alpha^{\mathfrak{m} }}$ associated to $\alpha^{\mathfrak{m} }$ is a strict subquotient of $H^0(X)$ whenever $\alpha \notin \mathbb Q$, since it is a factor of $\mathrm{coker}(H^0(\mathrm{Spec} \,\mathbb Q)  \rightarrow H^0(\mathrm{Spec} \,F) )$. The category of Artin motives $AM_{\mathbb Q}$ over $\mathbb Q$ is equivalent to the full tensor subcategory of $\mathcal{H}$ generated by the objects $H^0(X)$, and we could take this as its definition.

It is customary to consider only the Betti Galois group. The absolute Galois group $\mathrm{Gal}(\overline{\mathbb Q}/\mathbb Q)$ acts on the left on $\mathrm{Hom}(F,\mathbb C) = \mathrm{Hom}(F, \overline{\mathbb Q})$ via its action on $\overline{\mathbb Q}$ and gives an automorphism of the Betti fiber functor. Indeed, as is well-known, the Betti Galois group $G_{AM(\mathbb Q)}^B$ is the constant group scheme over $\mathbb Q$ corresponding to $\mathrm{Gal}(\overline{\mathbb Q}/\mathbb Q)$. Therefore the (right) action of $G_{AM_{\mathbb Q}}^{B}(\mathbb Q)$ on motivic algebraic numbers is equivalent to the (left) action of $\mathrm{Gal}(\overline{\mathbb Q}/\mathbb Q)$ on $\overline{\mathbb Q}$ via the isomorphism $(\ref{perisomonalg})$. The action of real Frobenius $F_{\infty}$ on the latter corresponds to complex conjugation on the former. The story usually ends here.

Now consider, somewhat unconventionally, the case of the de Rham Galois group. Its action on $H^0_{dR}(\mathrm{Spec} \,F)$ respects the diagonal map and hence the multiplication on $F$. Furthermore, it preserves $H^0_{dR}(\mathrm{Spec} \,K)$ for all subfields $K \subset F$.

**Definition 28**. Consider the functor $$\mathcal{A}_F(R) = \{ \alpha \in \mathrm{Aut}_R( F\otimes_{\mathbb Q} R) \hbox{ such that } \alpha (K\otimes_{\mathbb Q} R) \subset K\otimes_{\mathbb Q} R \hbox{ for all } K \subset F\}$$ from commutative $\mathbb Q$-algebras $R$ to groups. Define the *group of field automorphisms* to be the projective limit over all field extensions $F/\mathbb Q$ of finite type $$\mathcal{A}_{\overline{\mathbb Q}} = \varprojlim_{F} \mathcal{A}_F\ .$$

It follows from the fact that all algebraic relations between $\alpha^{\mathfrak{m} }$ are induced by linearity, inclusions of fields $K \subset F$ and diagonals $\mathrm{Spec} \,F \rightarrow \mathrm{Spec} \,F \times \mathrm{Spec} \,F$ that $$G^{dR}_{AM(\mathbb Q)} \cong \mathcal{A}_{\overline{\mathbb Q}}$$ (which shows in particular that the right-hand side is representable: its affine ring is generated by matrix coefficients $[H^0(\mathrm{Spec}\, F), f,v]^{\mathfrak{dr}}$, where $f\in F^{\vee}$ and $v\in F$). The comparison isomorphism implies that $$G^B_{AM(\mathbb Q)} \times \mathbb C\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow} G_{AM(\mathbb Q)}^{dR} \times \mathbb C$$ so the usual absolute Galois group can be retrieved as the complex points (or $\overline{\mathbb Q}$-points) of the de Rham Galois group.

Now consider a motivic algebraic number $\alpha^{\mathfrak{m} }$, for $\alpha \in \overline{\mathbb Q}$. The *degree* of $\alpha$ can be retrieved as the number of connected components of $G^{\bullet}_{\alpha^{\mathfrak{m} }}$ for $\bullet = B,dR$. Its *minimal object* $M(\alpha^{\mathfrak{m} })$ is an object of $AM_{\mathbb Q}$. Its periods generate the Galois closure $F'$ of $\mathbb Q(\alpha)$. Its de Rham group scheme is $G^{dR}_{\alpha^{\mathfrak{m} }} = \mathcal{A}_{\mathbb Q(\alpha)}$, and its Betti group $G^{B}_{\alpha^{\mathfrak{m} }}$ is the constant group scheme of $\mathrm{Gal}(F'/\mathbb Q)$. The quantity $\mathrm{rank}( \alpha^{\mathfrak{m} }) = \dim_{\mathbb Q} M_B(\alpha^{\mathfrak{m} })$ is the dimension of the $\mathbb Q$-vector space spanned by the Galois conjugates of $\alpha$ over $\mathbb Q$. This is called the *conjugate dimension* of $\alpha$ and, surprisingly, was introduced only very recently (see [@conjdim] and references therein).

Note that although the Betti orbit $G^{B}(\mathbb Q)\alpha^{\mathfrak{m} }$ of $\alpha^{\mathfrak{m} }$ corresponds the usual notion of Galois conjugates of $\alpha$, the de Rham orbit $G^{dR} (R) \alpha^{\mathfrak{m} }$ is sensitive to $R$.

## Motivic $2 \pi i$ (Lefschetz motive)

Let $X= \mathbb P^1 \backslash \{0,\infty\}$, $D=\emptyset$. Consider $H^1(X) = \mathbb Q(-1)$ in example $\ref{ex: motivicperiods}$. Its de Rham version is $H^1_{dR}(X;\mathbb Q) = \mathbb Q[{dx\over x}]$ and its Betti version is $H_1(X(\mathbb C)) = \mathbb Q[\gamma_0]$ where $\gamma_0$ is a small loop winding around $0$ in the positive direction. Define the Lefschetz motivic period[^7] $$\mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{m} } = [H^1(X), [\gamma_0],  [{dx/x}]]^{\mathfrak{m} } \quad \in \quad  W_2\, P^{\mathfrak{m} ,+}\ .$$ It satisfies $F_{\infty}\mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{m} }=-\mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{m} }$. By Cauchy's theorem, its period is $$\mathrm{per}(\mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{m} }) = \int_{\gamma_0} {dx \over x} = 2 \pi i\ .$$ It is the 'motivic version' of $2\pi i$. Since $H^1_{dR}(X)$ is a one-dimensional representation of $G^{dR}$, we obtain a homomorphism of affine group schemes $$\lambda: G^{dR} \longrightarrow\mathbb{G}_m\ .$$ Thus the group $G^{dR}(\mathbb Q)$ acts upon $\mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{m} }$ by multiplication $$g (\mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{m} })  = \lambda(g) \mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{m} }\ ,$$ where $\lambda(g) \in \mathbb Q^{\times}$. The character $\lambda_g$ is non-trivial: if $H^1_{dR}(X)$ were the trivial representation, then by theorem 4, $H^1(X)$ would be equivalent to the trivial object $\mathbb Q(0)=H^0(pt)$ which has rational periods. Since $$\mathrm{per}(\mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{m} }) = 2  \pi i  \notin \mathbb Q$$ is irrational, we conclude that $\lambda$ is non-trivial (this also follows from the fact that the Hodge structure on $H^1(X)$ is $\mathbb Q(-1)$ which is pure of weight 2). It follows that $\mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{m} }$ is transcendental: if there were a polynomial $P \in \mathbb Q[x]$ such that $P(\mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{m} }) = 0$, then every conjugate $\lambda(g) \mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{m} }$ would also be a root of $P$. Since a non-zero polynomial has only finitely many roots, it would follow that $P=0$.

On the other hand, it is convenient to define the de Rham version of $\mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{m} }$, denoted $\mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{dr}}\in \mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{H} }^{\mathfrak{dr}} = \mathcal{O}(G^{dR})$, to be the matrix coefficient $$\mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{dr}} = [H^1(X) ,  [dx/x]^{\vee} ,[dx/x] ]^{\mathfrak{dr}} \ .$$ The coaction $\Delta (\mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{m} } ) = \mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{m} } \otimes  \mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{dr}}$ is given by application of $(\ref{eqn: coaction})$, and it follows that $g(\mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{dr}}) = \lambda(g) \mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{dr}}$, and $\mathrm{ev}(g(\mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{dr}}))= \lambda(g)$, since $\mathrm{ev}(\mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{dr}})=1$.

## Motivic logarithms (Kummer motive)

Let $X= \mathbb P^1\backslash \{0,\infty\}$ and $D= \{1, \alpha\}$ for some $1< \alpha \in \mathbb Q$. Consider the object in $\mathcal{H}$ known as a Kummer motive $$K_{\alpha}= H^1(\mathbb P^1 \backslash \{0,\infty\}, \{1,\alpha\})\ .$$ It sits in an exact sequence $0 \rightarrow \mathbb Q(0) \rightarrow K_{\alpha} \rightarrow  H^1(X) \rightarrow 0$. A basis for the de Rham cohomology $(K_{\alpha})_{dR}$ is given by the relative cohomology classes of the forms ${dx \over x}$ and ${dx \over \alpha-1}$, which vanish along $D$. Let $\gamma_0$ be as in §5.2, and $\gamma_1$ denote the interval $[1,\alpha]\subset X(\mathbb R)$. Their boundaries are contained in $D(\mathbb C)$, and they form a basis for $(K_{\alpha})^{\vee}_B$. The comparison isomorphism is represented by the matrix $$\label{eqn: periodmatrixforlog}
\left(
\begin{array}{cc}
\int_{\gamma_1} {dx \over \alpha-1}  & \int_{\gamma_1} {dx \over x}        \\
  \int_{\gamma_0} {dx\over \alpha-1 }  &  \int_{\gamma_0} {dx \over x}
\end{array}\right) = \left(
\begin{array}{cc}
 1   & \log(\alpha)     \\
 0 & 2\pi i  \end{array}\right)$$ with respect to this choice of basis. Define the motivic logarithm to be $$\log^{\mathfrak{m} }(\alpha) = [K_{\alpha}, [\gamma_1], [ \textstyle{dx\over x}]]^{\mathfrak{m} } \in W_2 \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,+}\ .$$ Its period is $\mathrm{per}(\log^{\mathfrak{m} }(\alpha)) = \log(\alpha)$, and $F_{\infty} \log^{\mathfrak{m} } \alpha = \log^{\mathfrak{m} } \alpha$. The group $G^{dR}$ acts on $(K_{\alpha})_{dR}$, fixing the subspace $\mathbb Q(0)_{dR}$ and acting on the quotient $H_{dR}^1(X)=\mathbb Q(-1)_{dR}$ via $\lambda_g$ as in the previous example. Thus we have a homomorphism $$(\nu_{\alpha}, \lambda): G^{dR} \longrightarrow\mathbb{G} _a \rtimes \mathbb{G} _m\ .$$ Equivalently, the de Rham action is given for $g\in G^{dR}(\mathbb Q)$ by $$\label{eqn: Gactiononlog}
g \log^{\mathfrak{m} }(\alpha) = \lambda(g) \log^{\mathfrak{m} }(\alpha) + \nu_{\alpha}(g) \ .$$ For illustration, we can prove the functional equation of the motivic logarithm as follows. Let $1<\beta \in \mathbb Q$, and consider the morphisms of pairs of spaces $$\begin{aligned}
 (\mathbb{G} _m,\{1,\alpha\}) \overset{\times \beta}{\longrightarrow} (\mathbb{G} _m, \{\beta, \alpha\beta\}) &  \subseteq  & (\mathbb{G} _m, \{1, \beta, \alpha\beta\})  \nonumber \\
 \quad (\mathbb{G} _m, \{1, z\}) &\subseteq &  (\mathbb{G} _m, \{1, \beta, \alpha\beta\}) \quad  \hbox{ for }  z\in \{\beta, \alpha\beta\} \ . \nonumber

\end{aligned}$$ Since ${dx \over x}$ is invariant under multiplication, these give relations $$\begin{aligned}
\log^{\mathfrak{m} }(\alpha)  &=  & [ H^1 (\mathbb{G} _m, \{1, \beta, \alpha\beta\}),  [\beta, \alpha\beta], [\textstyle{{dx \over x}}]]^{\mathfrak{m} } \nonumber \\
\log^{\mathfrak{m} }(z)  & =  & [ H^1 (\mathbb{G} _m, \{1, \beta, \alpha\beta\}), [1, z],  [\textstyle{{dx \over x}}]]^{\mathfrak{m} } \quad  \hbox{ for }  z\in \{\beta, \alpha\beta\}\ . \nonumber
\end{aligned}$$ Finally use additivity with respect to Betti classes $[1, \alpha\beta] = [1, \beta]+ [\beta, \alpha\beta]$ to obtain the expected relation between the three motivic periods $$\log^{\mathfrak{m} } (\alpha\beta) = \log^{\mathfrak{m} }(\alpha) + \log^{\mathfrak{m} }(\beta)\ .$$ It follows that the motivic logarithms over $\mathbb Q$ are linear combinations of the motivic periods $\log^{\mathfrak{m} }(p)$ for $p\geq 2$ prime. Since $\log : \mathbb R_{>0} \rightarrow \mathbb R$ has a unique zero at $x=1$, the functional equation of the logarithm implies that the numbers $\log(p)$ are linearly independent over $\mathbb Q$, and *a fortiori* the $\log^{\mathfrak{m} }(p)$. By $(\ref{eqn: Gactiononlog})$ we have $$\Delta^u \log^{\mathfrak{m} }(p) = \log^{\mathfrak{m} }(p)\otimes 1 + 1 \otimes \nu_p$$ where $\nu_p$ is viewed in $\mathcal{O}(U^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} })$. We deduce that the decomposition map satisfies $$\Phi ( \log^{\mathfrak{m} }(p)) = 1 \otimes \nu_p   \qquad \in \qquad \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} ^{ss}} \otimes_{\mathbb Q} H^1(U^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} })\ ,$$ and the $\nu_p$ are independent, since $\Phi$ is injective. Since it is a homomorphism, $$\Phi\big( (\mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{m} })^k \prod_i  \log^{\mathfrak{m} }(p_i)^{n_i} \big)    = (\mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{m} })^k \otimes \prod_i (\nu_{p_i})^{\, \hbox{\rus x} \,n_i}$$ where the products on the right-hand side are with respect to the shuffle product, $p_i$ are a finite set of primes, and $n_i \geq 0$.

**Corollary 29**. *Since $\Phi$ is injective, the set of elements $\{\mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{m} },   \log^{\mathfrak{m} }(p)  \hbox{ for } p \hbox{ prime}\}$ are algebraically independent over $\mathbb Q$.*

This completes the description of all algebraic relations between the motivic periods $\log^{\mathfrak{m} } (\alpha)$, for $\alpha \in \mathbb Q$, and $\mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{m} }$.

### Single-valued versions

Define the de Rham version $$\log^{\mathfrak{dr}}(\alpha) =   [K_{\alpha}, [\textstyle{dx\over \alpha-1}]^{\vee},[ \textstyle{dx\over x}] ]^{\mathfrak{dr}} \in W_2 \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{dr}}\ ,$$ where $[{dx\over \alpha-1}]^{\vee} \in  (K_{\alpha})_{dR}^{\vee}$ takes the value $0$ on $[ \textstyle{dx\over x}]$ and $1$ on $[{dx\over \alpha-1}]$. It is precisely $\nu_{\alpha} \in \mathcal{O}(G^{dR})$, and the coaction formula $(\ref{eqn: coaction})$ gives $$\Delta \log^{\mathfrak{m} }(\alpha) = \log^{\mathfrak{m} }(\alpha) \otimes  \mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{dr}}   +   1^{\mathfrak{m} }\otimes  \log^{\mathfrak{dr}}(\alpha) \ ,$$ which is equivalent to $(\ref{eqn: Gactiononlog})$. It follows from the computations above that $\log^{\mathfrak{dr}}(p)$ for $p$ prime are also algebraically independent over $\mathbb Q$ (use the de Rham version of $\Phi$). The motivic period matrix associated to $\log^{\mathfrak{m} } \alpha$ is $$\label{logmotivicperiodmatrix} C^{\mathfrak{m} } =
\left(
\begin{array}{cc}
 1   & \log^{\mathfrak{m} }(\alpha)     \\
 0 & \mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{m} } \end{array}\right) \ .$$ The real Frobenius $F_{\infty}$ acts by $-1$ on the second row. Therefore $$(F_{\infty} C^{\mathfrak{m} })^{-1} C^{\mathfrak{m} } =
\left(
\begin{array}{cc}
 1   & 2 \log^{\mathfrak{m} }(\alpha)      \\
 0 &  -1 \end{array}\right)$$ and we deduce that $\mathbf{s}^{\mathfrak{m} } (\mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{dr}}) = -1$ and $\mathbf{s}^{\mathfrak{m} } (\log^{\mathfrak{dr}}(\alpha))= (1+ F_{\infty}) \log^{\mathfrak{m} }(\alpha) =2 \log^{\mathfrak{m} }(\alpha)$.

## Motivic multiple zeta values

Iterated integrals on the punctured projective line provide a class of motivic periods for which one knows how to compute the motivic coaction. This is most developed in the case of multiple zeta values. For any $n_1,\ldots, n_{r-1}\geq1$ and $n_r\geq 2$, there are motivic multiple zeta values $$\label{mmzv}
\zeta^{\mathfrak{m} }(n_1,\ldots, n_r) \in \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{HT} \cap\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,+}  \subset \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,+}$$ of weight $2n_1+ \ldots +2n_r$ (recall $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,+}_{HT}$ is graded by $W$) whose periods are $$\mathrm{per}(\zeta^{\mathfrak{m} }(n_1,\ldots, n_r)) = \zeta(n_1,\ldots, n_r) = \sum_{1\leq k_1 <\ldots  < k_r} {1 \over k_1^{n_1} \ldots k_r^{n_r}}\ .$$ They are defined as follows. Let $X= \mathbb P^1 \backslash \{0,1,\infty\}$ and set $$\zeta^{ \mathfrak{m}}(n_1,\ldots, n_r) = [ \mathcal{O}(\pi_1^{\mathfrak{m} }(X, \overset{\rightarrow}{1}\!_0, -\overset{\rightarrow}{1}\!_1),    \mathrm{dch} , w ]^{\mathfrak{m} }$$ where $\pi_1^{\mathfrak{m} }(X)$ is the motivic torsor of paths on $X$ [@DeGo] from the unit tangent vector at $0$ to minus the unit tangent vector at $1$, $w$ is the word $w = e_0^{n_1-1} e_1 \ldots e_0^{n_r-1} e_1$ in $e_0={dx \over x}$, and $e_1 = {dx \over 1-x}$, and $\mathrm{dch}$ is the Betti image of the straight line path from $0$ to $1$. For further details, see [@BrICM]. This actually defines the motivic multiple zeta values as motivic periods of the category $\mathcal{MT}(\mathbb Z)$ of mixed Tate motives over $\mathbb Z$. The latter admits a fully faithful functor to the category $\mathcal{H}$ [@DeGo], and so the ring of periods $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{MT}(\mathbb Z)}$ injects into $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }$ and we can identify it with its image. Furthermore, Beilinson's construction of the motivic torsor of path given in [@DeGo] can be realised in the form of example $\ref{ex: motivicperiods}$, so we can also view the images of the $\zeta^{ \mathfrak{m}}(n_1,\ldots, n_r) \in \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }$ as elements of $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,+}$ as claimed above.

The depth of $(\ref{mmzv})$ is defined to be $r$. The fact that the depth filtration is motivic implies the following bound for the unipotency degree $$\hbox{u. d.}( \zeta^{\mathfrak{m} }(n_1,\ldots, n_r)) \leq r   \   .$$ The unipotency degree has sometimes been referred to as the 'motivic depth'. A fascinating feature of multiple zeta values is the existence of a discrepancy between the unipotency degree and the depth, related to modular forms for $\mathrm{SL}_2(\mathbb Z)$.

The simplest examples are the motivic zeta values, $\zeta^{ \mathfrak{m}}(2n+1) \in C^1 \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,+}$, for $n\geq 1$, which admit a motivic period matrix of the expected form $$\left(
\begin{array}{cc}
 1   & \zeta^{ \mathfrak{m}}(2n+1)     \\
 0 & (\mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{m} })^{2n+1} \end{array}\right) \ $$ Let us define some symbols $f_{2n+1} \in H^1(U^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} })$, for $n \geq 1$, as the images of the motivic zeta values under the decomposition map $$\Phi (\zeta^{ \mathfrak{m}}(2n+1)) = 1\otimes f_{2n+1}$$ Each $f_{2n+1}$, for $n\geq 1$ spans a copy of $\mathbb Q(-2n-1)$ and has weight $4n+2$. The interpretation of these elements will be explained in §6 below. Either from the explicit formula for the coaction on motivic multiple zeta values, or from the results of §6, the decomposition map gives an injective homomorphism $$\Phi: \mathrm{gr}^C \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,+}_{\mathcal{MT}(\mathbb Z)} \longrightarrow\mathbb Q[ \mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{m} }] \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathbb Q\langle f_3, f_5,\ldots  \rangle$$ where the right-hand side denotes the shuffle algebra (tensor coalgebra) on symbols $f_{2n+1}$ over $\mathbb Q$. In this case, it is in fact known that $\Phi$ is an isomorphism (theorem 32). Now, the main result of [@BrMTZ] is a computation of the image under $\Phi$ of the elements $\zeta^{ \mathfrak{m}}(n_1,\ldots, n_r)$ where $n_i \in \{2,3\}$, and a proof that their images are linearly independent.[^8] Thus we can use these elements to split the coradical filtration $C$ on $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{MT}(\mathbb Z)}$, and deduce the existence of a canonical isomorphism [@MZVdecomp] $$\phi \quad : \quad  \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,+}_{\mathcal{MT}(\mathbb Z)} \cong \mathrm{gr}^C \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,+}_{\mathcal{MT}(\mathbb Z)}  \overset{\Phi}{\longrightarrow} \mathbb Q[ \mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{m} }] \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathbb Q\langle f_3, f_5,\ldots  \rangle\ .$$ One could use a different splitting of the coradical filtration $C$, which would lead to a different choice of isomorphism $\phi$.

It is hard to understand Galois aspects of multiple zeta values without some sort of model of this kind. Indeed, using this model we can easily write down the invariants defined earlier. If $\xi \in \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,+}_{\mathcal{MT}(\mathbb Z)}$ corresponds to $(\mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{m} })^{k} f_{a_1} f_{a_2} \ldots f_{a_r}$ under $\phi$, then the representation generated by $\xi$ is the vector space $$M(\xi)_{dR} = \langle  \ell^{k} f_{a_1} \ldots f_{a_i}:  \hbox{ for } 0\leq i \leq r \rangle_{\mathbb Q}\ $$ obtained by slicing off letters from the right. A representative for the period matrix for $\xi = (\mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{m} })^{k} f_{a_1} f_{a_2}$ is $$\mathrm{per}\left(
\begin{array}{ccc}
      (\mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{m} })^{k}   &  (\mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{m} })^{k} f_{a_1} &     (\mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{m} })^{k} f_{a_1} f_{a_2}     \\
0 &    (\mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{m} })^{k+a_1}   &     (\mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{m} })^{k +a_1} f_{a_2}     \\
0 & 0 &  (\mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{m} })^{k +a_1+ a_2}           \end{array}
\right)$$ which means the top left-hand entry is $\mathrm{per}((\mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{m} })^k) = (2 \pi i)^k$, and so on. The general pattern is clear from this example.

Applying the projection map $\pi$ to motivic multiple zeta values leads to *de Rham* multiple zeta values $\zeta^{\mathfrak{dr}}(n_1,\ldots, n_r) = \pi^{\mathfrak{dr},\mathfrak{m} +} \zeta^{ \mathfrak{m}}(n_1,\ldots, n_r)$. It is proved in [@BrMTZ] that the kernel of $\pi_{\mathfrak{dr},\mathfrak{m} +}$ on the ring generated by motivic multiple zeta values is the ideal generated by $\zeta^{ \mathfrak{m}}(2)$. Thus de Rham multiple zeta values are motivic MZV's modulo $\zeta^{ \mathfrak{m}}(2)$. The former have single-valued periods, and a calculation using the period matrix for $\zeta^{ \mathfrak{m}}(2n+1)$ similar to the one for the logarithm gives the single-valued versions $\mathbf{s}^{\mathfrak{m} }(\zeta^{\mathfrak{dr}}(2n+1)) = 2 \zeta^{ \mathfrak{m}}(2n+1)$. The de Rham versions of multiple zeta values also have $p$-adic periods, which can be thought of as follows. There are canonical Frobenius elements [@Yamashita] $$F_p \in G^{dR}_{\mathcal{MT}(\mathbb Z)}(\mathbb Q_p)\ ,$$ and hence homomorphisms $\mathrm{per}_p : \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{dr}}_{\mathcal{MT}(\mathbb Z)} = \mathcal{O}(G^{dR}_{\mathcal{MT}(Z)}) \rightarrow \mathbb Q_p$. The projection map enables us to associate $p$-adic periods to motivic multiple zeta values, which are a certain kind of $p$-adic multiple zeta values.[^9]

## Motivic Euler sums

Euler sums are defined by the nested sums $$\zeta(n_1,\ldots, n_r) = \sum_{1 \leq k_1 < \ldots<  k_r}  { \mathrm{sign}(n_1)^{k_1} \ldots \mathrm{sign}(n_r)^{k_r}  \over k_1^{|n_1|} \ldots k_r^{|n_r|} }$$ where $n_i \in \mathbb Z\backslash \{0\}$ and $n_r\neq 1$. Their depth is defined to be the quantity $r$. They can be written as iterated integrals on $X= \mathbb P^1\backslash \{0,\pm 1, \infty\}$ from $0$ to $1$, which leads to a definition of motivic Euler sums $$\zeta^{ \mathfrak{m}}(w) = [\mathcal{O}(\pi_1^{\mathfrak{m} }(X, \overset{\rightarrow}{1}\!_0, -\overset{\rightarrow}{1}\!_1),  \mathrm{dch}, w]^{\mathfrak{m} }$$ where $w$ is a certain word in $e_0= {dx\over x}$ and $e_{\pm 1} = {dx \over x \pm 1}$, and $\mathrm{dch}$ is as above. These are motivic periods of the category $\mathcal{MT}(\mathbb Z[ {1 \over 2}])$ of mixed Tate motives ramified at $2$. The decomposition map now provides an injective homomorphism $$\Phi: \mathrm{gr}^C \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} , +}_{\mathcal{MT}(\mathbb Z[{1\over 2}])} \longrightarrow\mathbb Q[\mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{m} }] \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathbb Q\langle \nu_2, f_3, f_5, \ldots \rangle \ ,$$ where $\nu_2$, corresponding to the logarithm of $2$, was defined earlier. It is an isomorphism by theorem 32. The results of Deligne [@DeN] can be translated into this setting. He proves that $\zeta^{ \mathfrak{m}}(n_1,\ldots, n_{r-1}, -n_r)$ where the $n_i$ are odd $\geq 1$ and form a Lyndon word, are algebraically independent. An important difference with the case of multiple zeta values, which considerably simplifies matters, is that the depth filtration in this case coincides with the unipotency filtration. We can construct a splitting of the coradical filtration using this basis and hence an isomorphism $$\phi^{(2)} :\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} , +}_{\mathcal{MT}(\mathbb Z[{1\over 2}])} \cong  \mathrm{gr}^C \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} , +}_{\mathcal{MT}(\mathbb Z[{1\over 2}])}  \overset{\Phi}{\longrightarrow} \mathbb Q[\mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{m} }] \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathbb Q\langle \nu_2, f_3, f_5, \ldots \rangle \ .$$ The periods of $\mathcal{MT}(\mathbb Z)$ correspond to elements with no $\nu_2$ in their $\phi$-image. Note, however, that the maps $\phi$ and $\phi^{(2)}$ are not compatible. To remedy this, one could replace $\phi$ with the restriction of $\phi^{(2)}$ on $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,+}_{\mathcal{MT}(\mathbb Z)} \subset \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,+}_{\mathcal{MT}(\mathbb Z[{1 \over 2}])}$, but this does not quite lead to an explicit basis for the periods of $\mathcal{MT}(\mathbb Z)$. These ideas are studied in Glanois' thesis [@Glanois], who also constructed a new basis for the motivic periods of $\mathcal{MT}(\mathbb Z)$ using certain modified Euler sums where the summation involves non-strict inequalities, weighted with certain powers of $2$.

# Towards a classification of motivic periods

We can use the decomposition into primitives to classify $\mathcal{H}$-periods up to elements of lower unipotency degree. In this section, we shall drop the superscript $dR$ and subscript $\mathcal{H}$ and write $S, U, G$ instead of $S^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} }$, $U^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} }$, $G^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} }.$

The decomposition map involves a space $\mathrm{gr}^C_1(\mathcal{O}(U))$, which is exactly $$\mathrm{Prim} (\mathcal{O}(U)) := \{f \in \mathcal{O}(U): \Delta f = f \otimes 1 + 1 \otimes f \}$$ In this paragraph we analyse this space in some detail, which leads to further invariants of motivic periods, and a first step towards their classification.

## Cohomology of $U$

The exact sequence $(\ref{UHexactsequence})$ will now be written $$1 \longrightarrow U \longrightarrow G \longrightarrow S \longrightarrow 1\ .$$

**Proposition 30**. *Let $n \geq 0$. There is an isomorphism of (right) $S$-modules $$H^n (U) \cong \bigoplus_{M \in \mathrm{Irr}(\mathcal{H} ^{ss})} \mathrm{Ext}^n_{\mathcal{H} }(\mathbb Q, M) \otimes_{\mathrm{End}(M)} M^{\vee}_{dR}\ ,$$ where $\mathrm{Irr}(\mathcal{H} ^{ss})$ denotes a set of representatives of isomorphism classes of simple objects in $\mathcal{H} ^{ss}$ (or equivalently, of irreducible $\mathcal{O}(S)$-comodules).*

*Proof.* First of all, we can write $U= \varprojlim U_n$ as a projective limit of unipotent affine group schemes $U_n$ of finite type (unipotent algebraic matrix groups). Likewise, a representation $V$ of $U$ is an inductive limit $V= \varinjlim V_n$ of $U_n$-representations. Since $\varinjlim  H^i(U_n, V_n)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow } H^i( U, V)$, the arguments which follow can be deduced from well-known results for matrix groups and by taking limits.

Let $M$ be an irreducible object of $\mathcal{H} ^{ss}$. Then $M_{dR}$ is an irreducible $\mathcal{O}(S)$-comodule. A Hochschild-Serre spectral sequence gives $$H^p(S, H^q(U,M_{dR})) \Rightarrow H^{p+q} (G, M_{dR})$$ and one knows that $S$ is of cohomological dimension $0$, since it is pro-reductive. Therefore since $U$ acts trivially on $M_{dR}$, $$H^0(S, H^n(U,M_{dR})) =  H^n (U, M_{dR}) ^{S} \cong ( H^n(U) \otimes_{\mathbb Q} M_{dR})^{S}   \ ,$$ where $H^n(U)$ denotes $H^n(U;\mathbb Q)$, and we deduce that $$\big(H^n (U) \otimes_{\mathbb Q} M_{dR}\big)^S \cong H^n (G, M_{dR})\ .$$ Let $M, N$ be irreducible $S$-modules. Then $(N_{dR}^{\vee} \otimes_{\mathbb Q}  M_{dR})^S = \mathrm{End}_S(M_{dR})$ if $N$ and $M$ are isomorphic, and zero otherwise, by Schur's lemma. It follows that $$H^n( U) \cong \bigoplus_{M \in \mathrm{Irr}(\mathcal{H} ^{ss})} H^n(G,M_{dR})   \otimes_{\mathrm{End}_S(M_{dR})  } M^{\vee}_{dR}\ .$$ Note that since $U$ acts trivially on $M_{dR}$, we have $\mathrm{End}_G(M_{dR})= \mathrm{End}_S(M_{dR})$. Since $\omega_{dR}: \mathcal{H} \rightarrow \mathrm{Rep}(G)$ is an equivalence, we deduce that $$H^n(G, M_{dR})= \mathrm{Ext}^n_{\mathrm{Rep}(G)} (\mathbb Q, M_{dR}) = \mathrm{Ext}^n_{\mathcal{H} }(\mathbb Q(0), M)\ ,$$ and $\mathrm{End}_S(M_{dR})= \mathrm{End}(M)$. ◻

It is a well-known fact due to Beilinson that $$\mathrm{Ext}^n_{\mathcal{H} }(\mathbb Q, M) =0 \qquad \hbox{ for } n \geq 2\ .$$

**Corollary 31**. *The cohomology $H^n(U;M)$ vanishes for all $n \geq 2$.*

Recall that $$H^1(U) \cong  \mathrm{gr}^C_1 \mathcal{O}(U)\cong C^1 \mathcal{O}(U)_+   \cong \mathrm{Prim} (\mathcal{O}(U)) \ .$$

**Theorem 32**. *The decomposition map $$\Phi: \mathrm{gr}^C_{\bullet}  \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} } \longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} ^{ss}} \otimes_{\mathbb Q} T^c( \mathrm{gr}^C_1 \mathcal{O}(U) )$$ is an isomorphism of $S$-modules.*

*Proof.* By proposition $\ref{lemPemtensor}$, there is a non-canonical isomorphism $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} } \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \overline{\mathbb Q} \cong  \mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{H} ^{ss}}^{\mathfrak{m} }   \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathcal{O}(U) \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \overline{\mathbb Q}.$ The group $U$ is of cohomological dimension $1$, by the previous corollary. Now apply corollary $\ref{cordecompisom}$ with $T=  \mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{H} ^{ss}}^{\mathfrak{m} } \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \overline{\mathbb Q}$ to conclude that the decomposition map, after extending scalars to $\overline{\mathbb Q}$, is an isomorphism. Since it was already injective over $\mathbb Q$, it follows that it is surjective over $\mathbb Q$. ◻

*Remark 33*. One can view $T^c(H^1(U) )$ as the associated graded, for the length filtration, of $H^0(\mathbb{B}(N))$ where $N$ is a DGA which computes the cohomology of $U$, and $\mathbb{B}$ is the (reduced) bar construction. The decomposition map $\Phi$ therefore resembles the bar construction of a fibration, and suggests thinking about elements of $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} ^{ss}}$ as functions on a 'base' corresponding to $S$, and $T^c( H^1(U) )$ as iterated integrals on a 'fiber' corresponding to $U$. From this point of view, $\delta$ can be thought of as a kind of Gauss-Manin connection. If one wants to copy this setup for mixed Tate motives over number fields rather than mixed Hodge structures, this suggests replacing $N$ with Bloch's cycle complex $\mathcal{N}$ and echoes the construction of [@Bloch-Kriz].

## Primitives in $\mathcal{O}(U)$

We analyse the statement of proposition $\ref{propcohomU}$ in more detail in the case $n=1$. It gives an isomorphism of $S$-modules $$\label{primtoext}
 \mathrm{Prim}(\mathcal{O}(U)) \overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}  \bigoplus_{M \in \mathrm{Irr}(\mathcal{H} ^{ss})}\mathrm{Ext}^1_{\mathcal{H} } (\mathbb Q(0), M^{\vee}) \otimes_{\mathrm{End}(M)} M_{dR}\ .$$ First of all, observe from remark $\ref{remunipmonod}$. that $$\mathrm{Prim}(\mathcal{O}(U^{ab})) \overset{\sim}{\rightarrow} \mathrm{Prim} (\mathcal{O}(U))\ .$$ The action of $S$ by conjugation on $U^{ab}$ induces an action of $S$ on $\mathcal{O}(U^{ab})$, and preserves the space of primitive elements. Since $S$ is the (de Rham) Tannaka group of $\mathcal{H} ^{ss}$, the $S$-module generated by any element $f\in \mathrm{Prim}(\mathcal{O}(U))$ defines a representation of $S$, and hence an object of $\mathcal{H} ^{ss}$ by theorem $\ref{thm:  Tannaka}$.

**Definition 34**. For any $f\in \mathrm{Prim}(\mathcal{O}(U))$, let $M_f$ denote the associated object of $\mathcal{H} ^{ss}$. Its de Rham vector space is the $\mathcal{O}(S)$-comodule generated by $f$. It comes equipped with a distinguished element $f\in (M_f)_{dR}$.

Let $f\in \mathrm{Prim}(\mathcal{O}(U))$. One associates an extension to $f$ as follows. Consider the short exact sequence of right $U$-modules $$0 \longrightarrow C_0 \mathcal{O}(U) \longrightarrow C_1 \mathcal{O}(U) \longrightarrow\mathrm{Prim}(\mathcal{O}(U)) \longrightarrow 0 \ .$$ It is not split, although the underlying sequence of $\mathbb Q$-vector spaces is split by the augmentation map. By remark $\ref{remunipmonod}$, it can be rewritten $$0 \longrightarrow\mathbb Q\longrightarrow C_1 \mathcal{O}(U^{ab}) \longrightarrow\mathrm{Prim}(\mathcal{O}(U^{ab})) \longrightarrow 0 \ .$$ It is an exact sequence in the category of right $U^{ab} \rtimes S$-modules. We can pull back this extension along the inclusion $(M_f )_{dR} \subseteq \mathrm{Prim}(\mathcal{O}(U^{ab}))$ to obtain $$0 \longrightarrow\mathbb Q\longrightarrow\mathcal{E}_{dR} \longrightarrow(M_f)_{dR} \longrightarrow 0\ .$$ Now choose an isomorphism $G/[U,U] \rightarrow U^{ab} \rtimes S$, i.e., a splitting of $1 \rightarrow U^{ab} \rightarrow G / [U,U] \rightarrow S \rightarrow 1$. It exists by Levi's (Mostow's) theorem. Via this isomorphism, the previous exact sequence can be viewed in the category of $G$-modules, and hence, via the Tannaka theorem, as an exact sequence in $\mathcal{H}$: $$\label{extclassdef}
0 \longrightarrow\mathbb Q\longrightarrow\mathcal{E} \longrightarrow M_f \longrightarrow 0\ .$$ Another choice of isomorphism $G/[U,U] \cong U^{ab} \rtimes S$ yields an isomorphic extension. The dual extension $0 \rightarrow M_f^{\vee}  \rightarrow\mathcal{E}^{\vee} \rightarrow \mathbb Q\rightarrow 0$, together with the vector $f \in (M_f)_{dR}$, defines a class $$[\mathcal{E}^{\vee}] \otimes f  \quad \in \quad  \mathrm{Ext}^1_{\mathcal{H} }(\mathbb Q, M_f^{\vee}) \otimes_{\mathbb Q}  (M_f)_{dR}$$ as required. By decomposing $M_f$ into $S$-isotypical components, we can project this element into the right-hand side of $(\ref{primtoext})$.

**Definition 35**. Let us denote the extension class of $(\ref{extclassdef})$ by $\mathcal{E}_f$.

In the other direction, consider an extension in $\mathcal{H}$ $$0 \longrightarrow M^{\vee} \longrightarrow\mathcal{E} \longrightarrow\mathbb Q\longrightarrow 0\ ,$$ and a vector $v\in M_{dR}$, where $M$ is a simple object. Choose a lift of the element $1 \in \mathbb Q_{dR}$ to $f \in \mathcal{E}_{dR}$, and a lift of $v$ to $\widetilde{v} \in \mathcal{E}_{dR}^{\vee}$ along the map $\mathcal{E}_{dR}^{\vee} \rightarrow M_{dR}$. The image of the following unipotent matrix coefficient (§3.7) in $\mathcal{O}(U)_+$ $$\xi = [ \mathcal{E}, \widetilde{v}, f]^{\mathfrak{u}} \in \mathcal{O}(U)_+$$ does not depend on the choices of $\widetilde{v}, f$. For instance, if $f'$ is another lift of $1$, then $f-f' \in M^{\vee}_{dR}$, and $[ \mathcal{E}, \widetilde{v}, f -f']^{\mathfrak{u}}$ is equivalent to $[M^{\vee}, v, f-f']^{\mathfrak{u}}$, which is constant because $U$ acts trivially on $M_{dR}$. Similarly, if $\widetilde{v}'$ is a lift of $v$ then $\widetilde{v}'-\widetilde{v} \in \mathbb Q_{dR}$ and $[ \mathcal{E}, \widetilde{v}'-\widetilde{v},f]^{\mathfrak{u}}$ is equivalent to a unipotent period of $\mathbb Q(0)$, hence constant. By Schur's lemma, a non-zero endomorphism $\alpha: M \rightarrow M$ is an automorphism. If $\mathcal{E}^{\alpha}$ denotes the extension $\mathcal{E}$ twisted by $\alpha^{\vee}$, then the identity map $\mathcal{E}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow} \mathcal{E}^{\alpha}$ gives an equivalence of matrix coefficients $\xi = [ \mathcal{E}^{\alpha}, \widetilde{v}, \alpha_{dR}^{-1}(f)]$. It is straightforward to check using $(\ref{eqn: coaction})$ and the formulae which follow that $\xi$ is a primitive element. This construction provides an inverse to $(\ref{primtoext})$.

## Extensions in $\mathcal{H}$

The contents of this section are standard and well-known. Let $M =(M_B, M_{dR}, c)$ be an object in $\mathcal{H}$. The following complex $$W_0 M^+_B \oplus F^0 W_0 M_{dR} \overset{\mathrm{id} - c }{\longrightarrow} (W_0 M_{B}\otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathbb C)^{c_{dR}}$$ represents $R\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{H} }(\mathbb Q(0), M)$. Recall that $c_{dR}$ is complex conjugation on the right-hand factor of $M_{dR} \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathbb C$. Its action on $M_B \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathbb C$ is $c \,c_{dR} \,c^{-1}  = F_{\infty} \otimes c_B$ where $c_B$ is complex conjugation on the right-hand factor of $M_B \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathbb C$.

The kernel of the above complex is $$\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{H} } (\mathbb Q(0), M) \overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow} W_0M_B^+ \cap  c(F^0 W_0 M_{dR})$$ and the map is given by the image of $1\in \mathbb Q_{dR} \overset{c}{\cong} \mathbb Q_{B}$. The cokernel is [@Carlson] $$\label{Ext1formula}
\mathrm{Ext}^1_{\mathcal{H} }(\mathbb Q, M)  \overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow} W_0M_B^+\backslash  (W_0M_B\otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathbb C)^{c_{dR}} / c( F^0W_0 M_{dR})\ .$$ The map is given as follows. If $\mathcal{E}$ is an extension of $\mathbb Q(0)$ by $M$ in $\mathcal{H}$, it gives rise, after applying a fiber functor $\bullet = B/dR$, to two exact sequences $$0 \longrightarrow M_{\bullet} \longrightarrow\mathcal{E}_{\bullet} \longrightarrow\mathbb Q_{\bullet} \longrightarrow 0 \ .$$ Choose $B$ and $dR$ splittings by choosing a lift of $1_B \in \mathbb Q_B$ to $1_B \in W_0 \mathcal{E}_B^+$ and of $1_{dR} \in \mathbb Q_{dR}$ to $1_{dR} \in F^0 W_0 \mathcal{E}_{dR}$. Then $1_B - c(1_{dR})$ gives a well-defined element in the right-hand side of $(\ref{Ext1formula})$. Note that $(\ref{Ext1formula})$ is uncountably generated. Let $\mathcal{H} (\mathbb R)$ denote the category of triples $(M_B, M_{dR}, c)$ where now $M_B, M_{dR}$ are vector spaces over $\mathbb R$ (replace the ground field $\mathbb Q$ by $\mathbb R$). There is a functor $\otimes \mathbb R: \mathcal{H} \rightarrow \mathcal{H} (\mathbb R)$, sending $(M_B, M_{dR}, c)$ to $(M_B \otimes \mathbb R, M_{dR} \otimes \mathbb R, c\otimes \mathrm{id} )$.

**Corollary 36**. *Suppose that $W_{-1}M=M$. Then $$\label{dimensionformula}
\dim_{\mathbb R} \mathrm{Ext}_{\mathcal{H} (\mathbb R)}^1(\mathbb R(0),M\otimes \mathbb R)=  \dim_{\mathbb Q}  M^-_B  - \dim_{\mathbb Q} F^0 M_{dR}$$*

*Proof.* Since $c_{dR}, F_{\infty}$ act trivially on $c(F^0 M_{dR}) \cap M_B^+$, so too must $c_B$, since by §3.1 we have $F_{\infty} \otimes c_B = c c_{dR} c^{-1}$. It follows that $c(F^0 M_{dR}) \cap M_B^+ \subset F^0 \cap \overline{F}^0 = 0$, since $M$ has weights $\leq -1$. Now $(M_B\otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathbb C)^{c_{dR}}  = (M_B^+ \otimes \mathbb R) \oplus (M_B^{-} \otimes i \mathbb R)$, and conclude using $(\ref{Ext1formula})$ together with the fact that $W_0M= M$. ◻

The formula $(\ref{Ext1formula})$, together with $(\ref{primtoext})$ and theorem $\ref{thmdecompisom}$ provides a complete description of $\mathcal{H}$-periods, graded for the coradical filtration, in terms of semi-simple objects in $\mathcal{H} ^{ss}$. In practice, we often wish to fix a full Tannakian subcategory of pure objects in $\mathcal{H} ^{ss}$ (such as the one generated by Tate objects $\mathbb Q(n)$), and consider all $\mathcal{H}$-periods of objects whose semi-simplifications are of this type (periods of mixed Tate objects, in this case). The above results give a precise description for the structure of $\mathcal{H}$-periods of this type.

In order for this to be an accurate reflection of the structure of motivic periods, we need to know something about the image of the decomposition map $\Phi$ on the subspace $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,+}$, which we address presently.

## Speculation and context

Recall that $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,+}\subset \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }$ was the ring of motivic periods, i.e., those which come from the cohomology of an algebraic variety, and $G^{dR}$ is the quotient of $G^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} }$ acting faithfully on $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,+}$. Let $U^{dR}$ denote its unipotent radical. Let $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,+}_{ss} =( \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,+})^{U^{dR}}$ denote the invariants under $U^{dR}$, and set $$\mathcal{M} = C^1 \mathcal{O}(U^{dR})_+ = \mathrm{Prim} (\mathcal{O}(U^{dR}))\ .$$ Via $(\ref{primtoext})$ we think of $\mathcal{M}$ as 'motivic' extension classes.

**Conjecture 3**. The decomposition map induces an isomorphism $$\Phi : \mathrm{gr}^{\mathcal{C}} \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,+}\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,+}_{ss}  \otimes_{\mathbb Q} T^c(\mathcal{M})\  .$$

This conjecture is a generalisation of Goncharov's freeness conjecture for mixed Tate motives, and states that there should be no relations between the decompositions of motivic periods. We can now try to describe the constituent pieces. This is all conjectural, so we shall be brief.

The putative Tannakian category of mixed motives over $\mathbb Q$ should have a functor $$\mathcal{MM}_\mathbb Q\overset{h}{\longrightarrow} \mathcal{H}$$ where $h= (\omega_B, \omega_{dR}, \mathrm{comp}_{B,dR})$ is fully faithful and hence morphisms $$\mathrm{Ext}^1_{\mathcal{MM}_\mathbb Q}(\mathbb Q, M) \hookrightarrow \mathrm{Ext}^1_{\mathcal{H} }(\mathbb Q, h(M)) \overset{ \otimes \mathbb R}{\longrightarrow} \mathrm{Ext}^1_{\mathcal{H} (\mathbb R)}(\mathbb R, h(M)\otimes \mathbb R) \ .$$ One expects $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,+}_{ss}$ to be generated by the cohomology of smooth projective algebraic varieties $X$ over $\mathbb Q$. There is a definition for the group $\mathrm{Ext}^1_{\mathcal{MM}_\mathbb Q}(\mathbb Q(0), M)$, when $M=H^p(X)(q)$, in terms of motivic cohomology [@MotCohom]. Thus we expect $\mathcal{M}$ to be generated by the image of $H^{p+1}_{\mathcal{M}} (X, \mathbb Q(q)) \otimes H^p_{dR}(X)^{\vee}(-q)$ in the right-hand side of $(\ref{primtoext})$. Here, motivic cohomology $H^{p+1}_{\mathcal{M}}(X,\mathbb Q(q))$ can be defined either as a piece of the Adams grading of the algebraic $K$-theory of $X$, or via Bloch's higher Chow groups. Finally, Beilinson's conjectures predict the rank of these groups. In the simplest possible case when $M$ is pure and of weight $\leq -3$, then the image of $\mathrm{Ext}^1_{\mathcal{MM}_\mathbb Q}(\mathbb Q, M)$ in $\mathrm{Ext}^1_{\mathcal{H} (\mathbb R)}(\mathbb R, h(M)\otimes \mathbb R)$ should be a lattice and its rank given by $(\ref{dimensionformula})$. See [@Nekovar] for further details.

Putting these conjectural pieces together gives a fairly complete but highly speculative picture for the structure of the ring of motivic periods. In particular, we obtain a precise prediction for the 'size' of the ring of motivic periods of given types. A strategy that one can pursue is to fix a given tensor category of pure motives (for example Tate motives), and try to construct geometrically the iterated extensions (or equivalently, their motivic periods). The decomposition map is a tool to show, by computing periods, that the extensions one has are independent (§5.4, §5.5)

*Remark 37*. A more detailed account of this subject would include a discussion of regulators and special values of $L$-functions. In the present framework, one can translate Deligne's conjecture on critical $L$-values as giving a formula for certain motivic periods of unipotency degree $0$. Beilinson's conjectures give a formula for certain determinants of motivic periods of unipotency degree $1$. It is tantalising to speculate that this might be the beginning of a tower of conjectural formulae describing certain periods of all higher unipotency degrees.

## Representatives for primitive elements

In this section we return to general $\mathcal{H}$-periods. One would like to represent elements of $\mathrm{gr}^C_1 \mathcal{O}(U^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} })$ as concretely as possible. It follows from corollary 14 that the decomposition in degree one $$\Phi: \mathrm{gr}^C_1 \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} } \longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} ^{ss}} \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathrm{gr}^C_1 \mathcal{O}(U^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} } )$$ is surjective. However, there is no canonical map from $\mathrm{gr}^C_1 \mathcal{O}(U^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} })$ to $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }$, nor can we assign a period to an element of $\mathrm{gr}^C_1 \mathcal{O}(U^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} })$ in any obvious way. In some special cases, one can in fact assign numbers to primitive elements via the following two constructions:

1.  Call $f\in \mathrm{Prim}(\mathcal{O}(U^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} }))$ *stable* []{#gloss: stable label="gloss: stable"} if $F^1 M_f =M_f$ and $M_f$ is effective. This implies that all its Hodge numbers $h_{p,q}$ vanish for $p\leq 0$ or $q\leq 0$. In this case, a representative for the extension class $\mathcal{E}_f$ is separated (§4.3), and its de Rham realisation splits by $(\ref{sepsplit})$ : $$(\mathcal{E}_f)_{dR}  \cong \mathbb Q\oplus (M_f)_{dR} .$$ Thus we can view $f\in (M_f)_{dR} =F^1 (\mathcal{E}_f)_{dR}$ and $1\in \mathbb Q^{\vee}_{dR} = F^0 \mathcal{E}^{\vee}_{dR}$ and define a canonical de Rham period $$\xi_f= [ \mathcal{E}_f,  1, f  ]^{\mathfrak{dr}} \in \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{dr}}_{\mathcal{H} }\ .$$ Its single-valued version $\mathbf{s}^{\mathfrak{m} }(\xi_f)$ lies in $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }$ and we can take its period to obtain a number. The action of $G^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} }$ on $\mathbf{s}^{\mathfrak{m} }(\xi_f)$ is compatible with the conjugation action of $G^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} }$ on $\xi_f$.

2.  As in $(1)$, but also assume that $(M_f)_B^+=0$. Then $(\mathcal{E}^+_f)^{\vee}_{B} \cong \mathbb Q^{\vee}_B$, and $1 \in \mathbb Q^{\vee}_B$ lifts to $1\in (\mathcal{E}^+_f)^{\vee}_B$. We can directly define a motivic period $[\mathcal{E}_f, 1 , f]^{\mathfrak{m} } \in \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }$. Taking its period assigns a number to such a primitive element.

## Example: Mixed Tate motives over $\mathbb Q$

One of the few situations in which Beilinson's conjectures are completely known is the category $\mathcal{MT}(\mathbb Q)$ of mixed Tate motives over $\mathbb Q$. Its simple objects are Tate motives $\mathbb Q(n)$. The real Frobenius $F_{\infty}$ acts on $\mathbb Q(n)_B$ by $(-1)^n$. Thus $$\mathrm{Ext}^1_{\mathcal{MT}(\mathbb Q)}(\mathbb Q(0), \mathbb Q(n))   \longrightarrow\mathrm{Ext}^1_{\mathcal{H} }(\mathbb Q(0), \mathbb Q(n)) = \mathbb R/(2 i \pi \mathbb Q)^n$$ which has rank one if $n$ is odd, and zero if $n$ is even. In this case, Beilinson's conjecture is known as a consequence of deep theorems due to Borel, and we have $$\label{MTextn}  \mathrm{Ext}^1_{\mathcal{MT}(\mathbb Q)}(\mathbb Q(0), \mathbb Q(n))  \cong K_{2n-1}(\mathbb Q) \otimes_{\mathbb Z} \mathbb Q$$ which has rank $1$ for $n\geq 3$ odd and rank $0$ for $n$ even. For $n=1$, $$\label{MTextn2}
\mathrm{Ext}^1_{\mathcal{MT}(\mathbb Q)}(\mathbb Q(0), \mathbb Q(1)) \cong K_1(\mathbb Q) \otimes_{\mathbb Z} \mathbb Q= \mathbb Q^*\otimes_{\mathbb Z} \mathbb Q\ $$ is isomorphic to the infinite dimensional $\mathbb Q$-vector space with one generator for every prime $p$. Furthermore, all higher Ext groups vanish. It follows that $$H^1(U^{dR}_{\mathcal{MT}(\mathbb Q)}) = \bigoplus_{n \geq 1}  \big(  K_{2n-1}(\mathbb Q) \otimes_{\mathbb Z} \mathbb Q(-n)_{dR}\big) \ .$$ Let $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,+}_{\mathcal{MT}(\mathbb Q)}$ denote the ring of effective periods of $\mathcal{MT}(\mathbb Q)$. The subspace of semi-simple periods is generated by $\mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{m} }$. The decomposition is an isomorphism $$\label{eqndecompMTQ}
 \mathrm{gr}^C_{\bullet } \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,+}_{\mathcal{MT}(\mathbb Q)}  \overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}   \mathbb Q[\mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{m} }]  \otimes T^c ( \bigoplus_{n\geq 1} K_{2n-1}(\mathbb Q) \otimes_{\mathbb Z}\mathbb Q(-n))\ .$$ The Tate objects $\mathbb Q(n)$ satisfy both conditions $(1)$ and $(2)$ of §6.5. A generator $f_{2n-1}$ of the image of $(\ref{MTextn})$ in $\mathrm{Ext}^1_{\mathcal{H} }$ gives a rational multiple of $\zeta^{ \mathfrak{m}}(2n-1)$ under the second prescription. Choose the rational multiple to be one.[^10] Similarly, choose generators $\nu_{p}$ of $(\ref{MTextn2})$ which correspond under $(2)$ to $\log^{\mathfrak{m} }(p)$ for $p$ prime.

With this choice of generators, there is an isomorphism $$T^c( H^1(U^{dR}_{\mathcal{MT}(\mathbb Q)})) \cong \mathbb Q\langle \nu_{p},   f_{3}, f_5, f_7, \ldots \rangle$$ where the right-hand side denotes the shuffle algebra (tensor coalgebra) on generators $\nu_{p}$, for $p$ prime, which span a copy of $\mathbb Q(-1)$ of weight 2, and $f_{2n+1}$, for $n\geq 1$ which span a copy of $\mathbb Q(-1-2n)$ of weight $4n+2$.

**Theorem 38**. *The decomposition into primitives $(\ref{eqndecompMTQ})$ gives an isomorphism $$\label{effectiveMTQperiodsmodel}
 \mathrm{gr}^C \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,+ }_{\mathcal{MT}(\mathbb Q)}  \overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow} \mathbb Q[\mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{m} }]    \otimes    \mathbb Q\langle \nu_{p},   f_{3}, f_5, f_7, \ldots \rangle  \ ,$$ where $\mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{m} }$ is the Lefschetz period §5.2.*

Since in this case we can associate to each $f_{2n-1}$ its canonical period $(2)$ of §6.5 which equals $\zeta(2n-1)$, this gives an elementary way to think about periods of mixed Tate motives over $\mathbb Q$ as formal words in tensor products of odd zeta values $\zeta(2n-1)$ and logarithms of prime numbers. See [@MZVdecomp] for examples.

*Remark 39*. Beilinson's conjectures hold more generally for mixed Tate motives over number fields. Since for the time being we are considering only periods over $\mathbb Q$, this discussion is postponed to §10.1.

# Families of periods

We now sketch a possible formalism for studying periods varying in a family, and explain how this generalises several concepts which have been used in the physics literature in the case of iterated integrals and polylogarithms.

## Vector bundles and local systems

See [@DeP1], §10.24-10.52. Let $S$ be a smooth geometrically connected scheme over a field $k\subset \mathbb C$. An algebraic vector bundle $V$ on $S$ is a locally free $\mathcal{O}_S$-module of finite type. Denote the corresponding analytic vector bundle on $S^{\mathrm{an}}$ by $V^{\mathrm{an}}=  \mathcal{O}_{S^{\mathrm{an}}}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_S} V$. Consider the category $$\begin{gathered}
  \nonumber
 \mathcal{A}(S) = \hbox{Algebraic vector bundles on } S \hbox{  equipped with an }  \\
 \hbox{integrable connection with regular singularities at infinity}  \ .

\end{gathered}$$ Let $\omega$ denote the functor which to any object of $\mathcal{A}(S)$ associates the underlying vector bundle and forgets the connection. The category $\mathcal{A}(S)$ is a Tannakian category over $k$, and $\omega$ is a fiber functor over $S$.

**Definition 40**. The de Rham algebraic fundamental groupoid is the groupoid (in the category of schemes over $k$, acting on $S$) defined by $$\pi_1^{\mathrm{alg},dR}(S) = \mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{A}(S)}^{\otimes}(\omega)\ .$$

Consider also the category $$\mathcal{L}(S) =  \hbox{Local systems of  finite-dimensional } k\hbox{-vector spaces on } S(\mathbb C) \ .$$ For any complex point $t\in S(\mathbb C)$, the 'fiber at $t$' defines a functor $\omega_t : \mathcal{L}(S) \rightarrow \mathrm{Vec}_{k},$ and $\mathcal{L}(S)$, equipped with $\omega_t$, is a neutral Tannakian category over $\mathbb Q$.

**Definition 41**. The Betti algebraic fundamental group is the affine group scheme over $k$ defined by the Tannaka group of $\mathcal{L}(S)$ $$\pi_1^{\mathrm{alg},B}(S, t) = \mathrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{L}(S)}^{\otimes}(\omega_t)\ .$$ Given two complex points $t, t' \in S(\mathbb C)$, the fibers of the Betti algebraic groupoid over $(t,t')$ are $\pi_1^{\mathrm{alg},B}(S, t,t') =\mathrm{Isom}^{\otimes}_{\mathcal{L}(S)}(\omega_t, \omega_{t'}).$

Denote the ordinary topological fundamental group of $S(\mathbb C)$ at a point $t \in S(\mathbb C)$ by $\pi_1^{\mathrm{top}}(S(\mathbb C), t)$, and recall that there is an equivalence of categories $$\mathcal{L}(S) \overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}  \hbox{Finite-dimensional representations over $k$ of }  \pi_1^{\mathrm{top}}(S(\mathbb C), t)\ ,$$ which to a local system associates its fiber at $t$ together with its action of the topological fundamental group. Thus every element of $\pi_1^{\mathrm{top}}(S(\mathbb C), t)$ defines an automorphism of the fiber functor $\omega_t$, giving a natural homomorphism $$\pi_1^{\mathrm{top}}(S(\mathbb C), t) \longrightarrow\pi_1^{\mathrm{alg},B}(S, t)(k)\ $$ which is Zariski-dense. Similarly there is a natural morphism of groupoids $$\pi_1^{\mathrm{top}}(S(\mathbb C), t,t') \longrightarrow\pi_1^{\mathrm{alg},B}(S, t,t')(k)\ $$ where $\pi_1^{\mathrm{top}}(S(\mathbb C), t,t')$ are homotopy classes of paths from $t$ to $t'$ in $S(\mathbb C)$.

Recall that the Riemann-Hilbert correspondence [@De4], [@DeP1] 10.32(a), is an equivalence of categories $\mathcal{A}(S \times \mathbb C)  \rightarrow \mathcal{L}(S \times  \mathbb C)$ over $\mathbb C$ ([@DeP1] 10.35). To a complex vector bundle with integrable connection $(V,\nabla)$ it assigns the locally constant sheaf of flat sections $(V^{\mathrm{an}})^{\nabla}$ of the corresponding analytic bundle. Thus there is an isomorphism of affine groupoid schemes over $\mathbb C$ $$\pi_1^{\mathrm{alg},B}(S \times \mathbb C, a,b)  \overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}  \pi_1^{\mathrm{alg},dR}(S\times \mathbb C, a,b)\ ,$$ where $\pi_1^{\mathrm{alg},dR}(S, a,b)$ denotes the fiber of $\pi_1^{\mathrm{alg},dR}(S\times \mathbb C)$ over $(a,b) \in (S\times S)(\mathbb C)$, or equivalently the affine group scheme over $\mathbb C$ given by $\mathrm{Isom}^{\otimes}_{\mathbb{A}(S\times \mathbb C)}(\omega_a, \omega_b)$ where $\omega_a,\omega_b$ denote the functors 'fiber at $a,b$' respectively.

*Remark 42*. The basepoint $s$ can be replaced by any simply-connected subset $X\subset S(\mathbb C)$. For any point $x\in X$, the fiber at $x$ defines a fiber functor $\omega_x: \mathcal{L}(S) \rightarrow \mathrm{Vec}_k$. A path $\gamma$ from $x$ to $x'$ defines an isomorphism of fiber functors $\omega_x \overset{\sim}{\rightarrow} \omega_{x'}$. Since $X$ is simply connected, such a path is unique up to homotopy, and the isomorphism $\omega_x =\omega_{x'}$ is canonical. Thus $\omega_x$ depends only on $x$ up to unique isomorphism. We shall abusively denote the resulting fiber functor, for any choice of $x\in X$, by $\omega_X$. Likewise, one can define the fundamental group of $S(\mathbb C)$ based at $X$, which we denote by $\pi^{\mathrm{top}}_1(S(\mathbb C),  X)$.

## A category of realizations

Let $k=\mathbb Q$ and $S$ be as in the previous paragraph. Based on [@DeP1] §1.21, consider the category $\mathcal{H} (S)$ consisting of triples $$( \mathcal{V}_{B}, \mathcal{V}_{dR}, c)$$ given by the following data:

1.  A local system $\mathcal{V}_B$ of finite-dimensional $\mathbb Q$-vector spaces over $S(\mathbb C)$, equipped with a finite increasing filtration $W_\bullet \mathcal{V}_B$ of local sub-systems.

2.  An algebraic vector bundle $\mathcal{V}_{dR}$ on $S$ in $\mathcal{A}(S)$ equipped with an integrable connection $\nabla: \mathcal{V}_{dR} \rightarrow \mathcal{V}_{dR} \otimes_{\mathcal{O}_S} \Omega^1_S$ with regular singularities at infinity, a finite increasing filtration $W_\bullet \mathcal{V}_{dR}$ of $\mathcal{V}_{dR}$ by sub-objects in $\mathcal{A}(S)$, and a finite decreasing fitration $F^{\bullet}$ of algebraic sub-bundles satisfying Griffiths transversality $\nabla : F^p \mathcal{V}_{dR} \subset   F^{p-1} \mathcal{V}_{dR} \otimes_{\mathcal{O}_S}  \Omega^1_S$.

3.  An isomorphism of analytic vector bundles with connexion $$c: \mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{an}}_{dR}  \overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow} \mathcal{V}_B \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathcal{O}_{S^{\mathrm{an}}}\ ,$$ which respects the filtrations $W$, and where the connexion on $V_B \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathcal{O}_{S^{\mathrm{an}}}$ is the one for which sections of $V_B$ are flat. This is equivalent to an isomorphism $(\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{an}}_{dR} )^{\nabla} \cong \mathcal{V}_B \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathbb C$ of local systems of complex vector spaces on $S(\mathbb C)$ which respects the weight filtrations on both sides.

4.  The data $\mathcal{V}_B, c$ is functorial in the choice of algebraic closure $\mathbb C$ of $\mathbb R$. In particular, there is an isomorphism of local systems $$F_{\infty} : \mathcal{V}_B \overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow} \sigma^* \mathcal{V}_B$$ where $\sigma: S(\mathbb C) \overset{\sim}{\rightarrow} S(\mathbb C)$ is induced by complex conjugation.

This data is subject to the following conditions:

-   At each point $t\in S(\mathbb C)$, the vector space $(\mathcal{V}_B)_t$ equipped with the filtration $W$ and $c F$ on $(\mathcal{V}_B)_t \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathbb C$ is a graded-polarisable mixed Hodge structure.

-   We shall not consider taking limits in these notes, but if one wishes to, one should add further constraints [@SteenbrinkZucker] to demand that $\mathcal{V}_B$ defines an admissible variation of mixed Hodge structures, has locally quasi-unipotent monodromy, admits relative weight filtrations, and so on.

-   Let $\mathcal{O}_{\overline{S}^{\mathrm{an}}}$ denote the sheaf of antiholomorphic functions on $S^{\mathrm{an}}$. Pulling back the comparison $(3)$ to $\overline{S}(\mathbb C)$ via $\sigma^*$ induces an $\mathcal{O}_{\overline{S}^{\mathrm{an}}}$-linear isomorphism $\overline{c}: \mathcal{V}_{dR} \otimes_{\mathcal{O}_S} \mathcal{O}_{\overline{S}^{\mathrm{an}}} \overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}  \sigma^*(\mathcal{V}_B) \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathcal{O}_{\overline{S}^{\mathrm{an}}}.$ The following diagram commutes: $$\begin{array}{ccc}
      c: \mathcal{V}_{dR}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_S} \mathcal{O}_{S^{\mathrm{an}}}  & \overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}   &  \mathcal{V}_{B}\otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathcal{O}_{S^{\mathrm{an}}}   \\
     \downarrow &   & \downarrow   \\
      \overline{c}:   \mathcal{V}_{dR}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_S} \mathcal{O}_{\overline{S}^{\mathrm{an}}} &    \overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}  & \sigma^*(\mathcal{V}_{B})\otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathcal{O}_{\overline{S}^{\mathrm{an}}}
    \end{array}$$ where the vertical map on the left (resp. right) is the identity on $\mathcal{V}_{dR}$ (resp. $F_{\infty}: \mathcal{V}_{B} \rightarrow \sigma^*(\mathcal{V}_{B})$) tensored with the map $f\mapsto \overline{f}: \mathcal{O}_{S^{\mathrm{an}}} \rightarrow \mathcal{O}_{\overline{S}^{\mathrm{an}}}.$

The morphisms in $\mathcal{H} (S)$ respect the above data.

The category $\mathcal{H} (S)$ is Tannakian and has exact, faithful, tensor functors: $$\begin{array}{ccccccc}
   \omega_{dR}:  \mathcal{H} (S)  &\longrightarrow&\mathcal{A}(S) & \qquad,\qquad &   \omega_{B}:  \mathcal{H} (S)  &\longrightarrow&\mathcal{L}(S)   \\
    (\mathcal{V}_{B}, \mathcal{V}_{dR}, c) & \mapsto &   \mathcal{V}_{dR} &  &(\mathcal{V}_{B}, \mathcal{V}_{dR}, c) & \mapsto &   \mathcal{V}_{B}  \nonumber \ .
  \end{array}$$ One can think of $c$ as an isomorphism of functors (not strictly speaking fiber functors) from $\omega_{RH} \circ \omega_{dR}$ to $\omega_B \otimes \mathbb C$, where $\omega_{RH} : \mathcal{A}(S) \rightarrow \mathcal{L}(S) \otimes \mathbb C$ is $\mathcal{V}\mapsto (\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{an}})^{\nabla}$.

### Fiber functors

In order to motivate the following definitions, note that:

-   families of periods (such as the dilogarithm $\mathrm{Li}_2(x)$) are multivalued functions, i.e., functions on a universal covering space of $S(\mathbb C)$. In applications, we are often given a region $X\subset S(\mathbb C)$ where the function has a prescribed branch, or, for example, a local Taylor expansion (the dilogarithm has an expansion $\sum_{n\geq 1} {x^n\over n^2}$ which converges on $X = \{x\in \mathbb C: \quad |x|<1\}$). This is the region where the chain of integration is unambiguous.
