-   in some applications, including Feynman integrals, we cannot control the poles of the integrand. However we may be given a region $Y\subset S(\mathbb C)$ which is guaranteed to be free of poles. In this region, the integrals are finite and can be evaluated (bearing in mind that the integrals may also be multi-valued). We shall allow the region $Y$ to be empty.

Having made these preliminary coments, we now define Betti and de Rham fiber functors relative to some extra data $X, Y$, as follows. First of all, for any simply-connected $X\subset S(\mathbb C)$ we have, by remark 42, a fiber functor $$\omega^{ X}_{B}=\omega_{ X} \omega_B  : \mathcal{H} (S) \longrightarrow\mathrm{Vec}_{\mathbb Q}$$ which neutralizes $\mathcal{H} (S)$ over $\mathbb Q$. On the other hand, the functor $$\omega : \mathcal{H} (S) \longrightarrow S$$ which to a triple $(\mathcal{V}_{B}, \mathcal{V}_{dR}, c)$ associates the vector bundle underlying $\mathcal{V}_{dR}$ and forgets the connection, is a fiber functor according to the definition §2. For any morphism $u:T \rightarrow S$ of schemes over $\mathbb Q$, the composite of $\omega$ followed by $u^*$ defines a fiber functor over $T$. Now consider a region $Y\subset S(\mathbb C)$, and furthermore assume that $Y$ is contained in some $U(\mathbb C)$ where $U\subset S$ is affine. Then we can consider the ring $\mathcal{O}_{S,Y} = \varinjlim_{U} \mathcal{O}_U$ where the limit is over all open affine $U\subset S$ such that $Y\subset U(\mathbb C)$. It is non-zero by assumption. Define a fiber functor by pulling back along the morphism $u_Y: \mathrm{Spec} \,(\mathcal{O}_{S, Y}) \rightarrow S$, which we denote by $$\omega^{ Y}_{dR} = u_Y^* \omega : \mathcal{H} (S) \longrightarrow\mathrm{Proj}(\mathcal{O}_{S,Y})$$ and takes values in the category of projective modules of finite type over $\mathcal{O}_{S,Y}$. The fiber functor $\omega^{ Y}_{dR}$ is simply $\Gamma( \mathcal{O}_{S, Y}, \bullet)$. We shall mainly consider:

1.  $Y=\emptyset$. Then $u_Y$ is the generic point of $S$, and $\mathcal{O}_{S,Y}= K_S$, where $K_S$ is the field of fractions of $S$. Our fiber functor is $$\omega^{\mathrm{gen}}_{dR}: \mathcal{H} (S) \longrightarrow\mathrm{Vec}_{K_S}\ .$$

2.  Let $Y = \{s\}$ where $s\in S(\mathbb Q)\subset S(\mathbb C)$ is a rational point of $S$. Then $\mathcal{O}_{Y,S}= \mathcal{O}_s$ is the local ring of $S$ at $s$. The fiber functor $$\omega^{s}_{dR}  : \mathcal{H} (S) \longrightarrow\mathrm{Proj}( \mathcal{O}_s)  \ ,$$ takes values in projective (hence free) modules over $\mathcal{O}_s$ of finite type.

3.  $S = \mathrm{Spec} \,B$ is affine, and $Y= S(\mathbb C)$. Then $\mathcal{O}_{S,Y} = \mathcal{O}_S = B$, and the fiber functor $\omega^{S}_{dR}: \mathcal{H} (S) \rightarrow  \mathrm{Proj}(B)$ is the global sections functor $\Gamma(S, \omega(\mathcal{V}_{dR}))$.

Denote the corresponding Tannaka groups by $G^{B}_{\mathcal{H} (S), X}=Aut_{\mathcal{H} (S)}^{\otimes}(\omega^B_{X})$, and $G^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} (S), Y}=Aut_{\mathcal{H} (S)}^{\otimes}(\omega^{ Y}_{dR})$. The functor $\omega_{dR}$ gives a morphism $$\label{restrict2algdR}
 \pi_1^{dR, \mathrm{alg}} (S, \omega^{ Y}_{dR}) \longrightarrow G^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} (S),  Y}$$ of affine group schemes over $\mathcal{O}_{S, Y}$, where $\pi_1^{dR, \mathrm{alg}} (S, \omega^{ Y}_{dR}) = Aut^{\otimes}_{\mathcal{A}(S)}(\omega^{ Y}_{dR}).$ Similarly, the functor $\omega^{ X}_{B}$ defines a morphism $\pi_1^{\mathrm{alg}, B}(S, X)   \rightarrow  G^{B}_{\mathcal{H} (S), X}$ of affine group schemes over $\mathbb Q$, and in particular a *monodromy homomorphism*: []{#gloss: monodromyhomorphism label="gloss: monodromyhomorphism"} $$\nonumber
  \pi_1^{\mathrm{top}}(S(\mathbb C), X)   \longrightarrow G^{B}_{\mathcal{H} (S), X}(\mathbb Q)\ .$$

## Ring of $\mathcal{H} (S)$-periods.

Let $X,  Y\subset S(\mathbb C)$ be as above, and let $\omega^{ X}_{B}$, $\omega^{ Y}_{dR}$ be the corresponding fiber functors on $\mathcal{H} (S)$. Define a ring $$\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} , X, Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)}= \mathcal{O}(\mathrm{Isom}^{\otimes}_{\mathcal{H} (S)}(\omega^{ Y}_{dR},  \omega^{ X}_{B}))$$ of matrix coefficients (denoted $\mathcal{P}^{\omega_{B}^{ X}, \omega^{ Y}_{dR}}_{\mathcal{H} (S)}$ as in §(sectMatrixcoeffs)), where $B_1= \mathbb Q, B_2 = \mathcal{O}_{S, Y},$ and $k=\mathbb Q$. It is a $\mathbb Q\otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathcal{O}_{S, Y}$-bimodule, and generated by matrix coefficients $$\label{eqnfamilymatrixcoeff} [(\mathcal{V}_{B}, \mathcal{V}_{dR}, c), \sigma, \omega]^{\mathfrak{m} }$$ where $\sigma \in  \omega^{ X}_{B}\mathcal{V}_B^{\vee}$, $\omega \in \omega^{ Y}_{dR} \mathcal{V}_{dR}$. Similarly, define a ring of 'de Rham periods' to be $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{dr}, Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)} = Aut^{\otimes}_{\mathcal{H} (S)}(\omega^{ Y}_{dR})$. It is an $\mathcal{O}_{S, Y} \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathcal{O}_{S, Y}$-bimodule. It is generated by matrix coefficients $[(\mathcal{V}_{B}, \mathcal{V}_{dR}, c), v, \omega]^{\mathfrak{m} }$ where $\omega$ is as above and $v\in \omega^{ Y}_{dR} \mathcal{V}_{dR}^{\vee}$.

These rings are functorial in the following way. Let $S'$ be a smooth geometrically connected scheme over $\mathbb Q$, and $f:S \rightarrow S'$ a smooth morphism. Let $X\subset S'(\mathbb C)$ be simply-connected such that $f( X) \subseteq  X'$, and let $Y'\subset S'(\mathbb C)$ such that $f( Y)\subseteq  Y'$. The pull-back defines a functor $f^*:\mathcal{H} (S') \rightarrow \mathcal{H} (S)$ and hence a morphism $$\label{Pmfunctorial}
 f^* :\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} , X', Y'}_{\mathcal{H} (S')} \rightarrow \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} , X, Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)}$$ and a similar map on replacing $\mathfrak{m}$ by $\mathfrak{dr}$ and making the obvious changes.

### Constants

Now apply this to $S'= \mathrm{Spec} \,\, \mathbb Q$, $X'= Y'=S'(\mathbb C)$ (example (3) of the previous section) and $f: S \rightarrow \mathrm{Spec} \,(\mathbb Q)$ the structural map. One checks that the category $\mathcal{H} (S')$ is equivalent to $\mathcal{H}$, and that the Betti and de Rham fiber functors on $\mathcal{H}$ and $\mathcal{H} (S')$ coincide. Therefore $$\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} , pt, pt}_{\mathcal{H} (\mathrm{Spec} \,(\mathbb Q))} = \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }\ ,$$ and we obtain canonical homomorphisms (*'constant' maps*) []{#gloss: constantmap label="gloss: constantmap"} $$\label{constant}
   \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} } \longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} , X, Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)}  \quad \hbox{ and  } \quad \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{dr}}_{\mathcal{H} } \longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{dr}, Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)}$$ In this way, $\mathcal{H}$-periods can be viewed as 'constant' $\mathcal{H} (S)$-periods, since the functor $f^*:    \mathcal{H} \rightarrow \mathcal{H} (S)$ associates to $(V_{B}, V_{dR}, c)$ a triple $(\mathcal{V}_{B}, \mathcal{V}_{dR}, c)$ where $\mathcal{V}_{dR}=V_{dR} \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathcal{O}_S$ with $\nabla = \mathrm{id} \otimes d$, and $\mathcal{V}_B$ is the constant local system with fibres $V_B$.

### Evaluation

Now suppose that there is a rational point $$\label{tcondition} t\in S(\mathbb Q) \quad  \hbox{ such that }\quad   t \in X \cap Y  \ .$$ There are *evaluation maps* []{#gloss: evaluationmap label="gloss: evaluationmap"} at the point $t$ $$\label{eqn: specialisation}
 \mathrm{ev}_t: \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} , X, Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)} \longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} } \qquad \hbox{ and  } \quad  \mathrm{ev}_t: \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{dr}, Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)} \longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{dr}}_{\mathcal{H} }\ ,$$ which are induced by the functor 'fiber at $t$' from $\mathcal{H} (S) \rightarrow \mathcal{H}$ via $t: \mathcal{O}_{S, Y} \rightarrow \mathbb Q$. The constant maps $(\ref{constant})$ are sections of the evaluation maps $(\ref{eqn: specialisation})$. Note that one may wish to weaken the condition $(\ref{tcondition})$ if one bears in mind that for $t\notin X$ the evaluation map is not well-defined (it is ambiguous up to the action of monodromy), and for $t \notin Y$ the evaluation may be infinite due to the presence of poles.

## Some properties of $\mathcal{H} (S)$-periods

The ring $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} , X, Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)}$ has a left *Galois action* by the group $G^{dR, Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)}$, or equivalently, a right coaction $$\label{H(S)coaction}
\Delta^{\!\mathfrak{m} }: \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} , X, Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)} \longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} , X, Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)} \otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{S, Y}} \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{dr}, Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)} \ ,$$ given by the same formula as $(\ref{eqn: coaction})$. Since the fiber functor $\omega^{ Y}_{dR}$ factors through $\omega_{dR}$, the action of $G^{dR, Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)}$ restricts to an action of the algebraic de Rham fundamental group $\pi_1^{dR, \mathrm{alg}}(S, \omega^{ Y}_{dR})$ via the map $(\ref{restrict2algdR})$. More generally, there is an action of the de Rham algebraic fundamental groupoid: $$\pi_1^{dR, \mathrm{alg}}(S, \omega^{ Y_1}_{dR}, \omega^{ Y_2}_{dR}) \times   \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} , X, Y_1}_{\mathcal{H} (S)} \longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} , X, Y_2}_{\mathcal{H} (S)}$$ for any $Y_1, Y_2 \subset S(\mathbb C)$ as above, where the left-hand side is $\mathrm{Isom}^{\otimes}_{\mathcal{A}(S)}(   \omega^{ Y_1}_{dR}, \omega^{ Y_2}_{dR})$.

The ring $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} , X, Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)}$ has an increasing *weight filtration* []{#gloss: weightfiltration2 label="gloss: weightfiltration2"} $W_{\bullet}$ which is inherited from the weight filtration on the category $\mathcal{H} (S)$ (specifically, $W_n$ is generated by matrix coefficients $(\ref{eqnfamilymatrixcoeff})$ such that the de Rham class satisfies $\omega \in \omega^Y_{dR} W_n \mathcal{V}_{dR}$), and is preserved by $G^{dR, Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)}$. The morphisms $(\ref{constant})$ and $(\ref{eqn: specialisation})$ preserve the weight filtrations. The same comments apply to the rings of de Rham periods $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{dr}, Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)}$. This notion, applied in the setting [@Cosmic] gives a rigorous meaning to intuitive notions of 'transcendental weight' of functions in the physics literature.

The ring $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} , X, Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)}$ also has a right action by $\pi_1^{\mathrm{alg},B}(S, X)$, and in particular, a right action by the topological fundamental group or *monodromy action*: []{#gloss: monodromyaction label="gloss: monodromyaction"} $$\label{monodromyonPe}
 \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} , X, Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)} \times \pi_1^{\mathrm{top}}(S(\mathbb C), X) \longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} , X, Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)}\ .$$ It commutes with the action of $G^{dR, Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)}$ and also respects $W$. The monodromy action can be read off matrix coefficients $(\ref{eqnfamilymatrixcoeff})$ by its action on the Betti class $\sigma$, since the $\mathbb Q$-vector space $(\mathcal{V}_B)_{ X}$ naturally carries a right $\pi_1^{\mathrm{top}}(S(\mathbb C), X)$-action. More generally, for any $X_1, X_2 \subset S(\mathbb C)$ simply connected we have an action of the topological fundamental groupoid or *continuation along paths* $$\label{continuationonPe}
 \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} , X_1, Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)} \times \pi_1^{\mathrm{top}}(S(\mathbb C), X_1, X_2) \longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} , X_2, Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)}\ .$$ These actions commute with the action of $G^{dR, Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)}$ and respect the weight filtration, since the latter are defined entirely in terms of the de Rham class.

The following structures on $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} , X, Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)}$ are *not preserved* by the action of $G^{dR, Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)}$. By the Tannaka theorem $\ref{thm:  Tannaka}$, $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} , X, Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)}$ is the $\omega^{ Y}_{dR}$-image of an ind-object in $\mathcal{H} (S)$. Denote its image under $\omega_{dR}$ by $\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathfrak{m} , X, Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)}$. It is an (infinite-dimensional) algebraic vector bundle on $S$, or ind-object of $\mathcal{A}(S)$, whose image under $u^*_{ Y}$, the restriction to $\mathrm{Spec} \,(\mathcal{O}_{S, Y})$, is $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} , X, Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)}$. Furthermore, it is equipped with an increasing weight filtration $W$, decreasing Hodge filtration $F$, and an integrable connection $$\nabla : \widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathfrak{m} , X, Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)} \longrightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathfrak{m} , X, Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)} \otimes_{\mathcal{O}_S} \Omega^1_S$$ which satisfies Griffiths transversality. Restricting to $\mathrm{Spec} \,(\mathcal{O}_{S, Y})$, we deduce the existence of a *Hodge filtration* []{#gloss: Hodgefilt2 label="gloss: Hodgefilt2"} $F^{\bullet}  \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} , X, Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)}$ and *connection* []{#gloss: connection label="gloss: connection"} $$\label{nablaonPe}
 \nabla: \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} , X, Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)} \longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} , X, Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)} \otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{S, Y}} \Omega^1_{\mathcal{O}_{S,Y}}\ ,$$ where $\Omega^1_{\mathcal{O}_{S,Y}}$ is the ring of Kähler differentials on $\mathcal{O}_{S, Y}$. The connection $(\ref{nablaonPe})$ is integrable, respects $W$, and satisfies Griffiths transversality with respect to $F$. On matrix coefficients the connection $(\ref{nablaonPe})$ is given by $$\nabla [(\mathcal{V}_{B}, \mathcal{V}_{dR}, c), \sigma, \omega]^{\mathfrak{m} } = [(\mathcal{V}_{B}, \mathcal{V}_{dR}, c), \sigma, \nabla \omega]^{\mathfrak{m} }\ ,$$ where $\nabla : u_ Y^* (\mathcal{V}_{dR}) \rightarrow  u_ Y^* (\mathcal{V}_{dR}) \otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{S, Y}}  \Omega^1_{S, Y}$ is the connection on $\mathcal{V}_{dR}$ restricted to $u_Y: \mathrm{Spec} \,(\mathcal{O}_{S, Y})\rightarrow S$. The space $F^n  \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} (S), X, Y}$ is generated by matrix coefficients $[(\mathcal{V}_{B}, \mathcal{V}_{dR}, c), \sigma, \omega]^{\mathfrak{m} }$ where $\omega \in \omega^Y_{dR}  F^n   \mathcal{V}_{dR}$. There is an analogous connection on the ring of left de Rham periods $\mathcal{P}^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} (S)}$, where the connection acts on $[(\mathcal{V}_{B}, \mathcal{V}_{dR}, c), v, \omega]^{\mathfrak{dr}}$ through its action on $\omega$. From formula $(\ref{eqn: coaction})$, namely $$\Delta {[}V, \sigma, \omega]^{\mathfrak{m} }  =  \sum_{i} [V, \sigma, e_i]^{\mathfrak{m} } \otimes  [V, e^{\vee}_i, v]^{\mathfrak{dr}}$$ one can check that the following diagram commutes $$\begin{array}{ccc}
 \mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{H} (S)}^{\mathfrak{m} , X, Y} & \overset{\Delta}{\longrightarrow}   & \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} , X, Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)} \otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{S, Y}} \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{dr}}_{\mathcal{H} (S), Y}   \\
  \downarrow &   &   \downarrow  \\
  \mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{H} (S)}^{\mathfrak{m} , X, Y} \otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{S, Y}} \Omega^1_{\mathcal{O}_{S, Y}} &  \overset{\Delta\otimes \mathrm{id} }{\longrightarrow}   &    \mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{H} (S)}^{\mathfrak{m} , X, Y} \otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{S, Y}}  \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{dr}, Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)} \otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{S,Y}} \Omega^1_{S, Y}
\end{array}$$ where the vertical map on the left is $\nabla$, and on the right is $\mathrm{id} \otimes \nabla$. Hence $$(\Delta \otimes \mathrm{id} )\nabla = (\mathrm{id} \otimes \nabla) \Delta \ ,$$ which relates the Galois coaction to the connection. Since the connection $(\ref{nablaonPe})$ only invokes the de Rham framing, it commutes with the monodromy action $(\ref{monodromyonPe})$.

*Remark 43*. The previous remarks give a proof of two formulae $(5.23)$ conjectured in [@Duhr] in the case of the multiple polylogarithms (iterated integrals on the moduli space of curves of genus $0$ with $n$ marked points), and generalise to all families of motivic periods (and in particular, motivic Feynman amplitudes).

Finally, let us suppose that $X,  Y$ are preserved by a subgroup $A$ of the group of automorphisms of $S$. Then the functoriality $(\ref{Pmfunctorial})$ gives rise to an action $$\label{AutosonPm}
   A \times \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,  X,  Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)} \longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,  X,  Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)}$$ of $A$ on $\mathcal{H} (S)$-periods, and similarly on $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{dr}}_{\mathcal{H} (S),  Y}$ under the weaker assumption that only $Y$ is stable under $A$.

## The period homomorphism

Let $X, Y\subset S(\mathbb C)$ with $X$ simply-connected. The period is defined by pairing the Betti and de Rham classes of a matrix coefficient $(\ref{eqnfamilymatrixcoeff})$ using the comparison $c$. In order to obtain a multi-valued function on the whole of $S(\mathbb C)$, and not just on $X$, these classes must be suitably extended as follows. Let $\pi: \widetilde{S}(\mathbb C)_{ X}\rightarrow S(\mathbb C)$ denote the universal covering space of $S(\mathbb C)$ based at $X$, and let $M_{ X, Y}(S(\mathbb C))$ denote the ring of meromorphic functions on $\widetilde{S}(\mathbb C)_{ X}$ which have no poles on $\pi^{-1}( Y)$. By this we mean that for every $f \in M_{ X, Y}(S(\mathbb C))$, and any $x \in \widetilde{S}(\mathbb C)_{ X}$, there exists a $g$, an element in the fraction field of $S$, such that $f \times  \pi^{-1}(g)$ is analytic in some open neighbourhood of $x$. If $x\in \pi^{-1}(Y)$ then we can take $g=1$, and $f$ is already analytic in some neighbourhood of $x$. Elements of $M_{ X, Y}(S(\mathbb C))$ can be thought of as multivalued meromorphic functions on $S(\mathbb C)$ with a prescribed branch on the set $X$, and poles outside $Y$.

Suppose that $Y$ is contained in the complex points of some open affine subset of $S$, as earlier. The *period map* []{#gloss: periodmap label="gloss: periodmap"} is then a homomorphism $$\mathrm{per}: \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} , X, Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)} \longrightarrow M_{ X, Y}(S(\mathbb C))\ ,$$ and is defined on matrix coefficients $[(\mathcal{V}_B, \mathcal{V}_{dR}, c), \sigma, v]^{\mathfrak{m} }$ as follows. The local system $\pi^*(\mathcal{V}_B^{\vee})$ is trivial on the simply connected space $\widetilde{S}(\mathbb C)_{ X}$ and $\sigma$ extends to a unique global section $\sigma \in \Gamma( \widetilde{S}(\mathbb C)_{ X},  \pi^*(\mathcal{V}_B^{\vee}))$. Let $x\in\widetilde{S}(\mathbb C)_{ X}$, and let $N_x$ be a sufficiently small neighbourhood of $x$ such that the restriction of $\pi$ to $N_x$ is an isomorphism. We obtain a local section $\sigma_x \in \Gamma(\pi(N_x), \mathcal{V}_B^{\vee})$, defined by $\sigma_x= (\pi|_{N_x}^{-1})^*\sigma$.

On the other hand, by assumption on $Y$, there exists an open affine $U\subset S$ with $Y\subset U(\mathbb C)$ such that $v\in \Gamma(U, \mathcal{V}_{dR})$. Let $W\subset S$ be an open affine such that $\pi(x) \in W(\mathbb C)$ and the restriction of $\mathcal{V}_{dR}$ to $W$ is trivial as a vector bundle. Since $S$ is irreducible, $U \cap W \neq \emptyset$ and we have $v|_{U\cap W}\in \Gamma(U \cap W, \mathcal{V}_{dR}) = \Gamma(W,\mathcal{V}_{dR}) \otimes_{\mathcal{O}_W} \mathcal{O}_{U \cap W}$. It can have poles on $W\backslash U$. We can 'clear its denominator', since $\mathcal{V}_{dR}$ is of finite type, there exists an $\alpha \in \mathcal{O}_W$ such that $\alpha  v \in \Gamma(W, \mathcal{V}_{dR})$. By making $N_x$ smaller, we can assume that $W(\mathbb C)$ contains $\pi(N_x)$, and we can view $\alpha v$, by restriction and passing to the associated analytic vector bundle, as an element in $\Gamma(\pi(N_x), \mathcal{V}_{dR}^{an})$. We have defined $$\sigma_x \in  \Gamma(\pi(N_x), \mathcal{V}_B^{\vee}) \qquad \hbox{ and } \qquad   \alpha  v \in \Gamma(\pi(N_x), \mathcal{V}_{dR}^{an})\ .$$ The comparison map $c : \mathcal{V}^{an}_{dR} \rightarrow \mathcal{V}_B \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathcal{O}_S^{an}$ yields an element $$\sigma_x( c ( \alpha v)) \in \Gamma( \pi(N_x), \mathcal{O}_S^{an})\ $$ which can be viewed as a locally analytic function on $N_x$. The period homomorphism is defined on the following matrix coefficient by $$\mathrm{per}( [(\mathcal{V}_B, \mathcal{V}_{dR}, c), \sigma, \alpha v]^{\mathfrak{m} })  = \sigma_x( c (  \alpha v))  \ ,$$ and the period of $[(\mathcal{V}_B, \mathcal{V}_{dR}, c), \sigma, v]^{\mathfrak{m} }$ is obtained by dividing by the rational function $\alpha$. It is well-defined (does not depend on the representative for the matrix coefficient) because morphisms in $\mathcal{H} (S)$ respect the comparison $c$. Note that it locally has poles along the zeros of $\alpha$. In the case when $\pi(x) \in Y \subset U(\mathbb C)$, we may assume $W=U$ in the above and hence $\alpha=1$, and the period has no poles. The period homomorphism therefore takes values in $M_{ X, Y}(S(\mathbb C))$ as claimed.

The period map satisfies the following properties, which follow from the definitions. First of all, the period is functorial with respect to smooth morphisms, and in particular is compatible with the constant map $(\ref{constant})$. This means that the following diagram commutes: $$\begin{array}{ccc}
 \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} } & \overset{(\ref{constant})}{\longrightarrow}  &  \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} , X, Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)}  \\
 \downarrow_{\mathrm{per}}&   & \downarrow_{\mathrm{per}}  \\
  \mathbb C&  \subset   & M_{ X, Y}(S(\mathbb C))
\end{array}$$ where the inclusion on the bottom line is the inclusion of constant functions. The period is also compatible with monodromy; there is a commutative diagram $$\begin{array}{ccc}
 \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} , X, Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)}    \times \pi_1^{\mathrm{top}}(S(\mathbb C),  X) & \longrightarrow&  \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} , X, Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)}  \\
 \downarrow_{\mathrm{per}\times \mathrm{id} }&   & \downarrow_{\mathrm{per}}  \\
  M_{ X, Y}(S(\mathbb C))   \times \pi_1^{\mathrm{top}}(S(\mathbb C),  X) &  \longrightarrow&     M_{ X, Y}(S(\mathbb C))
\end{array}$$ where the action of the topological fundamental group on $M_{ X, Y}(S(\mathbb C))$ is induced by the action of the group of deck transformations on $\widetilde{S}(\mathbb C)_{ X}$. If one thinks of elements of $M_{ X, Y}(S(\mathbb C))$ as multivalued functions on an open subset of $S(\mathbb C)$, this is just analytic continuation along loops. More generally, given two simply-connected subsets $X_1,  X_2$ of $S(\mathbb C)$, we have a compatibility of groupoid actions $$\begin{array}{ccc}
 \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} , X_1, Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)}    \times \pi_1^{\mathrm{top}}(S(\mathbb C),  X_1, X_2) & \longrightarrow&  \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} , X_2, Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)}  \\
 \downarrow_{\mathrm{per}\times \mathrm{id} }&   & \downarrow_{\mathrm{per}}  \\
  M_{ X_1, Y}(S(\mathbb C))   \times \pi_1^{\mathrm{top}}(S(\mathbb C),  X_1, X_2) &  \longrightarrow&     M_{ X_2, Y}(S(\mathbb C))
\end{array}$$ where the map along the bottom is defined by analytic continuation.

Now let $x \in \mathrm{Der}_{\mathbb Q}(\mathcal{O}_{S, Y})$ be a $\mathbb Q$-linear derivation of $\mathcal{O}_{S, Y}$. It defines a map $\Omega^1_{S,Y} \rightarrow \mathcal{O}_{S, Y}$. Let $\partial_x = (\mathrm{id} \otimes x) \nabla$, where $\nabla$ is $(\ref{nablaonPe})$. We have a commutative diagram $$\begin{array}{ccc}
 \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} , X, Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)}  &  \overset{\partial_x} \longrightarrow&  \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} , X, Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)}  \\
 \downarrow_{\mathrm{per}}&   & \downarrow_{\mathrm{per}}  \\
  M_{ X, Y}(S(\mathbb C))   &  \longrightarrow&     M_{ X, Y}(S(\mathbb C))
\end{array}$$ where the map along the bottom is differentiation of locally analytic functions along the vector field defined by $x$ on some open subset of $S(\mathbb C)$ containing $Y$. Thus the connexion on the ring of periods corresponds to differentiation of functions.

The period map is functorial. Suppose we are in the situation described in the lines preceeding $(\ref{Pmfunctorial})$. Then there is a commutative diagram $$\begin{array}{ccc}
 \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} , X', Y'}_{\mathcal{H} (S')}  &  \overset{f_*} \longrightarrow&  \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} , X, Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)}  \\
 \downarrow_{\mathrm{per}}&   & \downarrow_{\mathrm{per}}  \\
  M_{ X', Y'}(S'(\mathbb C))   &  \overset{f_*}{\longrightarrow}  &     M_{ X, Y}(S(\mathbb C))
\end{array}$$ where the map along the bottom is composition $\phi \mapsto \phi \circ f$.

Suppose that $t\in S(\mathbb Q)$ is a rational point and the image of $t$ in $S(\mathbb C)$ lies in $X \cap Y$. Then the period map is well-defined at $t$, and we have a commutative diagram $$\begin{array}{ccc}
 \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} , X, Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)} & \overset{\mathrm{ev}_t}{\longrightarrow}  &  \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }  \\
 \downarrow_{\mathrm{per}_t}&   & \downarrow_{\mathrm{per}}  \\
  \mathbb C&  =  & \mathbb C
\end{array}$$ where $\mathrm{per}_t$ is evaluation of elements of $M_{ X, Y}(S(\mathbb C))$ at $t$. Thus $(\ref{eqn: specialisation})$ corresponds to taking the value of a function at a point. In this manner many classical notions for multivalued functions have analogues on the ring of motivic periods. Nonetheless, the ring of motivic periods has extra features such as the weight and Galois group which are invisible on functions.

## Complex conjugation

Consider the category $\mathcal{H} (\overline{S})$ consisting of triples $(\mathcal{V}_{B}, \mathcal{V}_{dR}, \overline{c})$ defined in an identical manner as above, except that $$\overline{c}: \mathcal{V}_{dR} \otimes_{\mathcal{O}_S} \mathcal{O}_{\overline{S}^{\mathrm{an}}} \overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow} \mathcal{V}_B \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathcal{O}_{\overline{S}^{\mathrm{an}}}$$ is antiholomorphic (and respects $W$, etc). The real Frobenius defines an equivalence $F_{\infty} : \mathcal{H} (S) \rightarrow \mathcal{H} (\overline{S})$ which maps $(\mathcal{V}_{B}, \mathcal{V}_{dR}, c)$ to $(\sigma^* \mathcal{V}_B, \mathcal{V}_{dR}, \overline{c})$. We can form a ring of periods $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,  X,  Y}_{\mathcal{H} (\overline{S})}$ as before, and we have an isomorphism $$F_{\infty} : \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,  X,  Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)} \overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow} \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} , \overline{ X},  Y}_{\mathcal{H} (\overline{S})} \ .$$ Composing with the map $\sigma^* : \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,\overline{ X},  Y}_{\mathcal{H} (\overline{S})}  \rightarrow \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} , X,  Y}_{\mathcal{H} (\overline{S})}$, which sends $(\mathcal{V}_B, \mathcal{V}_{dR} , \overline{c} )$ to $(\sigma^* \mathcal{V}_B, \mathcal{V}_{dR}, \overline{c} \sigma^*)$, gives an isomorphism $\sigma^* F_{\infty}  : \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,  X,  Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)} \overset{\sim}{\rightarrow} \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} , X,  Y}_{\mathcal{H} (\overline{S})} .$ The period map on $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,  X,  Y}_{\mathcal{H} (\overline{S})}$ takes values in the ring $\overline{M}_{ X, Y}(S(\mathbb C))$ of antiholomorphic functions on $S(\mathbb C)$ with prescribed branch on $X$. The following diagram commutes $$\begin{array}{ccc}
  F_{\infty}\sigma^*:  \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,  X,  Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)}  & \overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}    & \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,  X,  Y}_{\mathcal{H} (\overline{S})}    \\
  \downarrow_{\mathrm{per}} &   &   \downarrow_{\mathrm{per}}   \\
   M_{ X, Y}(S(\mathbb C)) &  \overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}   &  \overline{M}_{ X, Y}(S(\mathbb C))
\end{array}$$ where the map along the bottom is complex conjugation $f \mapsto \overline{f}$.

# Further remarks

There are many constructions that can be made involving families of periods. I shall only mention the minimum required for applications to [@Cosmic], and single-valued functions and symbols which have independent applications to physics.

## Some jargon

Most of the definitions of earlier paragraphs generalise in an evident way to the case of families of periods. I will not repeat all of them here except to mention that an element $\xi \in \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} (S), X, Y}$ generates a representation of $G^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} (S), Y}$ which, by the Tannaka theorem, defines a (minimal) object $M(\xi)$ of $\mathcal{H} (S)$ (see §2.4). The *Hodge numbers* and *Hodge polynomial* of $\xi$ are defined as the Hodge numbers and polynomial of the fiber of $M(\xi)$ at any point $t\in  X$. Define the *local system associated to $\xi$* to be $M(\xi)_B$. It is equivalent to the *monodromy representation* of $\xi$ which is the vector space $\omega_{B, X} (M(\xi)) \in \mathrm{Vec}_{\mathbb Q}$ together with its left $\pi_1(S(\mathbb C),  X)$-action. Likewise, define the *vector bundle with connexion* associated to $M(\xi)$ to be $M(\xi)_{dR}$, equipped with its integrable connexion $\nabla$. The vector bundle and local system associated to $\xi$ are equivalent under the Riemann-Hilbert correspondence after tensoring with $\mathbb C$.

## Variant: families of periods with single-valued branch

For applications such as [@Cosmic], we are often faced with the situation where we have a family of periods on some open subvariety $S$ of a given variety $Z$, without knowing what $S$ is. This is in fact the typical situation; $S$ will be the complement of a discriminant locus which is complicated and not computable. We often have some further information, namely that the periods are well-defined on some connected open subset $U\cap S(\mathbb C) \subset S(\mathbb C)$ for the analytic topology. This motivates the following definition.

Let $Z$ be a smooth, geometrically connected algebraic variety over $\mathbb Q$, and let $U \subset Z(\mathbb C)$ be a connected open analytic subset. For any geometrically connected $S\subset Z$, consider the ring of periods $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} (S), s, \emptyset}$ for any $s\in S(\mathbb C)\cap U$. For any two points $s_1,s_2 \in U \cap S(\mathbb C)$, analytic continuation along paths gives $$\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} (S), s_1, \emptyset} \times \pi_1^{\mathrm{top}}(U \cap S(\mathbb C), s_1, s_2) \overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow} \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} (S), s_2, \emptyset}$$ and in particular, since $U\cap S(\mathbb C)$ is connected, a canonical isomorphism $$\big(\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} (S), s_1, \emptyset} \big)^{\pi_1(U\cap S(\mathbb C), s_1)} = \big(\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} (S), s_2, \emptyset} \big)^{\pi_1(U\cap S(\mathbb C), s_2)}\ .$$ Thus, by moving the base-point $s$ if necessary, we can define $$\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,U}_{\mathcal{H} (Z)} = \varinjlim_{S} \big(\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} (S), s, \emptyset}\big)^{\pi_1(U\cap S(\mathbb C), s)}$$ where the limit is over all such $S\subset Z$, since any two such opens $S_1 , S_2 \subset Z$ have a non-empty intersection $U\cap S_1 \cap S_2(\mathbb C)$. The periods of elements of $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,U}_{\mathcal{H} (Z)}$ restrict to single-valued meromorphic functions on $U$. The ring $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,U}_{\mathcal{H} (Z)}$ has similar properties to the rings of periods discussed earlier, except for the monodromy action.

## Single-valued versions

We can define single-valued versions of families of motivic periods in a similar way to §4.1, except that there are some slight differences. Let $M= (\mathcal{V}_{B}, \mathcal{V}_{dR}, c)$ by an object of $\mathcal{H} (S)$. As in §4.1, there is a universal comparison homomorphism $$c_M^{\mathfrak{m} }\ :\  \omega_Y(\mathcal{V}_{dR}) \longrightarrow\omega_X(\mathcal{V}_B) \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} (S),  X,  Y}\ .$$ Applying $F_{\infty}\sigma^*$ to the right-hand factor gives a homomorphism $$\overline{c_M}^{\mathfrak{m} }:= F_{\infty} \sigma^* c_M^{\mathfrak{m} }\ :\  \omega_Y(\mathcal{V}_{dR}) \longrightarrow\omega_X(\mathcal{V}_B) \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} (\overline{S}),  X,  Y}\ .$$ Define a ring $$\mathcal{P}= \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} (\overline{S}),  X,  Y}  \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} (S),  X,  Y}\  .$$ Embed $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} (S),  X,  Y}$ into $\mathcal{P}$ via $x \mapsto 1 \otimes x$, and $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} (\overline{S}),  X,  Y}$ via $x\mapsto x\otimes 1$. Thus $$c_M^{\mathfrak{m} } \quad ,\quad  \overline{c_M}^{\mathfrak{m} } \quad \in \quad \mathrm{Hom}( \omega_Y(\mathcal{V}_{dR}), \omega_X(\mathcal{V}_B)) \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathcal{P}$$ Finally, define $$\mathbf{s}^{\mathfrak{m} }_M = (\overline{c_M}^{\mathfrak{m} })^{-1} c_M^{\mathfrak{m} }   \quad \in  \quad \mathrm{End}( \omega_Y(\mathcal{V}_{dR})) \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathcal{P}\ .$$ We leave it to the reader to replace the above argument with universal arguments on torsors, exactly as in §4.1, to define a canonical element $$\mathbf{s}^{\mathfrak{m} } \in G^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} (S),  Y} (\mathcal{P})$$ or equivalently, a homomorphism $$\label{singlevaluedhomforfunctions}
 \mathbf{s}^{\mathfrak{m} }: \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{dr}}_{\mathcal{H} (S),  Y} \longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} (\overline{S}),  X,  Y}  \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} (S),  X,  Y}\ ,$$ where the left (resp. inverse right) action of $G^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} (S),  Y}$ on $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{dr}}_{\mathcal{H} (S),  Y}$ corresponds to the left action of $G^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} (S),  Y}$ on $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} (S),  X,  Y}$ (resp. $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} (\overline{S}),  X,  Y}$). It follows from the definition that the single-valued homomorphism $(\ref{singlevaluedhomforfunctions})$ is compatible with the connexion: $$(\mathbf{s}^{\mathfrak{m} } \otimes \mathrm{id} )\nabla ( \xi) = ( \mathrm{id} \otimes \nabla) \mathbf{s}^{\mathfrak{m} }(\xi) \ .$$ This means that, after taking the period homomorphism, the single-valued map respects the *holomorphic* (and only the holomorphic) differential.

*Remark 44*. When $S= \mathrm{Spec} \,(\mathbb Q)$ is a point, $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} (\overline{S}),  X,  Y} \cong   \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} (S),  X,  Y}  \cong \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }$ and the earlier definition $(\ref{single-valuedmapdefn})$ is obtained as the composition $$\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{dr}}_{\mathcal{H} } \overset{\mathbf{s}^{\mathfrak{m} }} \longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }  \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} } \longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }$$ where the second map is multiplication.

**Examples 45**. Consider the dilogarithm motivic period on $S = \mathbb P^1 \backslash \{0,1,\infty\}$, defined in $(\ref{defnmultiplepolylog})$ below. Here $X\subset S(\mathbb C)$ is the open interval $(0,1)$. Its universal period matrix over a point $x\in X\subset S(\mathbb C)$ is $$c^{\mathfrak{m} }_M =
\left(
\begin{array}{ccc}
 1  & \mathrm{Li}_1^{\mathfrak{m} }(x)   &   \mathrm{Li}_2^{\mathfrak{m} }(x)  \\
 0 & \mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{m} }      &    \mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{m} } \log^{\mathfrak{m} }(x)      \\
  0 & 0    &    (\mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{m} })^2
\end{array}
\right) \ .$$ Let $\gamma_0$ (resp. $\gamma_1$) denote a small path around $0$ (resp. $1$) based at $X$. Under the monodromy homomorphism they act by left multiplication by $$\rho(\gamma_0) = \left(
\begin{array}{ccc}
 1  & 1   &  0  \\
 0 & 1      &    0      \\
  0 & 0    &   1
\end{array}
\right) \quad \hbox{ and }  \quad \rho(\gamma_1) = \left(
\begin{array}{ccc}
 1  & 0   &  0  \\
 0 & 1      &    1      \\
  0 & 0    &   1
\end{array}
\right)\ .$$ Denoting the image of $\mathrm{Li}_k^{\mathfrak{m} }(x)$ under $F_{\infty} \sigma_*$ by $\overline{\mathrm{Li}}_k^{\mathfrak{m} }(x)$ and similarly for $\log^{\mathfrak{m} }(x)$, the matrix $\overline{c_M}^{\mathfrak{m} }$ is given by $$\overline{c_M}^{\mathfrak{m} }  =
\left(
\begin{array}{ccc}
 1  & \overline{\mathrm{Li}_1^{\mathfrak{m} }}(x)   &   \overline{\mathrm{Li}_2^{\mathfrak{m} }}(x)  \\
 0 & -\mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{m} }      &    -  \mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{m} } \overline{\log^{\mathfrak{m} }}(x)      \\
  0 & 0    &    (\mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{m} })^2
\end{array}
\right) \ .$$ By computing $(\overline{c_M}^{\mathfrak{m} } )^{-1} c_M^{\mathfrak{m} }$ we find that $\mathbf{s}^{\mathfrak{m} }(\mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{dr}}) = (-1)$ (yet again), $$\mathbf{s}^{\mathfrak{m} }(\log^{\mathfrak{dr}}(x)) = \log^{\mathfrak{m} }(x) +  \overline{\log^{\mathfrak{m} }}(x)\ ,$$ and similarly for $\mathrm{Li}_1^{\mathfrak{dr}}(x) = -\log^{\mathfrak{dr}}(1-x)$. The top-right corner gives $$\mathbf{s}^{\mathfrak{m} }(\mathrm{Li}_2^{\mathfrak{dr}}(x)) = \mathrm{Li}_2^{\mathfrak{m} }(x) -    \overline{\mathrm{Li}_2^{\mathfrak{m} }}(x)  + ( \log^{\mathfrak{m} }(x) +  \overline{\log^{\mathfrak{m} }}(x))\overline{\mathrm{Li}_1^{\mathfrak{m} }}(x)  \quad \in \mathcal{P}$$ whose period is $2 i$ times the Bloch-Wigner dilogarithm. Equivalent calculations for the associated period matrices were first carried out in [@Be-De] for the classical polylogarithms. For multiple polylogarithms, the computations are made much simpler using the language of non-commutative formal power series [@SVMP].

# Symbols

We briefly indicate how a certain class of motivic periods give rise to invariants involving differential forms. There are several possible variations on this theme which all specialise (in the mixed Tate case) to the notion of 'symbol of differential forms' as currently used in the physics literature.

## Some pitfalls

The symbol is commonly understood by physicists to be a tensor product of differential forms obtained by differentiating a family of period integrals with respect to a parameter. Consider the following examples:

1.  The classical polylogarithm $\mathrm{Li}_k(x) = \sum_{n\geq 1} {x^n \over n^k}$, where $k\geq 1$, satisfies the following differential equation for all $k\geq 2$: $$d \, \mathrm{Li}_k(x) =   \mathrm{Li}_{k-1}(x)  \, {dx \over x}\ .$$ Furthermore $d \mathrm{Li}_1(x) = {dx \over 1-x}$. The recipe in the physics literature for constructing the symbol is recursive by repeated differentiation. For example, $\mathrm{symbol}(\mathrm{Li}_1(x)) = {dx \over 1-x}$ and $\mathrm{symbol} ( \mathrm{Li}_k(x)) = \mathrm{symbol} (\mathrm{Li}_{k-1}(x) )\otimes {dx \over x}$, e.g., $$\mathrm{symbol}( \mathrm{Li}_2(x)) =  {dx \over 1-x} \otimes {dx \over x}\ .$$ Occasionally, the arguments $d\log f$ in the right-hand side are represented by their arguments $f$, and the symbol of $\mathrm{Li}_2(x)$ is written $1-x \otimes x$. This notion is already very useful for capturing functional relations between polylogarithms and is ubiquitous in the literature. Note that the symbol captures the information that $\mathrm{Li}_2(x)$ is an iterated integral of two one-forms on the punctured projective line, but some information is lost: one requires a path of integration to reconstruct the function $\mathrm{Li}_2(x)$ from its symbol. The symbol is sometimes used to infer notions such as 'transcendental weight' and monodromy data about functions.

2.  Simply differentiating functions is too naive, as one sees by considering examples such as $f(x) \mathrm{Li}_2(x) + g(x) \mathrm{Li}_1(x) \log(x)$, where $f, g$ are rational functions. Repeated differentiation leads to an infinite sequence of more complicated functions. To make sense of the definition $(1)$, one must find differential operators whose application successively decreases some quantity called the 'length' (in the examples in $(1)$, this happens to coincide with the 'transcendental weight', although this will not necessarily be true in more general settings). This will depend on choices, and shows that the symbol should in fact be defined as a tensor product *modulo certain equivalence relations*. These are described in §9.3.1. This recursive structure will be encoded by the notion of a *unipotent connection*.

3.  The symbol of a constant family of periods is necessarily zero since it is defined in terms of differentiation. However, the notion of symbol in the physics literature has somehow morphed into a version in which the arguments are formally allowed to be constants. This is the basis for the definition of 'motivic amplitudes' in [@Cluster]. One finds equations such as $$\mathrm{symbol} ( \mathrm{Li}_2(3)  )  =   -2 \otimes 3  ,$$ which are obtained by specialising the earlier example to $x=3$, and possibly based on Goncharov's notion of symbol (remark 57). This notion does not go very far to capture constants, since most are zero under this map: for example, the Goncharov symbol of the quantity $\zeta(3) \mathrm{Li}_1(x)$ is zero.

4.  A further problem with the formalism mentioned in $(3)$, which is only defined in terms of de Rham classes and with no mention of Betti classes, is that the de Rham analogue of $\zeta(2)$, or $2 \pi i$, is irretrievably zero, and so attempting to take the period leads to contradictions. This problem is fixed by replacing 'coproduct' with 'coaction', which is part of the structure of our definition of families of $\mathcal{H}$-periods.

5.  There have been attempts to incorporate constants into a common 'symbolic' framework such as [@Duhr], which contains both the coaction for motivic multiple zeta values, and the symbols of polylogarithms. The main properties of this framework were mostly conjectural and shown to be equivalent to complicated combinatorial identities. We shall show how these problems can be easily overcome in our setting using the notion of 'symbol based at a point $t$', which has all the properties conjectured in [@Duhr].

Our notion of families of motivic periods fixes these problems and subsumes all of the above notions. It also generalises these concepts to situations which are non-polylogarithmic. More precisely, the notions of symbol presently found in the physics literature are derived from the coaction on families of motivic periods and discarding more or less information. One must bear in mind, however, that one side of the motivic coproduct involves de Rham periods, not $\mathcal{H}$-periods, and the former do not have canonical periods. They do, however, possess single-valued periods.

## Abstract definition of the symbol

The symbol will be defined for a certain class of motivic periods. Recall that an algebraic vector bundle with connection $(\mathcal{V}_{dR}, \nabla)$ on $S$ is *unipotent* of length $n$ if there exists a filtration $$\label{Vfilt}
 0=\mathcal{V}_{-1} \subset \mathcal{V}_0 \subset \ldots \subset \mathcal{V}_n=\mathcal{V}$$ by algebraic sub-bundles, with $\nabla: \mathcal{V}_k \rightarrow \mathcal{V}_k \otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{S}} \Omega^1_{S}$, such that each graded quotient $\mathcal{V}_k/ \mathcal{V}_{k-1}$ is isomorphic to a direct sum of trivial vector bundles $(\mathcal{O}_{S}, d)$. Such a vector bundle automatically has regular singularities at infinity. The category of unipotent algebraic vector bundles with connection forms a full Tannakian subcategory $\mathcal{A}^{un}(S)$ of $\mathcal{A}(S)$.

A local system $V$ on $S$ is *unipotent* if it admits a finite increasing filtration $$\label{Vfilt}
 0=V_{-1} \subset V_0 \subset \ldots \subset V_n=V$$ of local sub-systems, such that each graded quotient $V_k/V_{k-1}$ is trivial, i.e., constant. Equivalently, for any basepoint $s \in S(\mathbb C)$, the representation $\omega_s(V)$ of $\pi_1(S(\mathbb C), s)$ associated to $V$ is unipotent, i.e., admits a finite increasing filtration such that the associated graded quotients are trivial representations. The category of unipotent local systems forms a full Tannakian subcategory $\mathcal{L}^{un}(S)$ of $\mathcal{L}(S)$. Since the category of unipotent vector bundles with connection (resp. local systems) behaves well with respect to extensions of scalars, the Riemann-Hilbert correspondence induces an equivalence $$\mathcal{A}^{un}(S) \otimes \mathbb C\sim  \mathcal{L}^{un}(S) \otimes \mathbb C\ .$$

**Definition 46**. A family of motivic periods $\xi \in \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} , X, Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)}$ is *differentially unipotent*, or has *unipotent monodromy*, []{#gloss: diffunip label="gloss: diffunip"} if the associated vector bundle with connection (§8.1) $M(\xi)_{dR}$ is unipotent. Equivalently, $\xi$ is differentially unipotent if the associated local system on $M(\xi)_B$ is unipotent. This means that $\xi$ can be represented as a matrix coefficient $(\ref{eqnfamilymatrixcoeff})$ where one of $\mathcal{V}_B, \mathcal{V}_{dR}$ (and hence both) are unipotent.

We define a family of de Rham motivic periods $\xi \in \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{dr}, Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)}$ to be *differentially unipotent* in an identical manner. It follows immediately from the definition that the motivic coaction $(\ref{H(S)coaction})$ preserves the quality of unipotency.

Note that the filtration involved in the definition of differential unipotency will not in general be the weight filtration.

The above categories give rise to Tannaka group schemes over $\mathbb Q$: $$\begin{aligned}
 \pi_1^{un} (S(\mathbb C) ,s )  &= &  \mathrm{Aut}^{\otimes}_{\mathcal{L}^{un}(S)}(\omega_s) \qquad \hbox{ for } s \in S(\mathbb C) \ ,  \nonumber \\
 \pi_1^{dR} (S ,s ) &  = &  \mathrm{Aut}^{\otimes}_{\mathcal{A} ^{un}(S)}(\omega_s)  \qquad \hbox{ for } s \in S(\mathbb Q) \ .   \nonumber

\end{aligned}$$ The first group is the unipotent or Malčev completion of the topological fundamental group, the second is the de Rham fundamental group. They are naturally quotients of the Betti algebraic and de Rham algebraic fundamental groups.

A family of motivic or de Rham periods is differentially unipotent if and only if the natural actions by the Betti algebraic fundamental group, de Rham algebraic fundamental group, or topological fundamental group factor through their unipotent quotients. It follows that for a differentially unipotent de Rham period, its image under the dual of the natural map $(\ref{restrict2algdR})$: $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{dr}, Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)} = \mathcal{O}( G^{dR}_{\mathcal{H} (S),  Y})  & \longrightarrow&  \mathcal{O}(  \pi_1^{dR, \mathrm{alg}} (S, \omega^{ Y}_{dR}) )\ , \nonumber \\
{ [}(\mathcal{V}_B, \mathcal{V}_{dR}, c), v, \omega]^{\mathfrak{dr}} & \mapsto&   (\alpha \mapsto v(\alpha(\omega))) \nonumber \ ,
\end{aligned}$$ where $\alpha \in  \pi_1^{dR, \mathrm{alg}} (S, \omega^{ Y}_{dR})$, actually lands in the unipotent subspace $$\mathcal{O}(  \pi_1^{dR} (S, \omega^{ Y}_{dR} )) \quad  \subset  \quad \mathcal{O}(  \pi_1^{dR, \mathrm{alg}} (S, \omega^{ Y}_{dR})) \ .$$ Next restrict to the affine open $\mathrm{Spec} \,(\mathcal{O}_{S, Y})$ of $S$, which gives a map on affine rings $$\mathcal{O}(  \pi_1^{dR} (S, \omega^{ Y}_{dR})) \longrightarrow\mathcal{O}(  \pi_1^{dR} (\mathrm{Spec} \,\mathcal{O}_{S, Y}, \omega^{ Y}_{dR}))\ .$$ Finally it follows either from Chen's $\pi_1$-de Rham theorem (over the complex numbers), or from the universal properties of the reduced bar construction $\mathbb{B}$ (whose zero'th cohomology is the universal unipotent extension of $\mathcal{O}_{S, Y}$), that the affine ring of the de Rham fundamental group on $\mathrm{Spec} \,\mathcal{O}_{S, Y}$ is described explicitly: $$\mathcal{O}(  \pi_1^{dR} (\mathrm{Spec} \,\mathcal{O}_{S, Y}, \omega^{ Y}_{dR})) \cong H^0 (\mathbb{B}(\Omega_{S, Y})) \ .$$ We shall recall the relevant definitions below.

**Definition 47**. We shall define the *symbol* []{#gloss: symbol label="gloss: symbol"} of a differentially unipotent family of de Rham periods $\xi \in \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{dr},  Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)}$ to be its image: $$\label{symboldefinition}
 \mathrm{smb}(\xi)   \in H^0(\mathbb{B}(\Omega_{S, Y})) \ .$$ There is a natural *length filtration* on $H^0(\mathbb{B}(\Omega_{S, Y}))$. The *length* of $\xi$ is bounded above by the length $n$ in the filtration $(\ref{Vfilt})$.

This a generalisation of the notion of symbol as used by physicists. Our abstract definition of the symbol could be generalised further still. For example, one could also consider relative unipotent completion instead of unipotent completion.

## Computing the symbol

We now explain how to compute the symbol in the spirit of the recursive differentiation procedure described in §9.1 $(1)$. Consider a differentially unipotent de Rham period $\xi = [(\mathcal{V}_B, \mathcal{V}_{dR}, c),  f, \omega]^{\mathfrak{dr}}$ in $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{dr}, Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)}$, where $f \in \omega_{ Y}(\mathcal{V}_{dR})^{\vee}$ and $\omega \in \omega_{ Y}(\mathcal{V}_{dR})$, and let $\mathcal{V}$ denote the pull-back of $\mathcal{V}_{dR}$ to $\mathrm{Spec} \,\mathcal{O}_{S, Y}$. We shall assume that $H_{dR}^0 (\mathcal{O}_{S, Y})=\mathbb Q$. Since $\xi$ is differentially unipotent, we can assume by equivalence of matrix coefficients that $\mathcal{V}_{dR}$ and hence $\mathcal{V}$ is unipotent. There is a filtration $$\label{Vfilt2}
 0=\mathcal{V}_{-1} \subset \mathcal{V}_0 \subset \ldots \subset \mathcal{V}_n=\mathcal{V}$$ by algebraic sub-bundles such that $\nabla: \mathcal{V}_k \rightarrow \mathcal{V}_k \otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{S, Y}} \Omega^1_{S, Y}$, and with respect to which $\nabla$ is unipotent. It splits because $H^1(\mathrm{Spec} \,\mathcal{O}_{S, Y}, \mathcal{O}_{S, Y}) =0$ since $\mathrm{Spec} \,\mathcal{O}_{S, Y}$ is affine and $\mathcal{O}_{S, Y}$ coherent. Choose a splitting of this filtration: $$\label{splitchoose}
  \mathcal{V}\cong \mathrm{gr}\,  \mathcal{V}\ .$$ The associated graded $(\mathrm{gr}\,  \mathcal{V}, \mathrm{gr}\, \nabla)$ is a direct sum of trivial vector bundles $(\mathcal{O}_{S, Y}, d)$, and $\mathrm{gr}\, \mathcal{V}\cong \mathcal{O}_{S, Y}\otimes_{\mathbb Q} H^0(\mathrm{gr}\, \mathcal{V}, \mathrm{gr}\, \nabla)$ has a $\mathbb Q$-structure given by flat sections. With respect to this choice of splitting, the connection map $$N= \nabla - d \quad \in \quad \mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{S, Y}}(     \mathcal{V}, \mathcal{V}\otimes_{\mathbb Q} \Omega^1_{S, Y})$$ satisfies the integrability condition $$d N +  N \wedge  N=0\ ,$$ which is equivalent to $\nabla^2=0$, and it also satisfies $N^m=0$ for some $m$ by the assumption of unipotency. It is an $\mathcal{O}_{S,  Y}$-linear operator, where $\mathcal{V}$ and $\mathcal{V}\otimes_{\mathbb Q} \Omega^1_{S, Y}$ are left $\mathcal{O}_{S, Y}$-modules. For computations, let us write $V_k = \Gamma( \mathcal{O}_{S, Y}, \mathcal{V}_k)$. Then $V_k/V_{k-1}$ is a free $\mathcal{O}_{S, Y}$-module and it follows by induction that the $V_k$ are free $\mathcal{O}_{S, Y}$-modules too. We can choose a basis of $V_n$ which is adapted to the filtration $V_k$, and is flat on the graded quotients $\mathrm{gr}\, V$, i.e., a basis element $e$ of $V_k$ satisfies $\nabla(e) \subset V_{k-1}$. Write the connection as $\nabla = d+ N$ in this basis. Thus $N$ is represented as an upper-triangular matrix of one-forms. Consider the element $$\mathrm{smb}_N (\xi) = \sum_{k \geq 0 } \langle f, N^k \omega\rangle   \quad \in \quad \mathcal{O}_{S, Y}\otimes_{\mathbb Q}  T^c(\Omega^1_{S, Y}) \ ,$$ where we recall that the tensor coalgebra $T^c(\Omega^1_{S, Y}) = \bigoplus_{k\geq 0} ( \Omega^1_{S, Y})^{\otimes k}$, and tensors are over $\mathbb Q$. It depends on the choice of splitting $(\ref{splitchoose})$ (resp. choice of basis of $V$).

One can think of the symbol in the following way. View $N$ as an $n\times n$ matrix with coefficients in $T^c(\Omega^1_{S,\mathcal{U}})$, since its entries can be considered as tensors of length one. Consider the following matrix $$\label{seriesinN} 1 + N+ N^2 + N^3 +  \ldots \qquad \in M_{n\times n}( T^c(\Omega^1_{S,\mathcal{T} }))\ ,$$ where the multiplication of matrix entries is given by the (non-commutative) concatenation product in $T^c(\Omega^1_{S,\mathcal{U}})$. The series is finite by the nilpotence of $N$. The vector $\omega$ and covector $f$ define an entry of this matrix, which is exactly $\mathrm{smb}_N(\xi)$.

### Reduced bar construction

Define an internal differential $$d_I :  T^c(\Omega^{\bullet} _{S, Y})   \rightarrow   T^c(\Omega^{\bullet}_{S, Y})$$ by $$\begin{gathered}
 d_I {[}\omega_1| \ldots | \omega_n ]  =  \sum_{i=1}^n (-1)^i [ j \omega_1| \ldots |j\omega_{i-1} | d\omega_i | \omega_{i+1} |  \ldots | \omega_n]  \nonumber \\  +  \sum_{i=1}^{n-1} (-1)^{i+1} [ j \omega_1| \ldots | j \omega_{i-1} | j\omega_i \wedge \omega_{i+1} | \omega_{i+2}| \ldots | \omega_n] \ . \nonumber
\end{gathered}$$ where $j$ acts on $\Omega^n_{S, Y}$ by $(-1)^n$. Define a grading on $T^c(\Omega^{\bullet}_{S, Y})$ by $$\deg \, {[}\omega_1| \ldots | \omega_n ]  = \sum_{i=1}^n \deg(\omega_i)-1 \ .$$ Consider the linear map $d_C: T^c(\Omega^{\bullet} _{S, Y})   \rightarrow  \mathcal{O}_S \otimes_{\mathbb Q} T^c(\Omega^{\bullet}_{S, Y})$ defined by $$d_C   {[} \omega_1 | \ldots | \omega_n ] =  - \varepsilon(\omega_1) [ \omega_2| \ldots | \omega_n] +  (-1)^{\nu} \varepsilon(\omega_n) [\omega_1 | \ldots | \omega_{n-1}]$$ where $\varepsilon: \Omega^{\bullet}_{S, Y} \rightarrow \mathcal{O}_{S, Y}$ is projection onto degree $0$ and $\nu$ is given by $(\deg(\omega_n)-1)\deg [\omega_1|\ldots |\omega_{n-1}]$. One verifies that $$d=\mathrm{id} \otimes d_I+\mathrm{id} \otimes d_C$$ satisfies $d^2=0$. See, for example, the presentation in [@HainZucker] (3.4). Note that the signs simplify drastically when all $\omega_i$ are of degree one, which is the case we are mainly interested in. Consider the smallest subspace $\mathcal{R}$ in $\mathcal{O}_{S}\otimes_{\mathbb Q} T^c(\Omega_{S, Y})$ generated by $$\mathcal{R} : \qquad \qquad [\omega_1 | \ldots |\omega_i | f | \omega_{i+1} | \ldots | \omega_n]$$ where $f \in \mathcal{O}_{S, Y}$, and stable under the differential $d$. The quotient of $\mathcal{O}_{S, Y}\otimes_{\mathbb Q} T^c(\Omega_{S, Y})$ by $\mathcal{R}$ is a complex which we denote by $\mathbb{B}(\Omega^{\bullet}_{S, Y})$. It is a close relative of Chen's reduced circular bar complex[^11] on $\Omega_{S, Y}$.

**Example 48**. If $f\in \mathcal{O}_{S, Y}$ and $\omega \in \Omega^1_{S, Y}$ is closed we have: $$\begin{aligned}
  d [ f | \omega] & = & - [df | \omega]  + [f\omega] - f[\omega]  \nonumber \\
d [ \omega | f] & =&   - [\omega | df] - [f\omega] + f [\omega] \ . \nonumber
\end{aligned}$$ The expressions on the right-hand side are therefore in $\mathcal{R}$.

### The symbol

The integrability of $N$ implies that the element $\mathrm{smb}_N(\xi)$ is *integrable*, i.e., is in the kernel of the differential $d$ in $\mathbb{B}(\Omega^{\bullet}_{S, Y})$. Furthermore, it is of degree zero. Its cohomology class $$\mathrm{smb}(\xi)   \in H^0(\mathbb{B}(\Omega_{S, Y})) \ ,$$ is exactly the symbol as defined in $(\ref{symboldefinition})$. It is true, but not obvious from the computational procedure defined above, that the cohomology class of $\mathrm{smb}(\xi)$ is well-defined, i.e., independent of the choice of $N$. The integrability of $\mathrm{smb}(\xi)$ can be seen directly by writing the matrix $(\ref{seriesinN})$ in the form $$[1] + [N] + [N| N] + \ldots \   .$$ The equation $dN + N\wedge N =0$ implies that it lies in the kernel of the differential $d$ applied formally to the previous expression in the obvious way.

*Remark 49*. The bar complex is equipped with a shuffle product (with signs) which is compatible with $d$, and this induces a commutative algebra structure on its cohomology. It follows from the definition $(\ref{symboldefinition})$ that the symbol is a homomorphism: $$\mathrm{smb}(\xi_1 \xi_2) = \mathrm{smb}(\xi_1) \, \hbox{\rus x} \,\mathrm{smb}(\xi_2)\  .$$ The shuffle product restricted to $H^0(\mathbb{B}(\Omega_{S, Y}))$ has no signs.

**Example 50**. Suppose that $\mathrm{gr}\,  \mathcal{V}$ has length two ($n=2$), so $$0=\mathcal{V}_{-1}  \subset \mathcal{V}_0 \subset \mathcal{V}_1 \subset \mathcal{V}_2 = \mathcal{V}$$ and that each graded quotient $\mathcal{V}_k / \mathcal{V}_{k-1}$ for $k=0,1,2$ is a rank one $\mathcal{O}_{S, Y}$-module. Choose a basis $e_0,e_1,e_2$ of $V = \Gamma(\mathcal{O}_{S, Y}, \mathcal{V})$ such that $\nabla e_0=0$, $\nabla e_1 =   e_0 \otimes \omega_1$ and $\nabla e_2 = e_1 \otimes  \omega_2 + e_0  \otimes \omega_{12}$. In this basis, the matrix $N$ is $$N=
\left(
\begin{array}{ccc}
0  &    \omega_1 & \omega_{12}   \\
 0 & 0  &  \omega_2  \\
  0& 0  &0
\end{array}
\right)\ .$$ For this to define a connection (or equivalently, $dN + N\wedge N=0$), we must assume that $\omega_1,\omega_2$ are closed, and $d\omega_{12} + \omega_1 \wedge \omega_2 =0$. If $\xi = [\mathcal{V}, e_0^{\vee}, e_2]^{\mathfrak{dr}}$, then $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathrm{smb}_N(\xi)  & =  & \langle e_0^{\vee}, e_2\rangle + \langle e_0^{\vee}, N e_2 \rangle + \langle e_0^{\vee}, N^2 e_2\rangle \nonumber \\
& = & \quad  0 \quad +  \qquad  \omega_{12}   \quad  +   \langle e_0^{\vee}, N e_1 \rangle \otimes   \omega_2 +   \langle e_0^{\vee}, N e_0 \rangle   \otimes \omega_{12} \nonumber \\
& = &  0 +  \omega_{12}  + \omega_1 \otimes \omega_2 + 0 \ .\nonumber
\end{aligned}$$ In bar notation, this is denoted by: $$\mathrm{smb}_N( \xi )= [\omega_1| \omega_2] + [\omega_{12}]\ .$$ Now change basis to $e_0',e_1',e_2'$ where $e_0'=e_0$, $e_1'= e_1$ and $e_2'= e_2+f e_1$, where $f \in \mathcal{O}_{S, Y}$. In the new basis, the matrix $N$ is replaced by $$N'=
\left(
\begin{array}{ccc}
0  &    \omega_1 & \omega_{12}  +f\omega_1 \\
 0 & 0  &  \omega_2 +df   \\
  0& 0  &0
\end{array}
\right)\ ,$$ and since $e_2 = e_2' - f e_1$ one checks by a similar computation that $$\mathrm{smb}_{N'}(\xi) = [\omega_1 | \omega_2 + df] +[\omega_{12} + f\omega_1] - f[ \omega_1] \ .$$ The difference between the two elements $$\mathrm{smb}_{N'}(\xi) - \mathrm{smb}_N(\xi) =   [\omega_1| df] + [f \omega_1 ] - f [\omega_1]$$ which is exactly a boundary $- d ([\omega_1 | f])$, by example 48.

### Variants

The above construction was defined for de Rham periods but can be embellished in any number of ways. Let $\xi \in \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} (S),  X, Y}$ be differentially unipotent. Then using the coaction $(\ref{H(S)coaction})$ we can define $$(\mathrm{id} \otimes \mathrm{smb})  \Delta(\xi)  \quad  \in \quad    \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,  X, Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{S, Y}} H^0(\mathbb{B}(\Omega_{S, Y})) \ .$$ A further possibility is to introduce a base point as follows. Suppose that $t\in S(\mathbb Q)$, whose image in $S(\mathbb C)$ lies in $X\cap Y$ so that the evaluation $(\ref{eqn: specialisation})$ is defined. Let $\xi \in \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,  X, Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)}$ be differentially unipotent, and define the symbol 'based at $t$' by []{#gloss: symbolatt label="gloss: symbolatt"} $$\mathrm{smb}_t(\xi) = (\mathrm{ev}_t \otimes \mathrm{smb}) \Delta (\xi)  \qquad \in \qquad  \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} } \otimes_{\mathbb Q} H^0(\mathbb{B}(\Omega_{S, Y}))\ .$$ This notion captures constants and satisfies similar properties to the symbol defined above. See for [@Duhr] for applications of such a construction.

*Remark 51*. Suppose that $\xi$ is a period of a mixed Tate variation. By this we mean that we can write $\xi = [ (\mathcal{V}_B, \mathcal{V}_{dR},c), \sigma, \omega]^{\mathfrak{m} }$, where $\mathrm{gr}_{n}^W (\mathcal{V}_B, \mathcal{V}_{dR},c)$ is zero if $n$ is odd, and isomorphic to a direct sum of constant Tate elements $\mathbb Q(-k)_{/S}$ if $n=2k$ is even (images of the pull-back of the Tate objects $\mathbb Q(-k)$ in $\mathcal{H}$ along the structural map $\pi: S \rightarrow \mathrm{Spec} \,(\mathbb Q)$). The connection underlying $\mathbb Q(-k)_{/S}$ is isomorphic to $(\mathcal{O}_{S}, d)$ and is trivial. It follows that the element $\xi$ is automatically differentially unipotent with respect to the weight filtration $\mathcal{V}_n = W_{2n} \mathcal{V}_{dR}$. If, furthermore, $\xi$ is effective then we can apply a version of the projection map of §$\ref{sectProjection}$ to associate to $\xi$ a de Rham period $\xi^{\mathfrak{dr}}$, which is necessarily unipotent, and take its symbol $\mathrm{smb}(\xi^{\mathfrak{dr}})$.

Thus we have shown that de Rham, and effective mixed Tate periods, always have symbols. All the examples used in physics seem to be of this special type.

## Cohomological symbol

The bar complex is somewhat cumbersome. We can define a coarser version of a symbol of length $n$ by passing to the associated length-graded of the bar complex.

**Definition 52**. Let $\xi \in \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{dr}, Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)}$ be a differentially unipotent de Rham period of length $\leq n$. Recall that this means the filtration $(\ref{Vfilt})$ satisfies $\mathcal{V}_n=\mathcal{V}$. Define its *cohomological symbol* in length $n$ to be its class $$\mathrm{cmb}_n(\xi)  = [\mathrm{smb}(\xi)] \quad \in \quad \mathrm{gr}^{\ell}_n  H^0(\mathbb{B}(\Omega_{S, Y}))\ ,$$ where $\ell$ denotes the length filtration.

An Eilenberg-Moore spectral sequence implies that the associated graded for the length filtration $\mathrm{gr}^{\ell}  H^0(\mathbb{B}(\Omega_{S, Y})) \cong H^0 (\mathbb{B}(H (\Omega_{S,  Y})))$ is the bar complex on the cohomology of $\Omega_{S,  Y}$, equipped with the trivial differential. Therefore $$\mathrm{cmb}_n (\xi)  \in  \mathcal{O}_{S, Y} \otimes_{\mathbb Q} H^1(  \Omega_{S, Y} ) ^{\otimes n} \ ,$$ and lies in the kernel of the map $$\begin{aligned}
 \label{cohommap}
H^1(  \Omega_{S, Y} ) ^{\otimes n}   &\longrightarrow&  \bigoplus_{k}  H^1(  \Omega_{S, Y} ) ^{\otimes k-1} \otimes_{\mathbb Q} H^2( \Omega_{S, Y}) \otimes_{\mathbb Q} H^1(  \Omega_{S, Y} ) ^{\otimes n-k-1}  \nonumber  \\ \qquad \qquad
{[}\omega_1 | \ldots | \omega_n ] & \mapsto & \sum_k [ \omega_1 | \ldots  | \omega_{k-1} | \omega_{k} \wedge \omega_{k+1}  | \omega_{k+2} |  \ldots | \omega_n] \ .
\end{aligned}$$

**Example 53**. The cohomological symbol is the length $n$ part of the symbol, after replacing forms with their cohomology classes. In our example $\ref{example symbol}$ it gives $$\mathrm{cmb}_2(\xi) = \big[ [\omega_1] \big| [\omega_2]\big]\ .$$ This lies in the kernel of $(\ref{cohommap})$ since $[\omega_1] \wedge [\omega_2] = [\omega_1 \wedge \omega_2 ] = [-d \omega_{12}]=0$.

This invariant can be computed directly and more simply in the following way. With the previous notations, consider the operator $$\overline{N} = \mathrm{gr}_{\bullet} \,  (\nabla- d)  : \mathrm{gr}_{\bullet} \,  \mathcal{V}\longrightarrow\mathrm{gr}_{\bullet-1} \mathcal{V}\otimes_{\mathbb Q} \Omega^1_{S, Y}\ .$$ Iterating it defines an operator $$(\overline{N})^{\otimes n} : \mathrm{gr}_n \mathcal{V}\longrightarrow\mathrm{gr}_0 \mathcal{V}\otimes_{\mathbb Q} ( \Omega^1_{S, Y})^{\otimes n}\ .$$ The vector $\omega$ defines a section in the associated graded via the map $\mathcal{V}\rightarrow \mathrm{gr}_n \mathcal{V}$. Similarly, since $\mathcal{V}_{-1}=0$, we have $\mathrm{gr}_0 \mathcal{V}=  \mathcal{V}_0 \subset \mathcal{V}$, and we can consider the image of the covector $f$ along the dual map $\mathcal{V}^{\vee} \rightarrow \mathrm{gr}_0 \mathcal{V}^{\vee}$. Now consider $$\label{csmbdef} \langle f,  (\overline{N})^{\otimes n} \omega\rangle \quad \in \quad  \mathcal{O}_{S, Y} \otimes_{\mathbb Q} ( \Omega^1_{S, Y})^{\otimes n}\ .$$ Because $dN+N \wedge N =0$, it follows that $d \overline{N}=0$ and $\overline{N} \wedge \overline{N} =0$.

**Examples 54**. Let $S = \mathbb P^n  \backslash D$ where $D = \cup_{i=0}^m D_i$ is a union of $m+1\geq 1$ distinct hyperplanes over $\mathbb Q$ and $Y = S(\mathbb C)$. Let $f_i=0$ be an equation of $D_i$, where $f_i \in \mathbb Q[x_0,\ldots, x_n]$ is homogeneous of degree one. A basis for $H_{dR}^1(S)$ is given by the cohomology classes of forms $$\omega_i  =  d  \log \Big({f_i\over f_0} \Big) \qquad \hbox{ for }  1 \leq i \leq m\ .$$ A (cohomological) symbol is simply a linear combination of tensor products of $\omega_i$ which satisfies the integrability condition $(\ref{cohommap})$. Since this case is mixed Tate, the length filtration on the bar construction coincides with (one half of) the weight filtration, and is canonically split by the Hodge filtration. It follows either from this, or from formality of the cohomology of a hypersurface complement [@BrENS] §3.2, that $H^0(\mathbb{B}(\Omega^{\bullet}_{S})) \cong H^0(\mathbb{B}(H^{\bullet}(\Omega_S))$ and so there is no significant difference between symbols $\mathrm{smb}$ and their cohomological versions $\mathrm{cmb}_n$. They are integrable words in the one-forms $\omega_i$ (resp. their cohomology classes $[\omega_i]$).[^12] In the case $n=1$, the integrability condition $(\ref{cohommap})$ is trivially satisfied. Example: $$\mathrm{smb}(\mathrm{Li}_{n}^{\mathfrak{dr}}(x)) = [d\log (1-x)  |   d \log x | \ldots | d \log x]\ .$$ This setting covers much of the recent work of physicists on symbols. Furthermore, using the motivic fundamental group of such a space [@DeGo] 4.12, one can define a notion of motivic iterated integrals.

*Remark 55*. The theory of iterated integrals enables us to construct a map in the opposite direction and associate a family of motivic periods to a symbol together with some extra data. This discussion would take us too far afield. A full treatment should also incorporate the mixed Hodge structure on the reduced bar construction [@Ha] and a discussion of admissible variations and tangential base points.

It is important to remark that symbols *do not* have periods in their own right: to define periods one requires a path of integration, or at the very least a base-point (if one only wishes to define the associated single-valued periods).

# Some geometric examples {#sect:  geometric examples}

Let $S$ be as in §7 and let $\pi: S \rightarrow \mathrm{Spec} \,(\mathbb Q)$ be the structural map. Denote the Tate variation on $S$ by $\mathbb Q(-n)_{/S}$. It is the object of $\mathcal{H} (S)$ which is defined by $\pi^* \mathbb Q(-n)$. Concretely, it is the triple $(\mathcal{V}_B, \mathcal{V}_{dR}, c)$ where $\mathcal{V}_{dR} = (\mathcal{O}_{S}, d)$ is the trivial vector bundle with trivial connection; $\mathcal{V}_B$ is the constant local system $\mathbb Q$; and $c :\mathbb C= (\mathcal{V}_{dR}^{an})^{\nabla} \overset{\sim}{\rightarrow} \mathcal{V}_B \otimes \mathbb C=\mathbb C$ is multiplication by $(2 \pi i)^{n}$. It has weight $2n$, and the Hodge filtration satisfies $F^{n} \mathcal{V}_{dR}= \mathcal{V}_{dR}$, $F^{n+1} \mathcal{V}_{dR}=0$.

## Mixed Tate motives over number fields

In the following example, let $S= \mathrm{Spec} \,(F)$ for $F$ a number field. Since $S(\mathbb C)= \mathrm{Hom}(F,\mathbb C)$, it is not geometrically connected and therefore does not immediately fit into the framework of the previous paragraphs. However, one can define a category $H(S)$ of realisations without difficulty. An element of $H(S)$ consists of: $V_{dR} \in \mathrm{Vec}_{F}$ a vector space with the zero connexion; $\mathcal{V}_B$ a collection of vector spaces $V_{\sigma} \in \mathrm{Vec}_\mathbb Q$ for every $\sigma \in S(\mathbb C)$; and a comparison $c= (c_{\sigma})_{\sigma}$, where $c_{\sigma}: V_{dR} \otimes_{F,\sigma} \mathbb C\cong V_{\sigma} \otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathbb C$ for every $\sigma\in S(\mathbb C)$, together with filtrations $W, F$ defined as before. There are Frobenius isomorphisms $F_{\infty} : V_{\sigma} \cong V_{\overline{\sigma}}$ which are compatible with the comparison isomorphisms via the action of complex conjugation. For example, the object $\mathbb Q(0)_{/S}$ is the triple $(\{V_\sigma\}, V_{dR}, c)$ where $V_{dR} = F$, and $V_{\sigma}= \mathbb Q$ for each $\sigma$, and the isomorphisms $c_{\sigma}$ are the canonical ones. Taking $\alpha \in F$, and $\tau: F \hookrightarrow \mathbb C$, we can view algebraic numbers as $H(S)$-periods $$\alpha^{\mathfrak{m} ,\tau} = [ \mathbb Q(0)_{/S} , \tau, \alpha]^{\mathfrak{m} } \in \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} , \tau, \mathrm{Spec} \,\, F}_{H(S)}\ .$$ Its period $\mathrm{per}(\alpha^{\mathfrak{m} ,\tau})=\tau(\alpha) \in \mathbb C$. Note that the motivic Galois action is trivial on $\alpha^{\mathfrak{m} ,\tau}$, since $\alpha^{\mathfrak{m} }$ is viewed as a family of periods over $F$ (but observe that if $F$ is Galois, the Galois action on algebraic numbers could be retrieved by considering the action of the automorphism group of $\mathrm{Spec} \,(F)$).

Now consider the category $\mathcal{MT}(\mathcal{O})$ of mixed Tate motives over a ring of integers $\mathcal{O}$ in $F$ defined in [@DeGo]. The de Rham fiber functor is $\omega_{dR}: \mathcal{MT}(\mathcal{O}) \rightarrow \mathrm{Vec}_{F}$, and there is a Betti fiber functor $\omega_{\sigma}: \mathcal{MT}(\mathcal{O}) \rightarrow \mathrm{Vec}_{\mathbb Q}$ for every $\sigma \in S(\mathbb C)$. Hence there is a functor $\mathcal{MT}(\mathcal{O}) \rightarrow H(S)$, which is known to be fully faithful [@DeGo]. In this manner, we can view the periods of $\mathcal{MT}(\mathcal{O})$ as families of periods over $\mathrm{Spec} \,(F)$.[^13]

However, in this, mixed Tate, situation, the de Rham functor is in fact obtained from a canonical fiber functor $\omega: \mathcal{MT}(\mathcal{O}) \rightarrow \mathrm{Vec}_{\mathbb Q}$ by extension of scalars $\omega_{dR} = \omega \otimes_{\mathbb Q} F$. This leads to a slightly different point of view. Define, for every $\sigma \in S(\mathbb C)$, a ring of motivic periods $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} _{\sigma}}_{\mathcal{MT}(\mathcal{O})}$ over $\mathbb Q$ where $\mathfrak{m} _{\sigma}$ is the pair of fiber functors $(\omega_{\sigma}, \omega)$ in the manner of §2.3. It is spanned by matrix coefficients $[M, x, v]^{\mathfrak{m} }$ where $M \in \mathcal{MT}(\mathcal{O})$, $x\in M_{\sigma}^{\vee}$ and $v \in \omega(M)$. Every automorphism $\alpha \in \mathrm{Gal}(\overline{\mathbb Q}/\mathbb Q)$ defines an isomorphism $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} _{\sigma}}_{\mathcal{MT}(\mathcal{O})} \cong \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} _{\sigma\alpha}}_{\mathcal{MT}(\mathcal{O})}$ via its action on the Betti class.

Following an identical argument to theorem §32, we deduce the following

**Theorem 56**. *The decomposition map gives a canonical isomorphism $$\label{PhiforMT}
 \Phi: \mathrm{gr}^C \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} _{\sigma},  +}_{\mathcal{MT}(\mathcal{O})} \overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow} \mathbb Q[\mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{m} }] \otimes_{\mathbb Q} T^c(\bigoplus_n K_{2n-1}(\mathcal{O}) \otimes_{\mathbb Z} \mathbb Q(-n)_{dR})$$ in an identical manner to §6.6, which describes the structure of its motivic periods. There is a de Rham version of the previous isomorphism: $$\label{dRPhiforMT} \mathrm{gr}^C \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{dr}}_{\mathcal{MT}(\mathcal{O})} \overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow} \mathbb Q[\mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{dr}}, (\mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{dr}})^{-1}] \otimes_{\mathbb Q} T^c(\bigoplus_n K_{2n-1}(\mathcal{O}) \otimes_{\mathbb Z} \mathbb Q(-n)_{dR})$$ where $\mathfrak{dr}$ means $(\omega, \omega)$, for $\omega$ the canonical fiber functor. The projection map $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} _{\sigma},  +}_{\mathcal{MT}(\mathcal{O})} \rightarrow
 \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{dr}}_{\mathcal{MT}(\mathcal{O})}$ corresponds to the homomorphism $\mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{m} } \rightarrow 0$.*

*Remark 57*. Goncharov considered the image (of what, in our language would be a $U_{\mathcal{MT}(\mathcal{O})}^{dR}$-period) in the de Rham version of $(\ref{dRPhiforMT})$ in the quotient $$T^c (   K_1(F) \otimes_{\mathbb Z} \mathbb Q(-1)) \cong \bigoplus_{n\geq 0} (K_1(F) \otimes_{\mathbb Z} \mathbb Q)^{\otimes n} = \bigoplus_{n\geq 0} (F^{\times})^{\otimes n}\ ,$$ (see discussion preceding lemma 3.7 in [@GoMTM]). One can ignore the coradical grading in this case, since in this particular quotient it is equivalent to the weight-grading (this is false in general). Thus Goncharov's notion of symbol is the homomorphism $$\mathcal{O}(U_{\mathcal{MT}(\mathcal{O})}^{dR} ) \longrightarrow\bigoplus_{n\geq 0} (F^{\times})^{\otimes n}\ .$$ This map has a huge kernel and loses most of the information about periods. For example, if $\mathcal{O}= \mathbb Z$, then this homomorphism is identically zero and all (unipotent de Rham) multiple zeta values map to zero.

A version of this notion of symbol for variations, defined in [@GoSymbol], §1.3, is used in the physics literature, and the 'motivic amplitude' considered in [@Cluster] is defined by extrapolation as an element of $(F^{\times})^{\otimes n}$ for $F$ a certain field. For the reasons above, this notion loses information about periods and does not apply in the non-mixed Tate case. It is not to be confused with the notion of motivic periods defined here.

## A family of examples

The following family of examples is sufficient for the purposes of [@Cosmic]. Let $D\subset X$ be a family of simple normal crossing divisors relative to a smooth morphism $\pi: X\rightarrow S$, where $S$ smooth over $\mathbb Q$ and geometrically connected. Furthermore, we assume that $\pi$ is topologically trivial on the underlying analytic varieties (it is a locally trivial fibration of stratified varieties, according to [@Go-Ma]). Let $j: X\backslash D \hookrightarrow X$ be the inclusion. Define an object $H^n(X, D)_{/S}$ in the category $\mathcal{H} (S)$ as follows. Its Betti realisation is $$H^n_B(X,D)_{/S} = R^n \pi_* j_{!} \mathbb Q$$ where $\mathbb Q$ is the constant sheaf on $(X\backslash D)(\mathbb C)$. Since $\pi$ is topologically trivial, this is a local system, and its fibres at a point $s\in S(\mathbb C)$ are $H^n_B(X_s,D_s)$, where $X_s, D_s$ denote the fibres of $X,D$. For de Rham, denote the irreducible components of $D$ by $D_i$, for $i\in I$, where $I$ is an ordered set, and write $D_J = \cap_{j\in J} D_i$ for any $\emptyset \neq J\subset I$. Consider the double complex of sheaves of relative differential forms on $X$ $$\Omega^{\bullet}_{D_{\bullet}/S} : \qquad\qquad \Omega^{\bullet}_{X/S} \longrightarrow\bigoplus_{|J|=1} \Omega^{\bullet}_{D_J/S} \longrightarrow\ldots \longrightarrow\bigoplus_{|J|=n} \Omega^{\bullet}_{D_J/S}$$ where the horizontal maps are determined by the usual rule: if $i_j$ denotes the inclusion of $D_{J\backslash \{i_j\} } \hookrightarrow D_{J }$, where $i_j$ is the $k\mathrm{th}$ element of $J$ then $i_j^*$ occurs with the sign $(-1)^k$. The $\Omega^{\bullet}_{D_J/S}$ denote the direct images of the corresponding sheaves on $D_J$, and vanish outside $D_J$.

Define the de Rham realisation by $$H^n_{dR}(X,D)_{/S} = \mathbb{R}^n \pi_* ( \Omega^{\bullet}_{D_{\bullet}/S})\ .$$ It is the sheaf associated to the presheaf whose sections over an affine open $U\subset S$ are the hypercohomology of $\Omega^{\bullet}_{D_{\bullet}/S}  (\pi^{-1}(U))$. It is a locally free sheaf of $\mathcal{O}_S$-modules and its fibres at the point $s$ are the relative de Rham cohomology groups: $$(H^n_{dR}(X,D)_{/S} )(s) =  H^n_{dR}(X_s,D_s)\ .$$ It admits an integrable connection $\nabla$ by a relative version of [@Katz-Oda]. To check the comparison isomorphism, denote by $\mathbb Q_J$ the constant sheaf $\mathbb Q$ on $D_J(\mathbb C)$, extended by zero to the whole of $X(\mathbb C)$. The complex of sheaves $$\mathbb Q_{D_{\bullet}/S} : \qquad \qquad  \mathbb Q\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{|J|=1} \mathbb Q_{J} \longrightarrow\bigoplus_{|J|=2} \mathbb Q_{J} \longrightarrow\ldots \longrightarrow\bigoplus_{|J|=n} \mathbb Q_{J}$$ where the sign conventions are exactly as defined for the complex $\Omega^{\bullet}_{D_{\bullet}/S}$, defines a resolution of $j_!\mathbb Q$. The analytification $\Omega^{\bullet,an}_{D_{\bullet}/S}$ of $\Omega^{\bullet}_{D_{\bullet}/S}$ is a resolution of $\mathbb Q_{D_{\bullet}/S}\otimes \mathbb C$ over $S^{an}(\mathbb C)$. Using the triviality of $\pi$, and arguing as in [@De4], Proposition 2.28, there is a natural isomorphism $$c^{-1}: H^n_B(X,D)_{/S} {\otimes_{\mathbb Q}} \mathcal{O}_S^{an} \overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow} (H^n_{dR}(X,D)_{/S})^{an}\ .$$ It is known that $H^n_B(X,D)_{/S}$, equipped with its weight filtration and Hodge filtration from $c H^n_{dR}(X,D)_{/S}$, is a variation of mixed Hodge structure. It is effective: the Hodge numbers on every fiber satisfy $h_{p,q}=0$ if $p$ or $q$ are $<0$.

## Face maps {#sect:  facemapsgeneral}

With the above notations, let $D_I =\cap_{i\in I} D_i$ denote an intersection of ireducible components of $D$ of codimension $k$, and let $D^I = \bigcup_{j \notin I} D_j$ denote the union of all remaining irreducible components. The pair $D_I \supset D_I \cap D^I$ satisfies the conditions of the previous paragraph.

There are natural morphisms, that we shall call *face maps*, in the category $\mathcal{H} (S)$ $$\label{eqn: facemapsgeneral}
 H^{n-k}( D_I, D_I \cap D^I)_{/S} \longrightarrow H^{n}( X, D)_{/S} \ .$$ For the de Rham (respectively Betti) realisation, this is given by the inclusion of complexes $\Omega^{\bullet}_{D^I_{\bullet}/S} \rightarrow \Omega^{\bullet}_{D_{\bullet}/S}$ (respectively $\mathbb Q_{D^I_{\bullet}/S}\rightarrow \mathbb Q_{D_{\bullet}/S}$).

On the other hand, let $0\leq k \leq n-1$ and let $D^{(k)} = \bigcup_{|I|=n-k}  D_I$ denote the $k$-dimensional skeleton of $D$. Then $H^k(D^{(k)})_{/S}$ defines an object of $\mathcal{H} (S)$ given by truncating the complexes $\Omega^{\bullet}_{D_{\bullet}/S}$ and $\mathbb Q_{D_{\bullet}/S}$ on the left so that the non-zero components are $|J| \geq n-k$. The inclusion of these complexes similarly defines $$\label{skelmap}  H^k(D^{(k)})_{/S} \longrightarrow H^n(X, D)_{/S}\ .$$ The case $k=n-1$ is the boundary map in the relative cohomology sequence $$\cdots\longrightarrow H^{n-1}(X)_{/S} \longrightarrow H^{n-1}(D^{(n-1)})_{/S} \longrightarrow H^n(X,D)_{/S}\longrightarrow H^n(X)_{/S}\longrightarrow\cdots$$

The face maps $(\ref{eqn: facemapsgeneral})$ factor through $(\ref{skelmap})$.

## Weight filtration

By strictness, apply the weight functor to the previous long exact cohomology sequence to obtain an exact sequence $$\cdots  \rightarrow W_k H^{n-1}(X)_{/S}  \rightarrow W_k H^{n-1}(D^{(n-1)})_{/S} \rightarrow W_k H^n(X,D)_{/S} \rightarrow W_k H^n(X)_{/S} \rightarrow \cdots$$ Since $H^n(X)$ has smooth fibers, it has weights concentrated in degrees between $n$ and $2n$ (by [@De2] 8.2.4). It follows that $$W_k H^{n-1}(D^{(n-1)})_{/S} \longrightarrow W_k H^n(X,D)_{/S}$$ is surjective if $k={n-1}$, and an isomorphism if $k< n-1$. The following proposition generalises the previous fact, and is presumably well-known. We include a quick proof for completeness. It requires the following lemma.

**Lemma 58**. *Let $\phi: C \rightarrow C'$ be a morphism of cochain complexes such that $\phi_i: C_i \rightarrow C'_{i}$ is surjective, and $\phi_j : C_j \rightarrow C'_j$ are isomorphisms for all $j>i$. Then the induced maps on cohomology have the same properties: $H^i(C) \rightarrow H^i(C')$ is surjective, and $H^j(C) \overset{\sim}{\rightarrow} H^j(C')$ is an isomorphism for all $j>i$.*

*Proof.* Exercise. ◻

**Proposition 59**. *Let $m<n$. The map $$W_k H^m(D^{(m)})_{/S} \longrightarrow W_k H^n(X, D)_{/S}$$ is surjective if $m=k$ and is an isomorphism if $k < m$.*

*Proof.* Since the comparison isomorphism respects the weight filtration, it suffices to verify the statement in the Betti realisation. For this it is enough to check the statement on every fiber: for every $t \in S(\mathbb C)$, $$W_k H_B^{m} ( D^{(m)})_t \longrightarrow W_k H_B^n(X, D)_t$$ is an isomorphism for $k<m$ and surjective for $k=m$. To alleviate the notation, write $B_I= (D_I)_t(\mathbb C)$ and $Y = X_t(\mathbb C)$. Consider $$\label{eqn: inproofHmBtoHYB}
 H^{m} (B^{(m)})  \longrightarrow H^n(Y, B)  \ .$$ There are relative cohomology spectral sequences $$\label{relcomss}
  E_1^{p,q} (Y)= \bigoplus_{|J|=p} H^q(B_J)   \quad \Longrightarrow \quad  H^{p+q}(Y,B)$$ and $$E_1^{p,q}(B^{(m)}) = \bigoplus_{|J|=p+n-m } H^q(B_J)   \quad \Longrightarrow \quad  H^{p+q}(B^{(m)})$$ The morphism $(\ref{eqn: inproofHmBtoHYB})$ induces a map of spectral sequences $$E_1^{p,q}(B^{(m)}) \longrightarrow E_1^{p+n-m,q} (Y)$$ which is the identity on each summand $H^q(B_J)$.
