Let $j \leq k$ and apply the functor $\mathrm{gr}^W_j$. It is exact, giving a morphism of spectral sequences $$\label{Cpnmq}
  \mathrm{gr}^W_{j} E_r^{p,q}(B^{(m)}) \longrightarrow\mathrm{gr}^W_j E_r^{p+n-m,q} (Y) \ .$$ Since $B_J$ is smooth, $H^q(B_J)$ has weights in the interval $[q,2q]$ by [@De2] 8.2.4, and therefore both sides of $(\ref{Cpnmq})$ vanish for all $q >  j$. The entries of $(\ref{Cpnmq})$ for $r=1$ are identical in the range $p\geq 0$. By running the spectral sequence, and applying the previous lemma, one verifies by induction on $r$ that $(\ref{Cpnmq})$ is an isomorphism for $p\geq r-1$ or $p+ q \geq m+1$ and surjective for other values of $p\geq 0$. ◻

The spectral sequence $(\ref{relcomss})$ implies the

**Corollary 60**. *Let $0 \leq k \leq n$. Then $\mathrm{gr}^W_k H^n(X,D)_{/S}$ is isomorphic to a subquotient of $\bigoplus_{|I| \geq  n-k}\mathrm{gr}^W_k \ H^{n-|I|} (D_{I})_{/S}$.*

Putting $k=0$, $m=1$ in the previous proposition gives the following corollary.

**Corollary 61**. *We have $$\label{eqn: Weight0HXD}
 W_0 H^n(X,D)_{/S} \cong  \mathbb Q(0)^{\oplus m}_{/S}$$ where $m = \dim_{\mathbb Q}  \widetilde{H}^{n-1}(D_t(\mathbb C))$ for any $t\in S(\mathbb C)$. In particular, the motivic periods of $H^n(X,D)_{/S}$ of weight zero are constant and rational.*

*Proof.* Note that if $|I|=n$ then $D_{I} \cong \mathrm{Spec} \,\, S$ and $H^0(D_{I})_{/S} = \mathbb Q(0)_{/S}$, so $(\ref{eqn: Weight0HXD})$ holds for some $m$. To determine $m$, pass to the Betti realisation at the fiber $s$. With the notations of the previous proposition, we have $$\mathrm{gr}^W_0 H^n(Y,B) \cong  E_2^{n,0}(Y) =   \mathrm{coker}\,\, \big( \bigoplus_{|I|=n-1} H^0(B_{I})\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{|I|=n} H^0(B_{I})\big)$$ Since the $B_I$ are connected, the dimension of this cokernel is the dimension of the reduced cohomology $\dim \widetilde{H}^{n-1}(B)$. ◻

We considered earlier the case $S= \mathrm{Spec} \,\mathbb Q$, and $D$ normal crossing, rather than simple normal crossing. Then a similar argument proves that the weight zero part of $H^n(X,D)_{/S}$ is the (realisation of a) constant Artin motive. The action of $\mathrm{Gal}(\overline{\mathbb Q}/\mathbb Q)$ upon its Betti realisation is induced by the Galois action on the points $\bigcup_{|I|=n} D_I(\mathbb C)$.

Under some further assumptions, the face maps provide information about the mixed Hodge structure on $H^n(X,D)_{/S}$ in low weights.

**Proposition 62**. *Suppose that $H^k((D_{I})_s)=0$ for all $k> n- |I|$ (for example, if the strata $D_I$ have affine fibres of dimension $n-|I|$).*

*Let $m<n$. Then for any $k\leq m$, the sum of face maps $$\bigoplus_{|I|=n-m}  W_k H^{m} (D_I,  D_I \cap D^I)_{/S} \longrightarrow W_k H^n(X, D)_{/S}\ .$$ is surjective.*

*Proof.* A similar spectral sequence argument as in the previous proposition. The assumption implies that $E_1^{p,q}$ vanishes above the diagonal, i.e., for all $p+q >n$. Now consider the map of spectral sequences induced by the sum of face maps. Take their fibers as in the previous proposition and apply $\mathrm{gr}^W_j$ for $j\leq k$. On $E^{p,q}_1$ terms we obtain the natural map $$\label{natssE1grw}     \mathrm{gr}^W_j     \bigoplus_{|I|=n-m}   \bigoplus_{J \supset I , |J|=p} H^q(B_J) \longrightarrow\mathrm{gr}^W_j      \bigoplus_{ |J|=p} H^q(B_J)   \ .$$ This is an isomorphism above the diagonal ($p+q>n$) since both sides are identically zero in that region by assumption. Furthermore, both sides vanish of $(\ref{natssE1grw})$ in the region $q> j$ (since the $B_J$ are smooth and $H^q(B_J)$ has weights in the interval $[q,2q]$) and therefore in particular in the region $q> m$. It follows that $(\ref{natssE1grw})$ is also surjective along the diagonal $p+q=n$. By induction on $r$ and lemma 58, the induced map is surjective on the diagonal for all $r$. ◻

## The periods

In the situation of §10.2, let $s\in S(\mathbb C)$ and let $\sigma_s\subset X_s(\mathbb C)$ be a topological $n$-chain whose boundary is contained in $D_s(\mathbb C)$. It defines a relative homology class $[\sigma_s] \in H^n_B (X_s(\mathbb C), D_s(\mathbb C))^{\vee} = (H^n_B (X,D)_{/S})^{\vee}_s.$ By local triviality, there exists a small neighbourhood $N\subset S(\mathbb C)$ of $s$, and isomorphism $$(X(\mathbb C), D(\mathbb C)) \cap \pi^{-1}(N) \cong N \times (X_s(\mathbb C), D_s(\mathbb C))\ .$$ Via this isomorphism, the chain $\sigma_s$ uniquely extends to a family of topological $n$-chains $\sigma_t \subset X_t(\mathbb C)$ whose boundaries are contained in $D_t(\mathbb C)$ for all $t\in N$.

For simplicity, let us consider the particular case when we are given a global form $\omega \in \Omega^n_{\pi^{-1}(U)/U}$ for some Zariski open $U \subset S$ and suppose that the fibres of $X$ are of dimension $n$. Since the restrictions of $\omega$ to components of $D$ vanish for reasons of dimension, it defines a relative class $[\omega ] \in  \Gamma(U, H^n_{dR} (X, D))$, and $$\xi = [ H^n(X,D)_{|S}, [\sigma_s] , [\omega] ]^{\mathfrak{m} } \quad \in \quad \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} , \{s\},  Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)}$$ for any $Y\subset U(\mathbb C)$. For any $t\in U(\mathbb C)$, let $\omega_t$ denote the restriction of the form $\omega$ to a fiber $t$. The period is then $$\mathrm{per}(\xi)(t) =  \int_{\sigma_t} \omega_t$$ for all $t$ in the open set $U(\mathbb C) \cap N$, and is extended by analytic continuation to a meromorphic function on the universal covering of $S(\mathbb C)$ based at $s$.

## Example: iterated integrals on the projective line minus 3 points

Let $S= \mathbb P^1 \backslash \{0,1,\infty\}$ throughout this section.

### Motivic logarithm

Let $Z= S \times \mathbb{G} _m$ and $\pi: Z \rightarrow S$ the projection onto the first factor. If $x$ is the coordinate on $S$, and $y$ the coordinate on $\mathbb{G} _m$, let $D= \{y=1 \} \cup \{y=x\}$. Let $X\subset S(\mathbb C)$ denote the real interval $(0,1)$, and for all $x\in  X$, let $\sigma_x \subset \mathbb{G} _m(\mathbb C)$ denote the straight path from $1$ to $x$ in the fiber over $x$. Its image is $\{x\}\times [1,x]$. Define the motivic logarithm by $$\log^{\mathfrak{m} }(x) = [ H^1(Z,D)_{|S}, [\sigma_x], [ \textstyle{dy \over y}]]^{\mathfrak{m} } \qquad \in \qquad \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} (S),  X,   Y}$$ where $Y= S(\mathbb C)$. Note that $\mathcal{O}_{S, Y}= \mathcal{O}_S = \mathbb Q[x, x^{-1}, (1-x)^{-1}]$ (this was example $(3)$ in §7.2.1). Its period is the logarithm as expected: $$\mathrm{per}(\log^{\mathfrak{m} }(x)) = \log (x) = \int_{\sigma_x} {dy \over y} \qquad \hbox{ for } x \in  X\ .$$ The long exact cohomology sequence reduces to $$0 \longrightarrow\mathbb Q(0)_{|S} \longrightarrow H^1(Z,D)_{|S} \longrightarrow\mathbb Q(-1)_{|S}\longrightarrow 0 \ ,$$ and with respect to the de Rham basis $[ {dy\over y}]$, $[{dy \over x-1}]$ and Betti basis $[\sigma_x], [\gamma]$, where $\gamma$ is a small loop around $0$ in the fiber, the period matrix is given by the identical formula to $(\ref{logmotivicperiodmatrix})$, where $\mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{m} }$ is now viewed a constant family of periods over $S$ and $\alpha=x$. The coaction satisfies $\Delta \log^{\mathfrak{m} }(x) = \log^{\mathfrak{m} }(x) \otimes \mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{dr}} + 1 \otimes \log^{\mathfrak{dr}}(x)$, and the Galois group is $\mathbb{G} _a \rtimes \mathbb{G} _m$, and is in fact defined over $\mathbb Q$ in this case. Recall that $\log^{\mathfrak{dr}}(x)$ does not have a period, but has a single-valued period $2 \log |x|$.

### Motivic fundamental groupoid

Now consider the ind-object of $\mathcal{H} (S)$ $$\mathcal{O}(\pi_1^{\mathcal{H} }(S,  \overset{\rightarrow}{1}\!_0, \bullet  ) ): = ( \mathcal{O}(\pi_1^{un}(S, \overset{\rightarrow}{1}\!_0, \bullet  )), \mathcal{O}(\pi_1^{dR}(S ))\otimes_{
 \mathbb Q} \mathcal{O}_S, \mathrm{comp})$$ where $\overset{\rightarrow}{1}\!_0$ is the tangential base-point at $0$ with unit length. The first entry (Betti local system) is the affine ring of the unipotent completion of the torsor of paths beginning at $\overset{\rightarrow}{1}\!_0$ on $S(\mathbb C)$ and defines a local system on $S(\mathbb C)$: its fiber at a point $x\in S(\mathbb C)$ is $\mathcal{O}(\pi_1^{un}(S, \overset{\rightarrow}{1}\!_0, x))$ with the action of $\pi^{\mathrm{top}}(S(\mathbb C), x)$. The second entry does not in fact depend on basepoints and is the affine ring of the (unipotent) de Rham fundamental group. It is a shuffle algebra on two generators $$\mathcal{O}(\pi_1^{dR}(S)) \cong  T^c (\mathbb Qe_0 \oplus \mathbb Qe_1 )$$ where $e_0,e_1$ correspond to the one-forms ${dx \over x}$ and ${dx \over 1-x}$. It defines a trivial vector bundle on $S$, and is equipped with the Kniznhik-Zamolodchikov connection $$\nabla e_{i_1} \ldots e_{i_n}  = \begin{cases}
e_{i_1}\ldots e_{i_{n-1}} \otimes {dx \over x} \qquad  \,\,\, \hbox{ if } i_n=0 \\
   e_{i_1}\ldots e_{i_{n-1}} \otimes {dx \over 1-x} \qquad \hbox{ if } i_n=1 \ .
   \end{cases}$$ It is clearly unipotent with respect to the weight filtration. The weight filtration on $\mathcal{O}(\pi_1^{dR}(S))$ is in fact a grading in this case (it is split by the Hodge filtration since it is mixed Tate), and the grading assigns degree two to $e_0,e_1$.

We take $X= (0,1)$ and $Y=S(\mathbb C)$ as before. Define the motivic multiple polylogarithm to be the family of motivic periods: $$\label{defnmultiplepolylog}
 \mathrm{Li}^{\mathfrak{m} }_w(x) =   [ \mathcal{O}(\pi_1^{\mathcal{H} }(S,  \overset{\rightarrow}{1}\!_0, \bullet )) , \sigma_x, w]^{\mathfrak{m} } \quad \in \quad \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,  X,  Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)}$$ where $w$ is a word in $e_0,e_1$ and $\sigma_x$ is the straight line path from $\overset{\rightarrow}{1}\!_0$ to $x$. The path $\sigma_x$ is viewed as an element of $\mathcal{O}(\pi_1^{un}(S, \overset{\rightarrow}{1}\!_0, x ))^{\vee}$ via the natural map $$\label{naturalpi1mapend}
 \pi^{\mathrm{top}}_1(S(\mathbb C), \overset{\rightarrow}{1}\!_0, x) \longrightarrow\pi_1^{un}(S, \overset{\rightarrow}{1}\!_0, x)(\mathbb Q)\ .$$ The period of $\mathrm{Li}^{\mathfrak{m} }_w(x)$ is $\mathrm{Li}_w(x)$, which is the iterated integral $\int_{\sigma_x} w$, and we have $\mathrm{Li}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{e_0}(x) = \log^{\mathfrak{m} }(x)$. More generally we write $\mathrm{Li}^{\mathfrak{m} }_n(x)$ for $\mathrm{Li}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{e_1 e_0^{n-1}}(x)$, and call it the motivic classical polylogarithm. The connection satisfies $$\nabla \mathrm{Li}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{we_s } (x) =(-1)^s \mathrm{Li}^{\mathfrak{m} }_w(x) \otimes {dx \over x - s} \qquad \hbox{ where } s \in \{0,1\}\ , \    w \in \{e_0,e_1\}^{\times}$$ and $\mathrm{Li}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\emptyset}(x)$ is the constant motivic period $1$. The de Rham motivic multiple polylogarithms are defined by $$\mathrm{Li}^{\mathfrak{dr}}_w(x) =   [ \mathcal{O}(\pi_1^{\mathcal{H} }(S,  \overset{\rightarrow}{1}\!_0, \bullet )) , \varepsilon, w]^{\mathfrak{m} } \quad \in \quad \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{dr},  Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)}$$ where $\varepsilon: \mathcal{O}(\pi_1^{dR}(S)) \rightarrow \mathbb Q$ is the augmentation map (it sends every non-trivial word $w$ to zero). The de Rham versions are the images of $\mathrm{Li}^{\mathfrak{m} }_w(x)$ under the projection map §4.3. Our definition of the symbol satisfies, as expected, $$\mathrm{smb}(\mathrm{Li}^{\mathfrak{dr}}_{w}(x))  = w \qquad \in \qquad T^c (\Omega^1(S)) \cong H^0(\mathbb{B}(\Omega^1(S)))\ .$$ The single-valued versions of $\mathrm{Li}^{\mathfrak{dr}}_w(x)$ are obtained in an identical way to [@SVMP], by simply writing superscript $\mathfrak{m}$ everywhere (with a possible sign difference of $(-1)^{|w|}$).

Recall that for every word $w$ we defined the (image in the ring of $\mathcal{H}$-periods) of the shuffle-regularised motivic multiple zeta values: $$\zeta^{ \mathfrak{m}}(w) =  [ \mathcal{O}(\pi_1^{\mathcal{H} }(S,  \overset{\rightarrow}{1}\!_0, -\overset{\rightarrow}{1}\!_1)) , \varepsilon, w]^{\mathfrak{m} } \quad \in \quad \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }\ ,$$ where $-\overset{\rightarrow}{1}\!_1$ is the tangent vector $-1$ at the point $1$. Denote their pull-backs to $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,X, Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)}$ via the structural map $\pi: S \rightarrow \mathrm{Spec}\, \mathbb Q$ by the same symbol. Let $$\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} , +}_{\mathcal{M}_{0,4}}  \qquad \subset \qquad \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} , X,  Y}_{\mathcal{H} (S)}$$ denote the $\mathcal{O}_S$-module generated by the $\zeta^{ \mathfrak{m}}(w), \mathrm{Li}^{\mathfrak{m} }_w(x)$ and $\mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{m} }$. Then $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,+}_{\mathcal{M}_{0,4}}$ is a ring of motivic periods which is stable under the monodromy action of the fundamental group of $S(\mathbb C)$ at the base-point $\overset{\rightarrow}{1}\!_0$. More generally, one can consider in a similar manner the motivic periods given by iterated integrals on the moduli spaces of curves $\mathcal{M}_{0,n}$ whose periods are multiple polylogarithms in several variables. For example, the space of functions $H$ used in the analytic bootstrap for the 6-point function in the planar limit of $N=4$ SYM theory described in the introduction of [@Cosmic] is contained in the space $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,+}_{\mathcal{M}_{0,6}}$.

*Remark 63*. These examples can be expressed geometrically in the spirit of §10.2 using Beilinson's construction for the unipotent fundamental group, and indeed defines a variation of mixed Tate motives in the sense of [@DeGo], 4.12.

If $t$ is a rational point on $S$ (or even a tangential base point), we can define the evaluation $\mathrm{ev}_t$ at the point $t$. In this situation, the de Rham coaction $(\ref{H(S)coaction})$ commutes with evaluation at $t$: $$\Delta  \mathrm{ev}_t( \mathrm{Li}^{\mathfrak{m} }) =  ( \mathrm{ev}_t \otimes \mathrm{ev}_t)  \Delta \mathrm{Li}^{\mathfrak{m} }(x)\ .$$ The follows from the triviality of the de Rham vector bundle $\mathcal{O}(\pi_1^{dR}(S))$ and equation $(\ref{eqn: coaction})$. The unipotent coaction can be computed by transposing a formula due to Goncharov [@Go] to this setting. By way of example, the de Rham coaction on the motivic dilogarithm satisfies $$\Delta \mathrm{Li}_2^{\mathfrak{m} }(x) =\mathrm{Li}^{\mathfrak{m} }_2(x)  \otimes  (\mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{dr}})^2  +  \mathrm{Li}_{1}^{\mathfrak{m} }(x) \otimes \mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{dr}} \log^{\mathfrak{dr}}(x)   + 1 \otimes \mathrm{Li}^{\mathfrak{dr}}_2(x)$$ and was discussed in further detail in example $\ref{exdilog}.$ The unipotent coaction is obtained by replacing $\mathfrak{dr}$ in the right-hand terms by $\mathfrak{u}$, and noting that $\mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{u}}=1$.

In conclusion, the notion of families of motivic periods provides a framework for motivic multiple polylogarithms which includes constants and is sufficient for many applications to high-energy physics. It satisfies the properties conjectured in [@Duhr].

[^1]: in fact, two groups, one for each fiber functor Betti or de Rham.

[^2]: the relations should come through the back door as a consequence of bounds on algebraic $K$-theory and the Tannakian formalism. The theory of multiple zeta values provides many examples of relations between periods which can be proved by analytic methods but for which a proof using algebraic correspondences are not presently known.

[^3]: In [@SVMP; @BrMTZ] I wrote $\mathfrak{m} = ( \omega_{dR}, \omega_{B})$, and put the coaction on the left, for purely psychological reasons. The corresponding rings of periods are identical.

[^4]: the ring of mixed Tate periods corresponds to the $\mathcal{H}$-periods of mixed Tate objects: those whose associated weight-graded is a direct sum of Tate objects $\mathbb Q(n)$

[^5]: One might be tempted, by analogy with algebraic numbers, to define notions of Betti/de Rham/biconjugates of $\xi$ by considering the orbits of $\xi$ under the group of $R$-points of the corresponding groups $G^{B / dR}_{\mathcal{H} }(R)$, for $R$ a commutative $\mathbb Q$-algebra. We shall not.

[^6]: these exist over any number field, but for the time being we are working only over $\mathbb Q$

[^7]: In [@SVMP] the Lefschetz motivic period was viewed as an object in $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{MT}(\mathbb Z)}$, where $\mathcal{MT}(\mathbb Z)$ is the category of mixed Tate motives unramified over $\mathbb Z$. There is an injection $\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{MT}(\mathbb Z)}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} ,+} \subset  \mathcal{P}^{\mathfrak{m} }_{\mathcal{H} }$, and the object $\mathbb{L} ^{\mathfrak{m} }$ defined here is its image. A similar remark applies for the later examples.

[^8]: The use of the decomposition map considerably simplifies many of the arguments of [@BrMTZ]

[^9]: This point of view quickly leads to new constructions. For example, one can consider curious hybrid quantities defined by the convolution of $\mathrm{per}$ with $\mathrm{per}_p$: $$\nonumber
     \zeta_{\mathbb R*p }(n_1,\ldots, n_r ) : =  m( \mathrm{per}\otimes \mathrm{per}_p \pi^{\mathfrak{dr},\mathfrak{m} +}) \Delta \zeta^{ \mathfrak{m}}(n_1,\ldots, n_r)  \in    \mathbb R\otimes_{\mathbb Q} \mathbb Q_p \ ,$$ where $m$ is multiplication, and $\Delta$ the coaction $(\ref{eqn: coaction})$.

[^10]: Note that prescription $(1)$ applied to $f_{2n-1}$ gives the single-valued motivic zeta value which is exactly double that, namely $2\zeta^{ \mathfrak{m}}(2n-1)$.

[^11]: In the usual formulation, due to Chen [@Chen], one considers the tensor algebra $T^c(\Omega^{\geq 1}_{S, Y})$ and quotients out by a certain family of relations. The reader may like to check that Chen's relations are boundaries in the complex we have defined here and are therefore incorporated automatically.

[^12]: Furthermore, $H^1(\mathbb P^n;\mathcal{O})=0$, so the canonical extension $\overline{\mathcal{V}}$ of a unipotent vector bundle $\mathcal{V}$ is trivial, and we can take as de Rham fiber functor the global sections functor $\mathcal{V}\mapsto \Gamma(\mathbb P^n, \overline{\mathcal{V}})\in \mathrm{Vec}_{\mathbb Q}$.

[^13]: In order to capture better the idea of a family of periods ramified over certain primes, then the current set-up in which we only consider Betti and de Rham information is inadequate. One could proceed along the lines of [@DeP1], §1.18.
